### Stage: Natural science

The natural order of study would place substances and their kinds before accidents and theirs.[^V04-1] We have postponed the discussion of substances for two reasons. First, most things we can prove about them depend on principles established in the study of accidents. Second, knowledge of substances fits more naturally in divine science, which studies the realities of things themselves, than in philosophy, which studies general concepts and their first divisions.[^V04-2] For these reasons, we have treated accidents before substances.

This stage has an introduction and several parts. The introduction lists the categories. Philosophers count ten: substance and nine kinds of accident—quantity, quality, relation, place, position, time, possession, acting, and being acted on.

There are four questions to consider. The first is how to show that each of the ten categories is a broader kind for the kinds under it. This requires five things. First, the kinds grouped under each category must share a meaning; that is the least a broader kind must do. Second, this shared meaning must be positive, since absences cannot be broader kinds of existing things, except when words such as “substance” or “quality” point to general meanings.

Third, the shared positive feature must be said of the kinds below it in one and the same sense, not by degrees. In our view, degrees belong only to existence, not to essences, and existence is not part of an essence. Fourth, this feature must be part of the kinds beneath it, not an accident added from outside. Fifth, the shared part must make up the whole essence of the broader kind.

The second question is whether any two of these ten categories fall under one broader broader kind. Ancient philosophers did not give a proof that they do not. The Sheikh reports that some people thought acting and being acted on were kinds of quality. That is wrong. If heating were the same as heat, then whatever heats something would itself be hot. Even motion would have to be hot if it heats something. But this is not so. To become warm or black is to seek warmth or blackness; what seeks a thing cannot be that very thing.

Some philosophers reduced the categories to four: substance, quantity, quality, and relation, with relation as the broader kind of the other seven. The author of *Insights* agreed with them.[^V04-3] The author of *The Discussions* counted five: those four plus motion, and offered an argument for limiting the categories to these five. We will first report that argument, then show what is wrong with it.

After dividing the familiar categories into five, we later found a passage in *Insights* that reduces them to the four just mentioned. That four-part list cannot be right. Motion is not a kind of substance, because it is an accident; it is not quantity itself, even though it has measure; and it is not quality, which is a stable state that in itself entails neither division nor relation to something else, even though a quality may be related to the thing that has it.

#### A problem and its solution

At first sight, this objection applies not only to the proposed four or five categories, but also to the usual count of nine kinds of accident, since motion is treated as another category. This is a problem for both views, and it needs an answer.

The answer is that motion is a way in which something exists gradually. It has no essence beyond this gradual existence. Existence lies outside the essences of substances and accidents. But a nature that is a broader kind cannot lie outside the essence of the kinds beneath it. So motion is not a broader kind, much less a category. When we speak of motion as one in kind, species, or individual, we mean the thing to which the motion belongs.

The author of *The Discussions* then says that someone seeking to reduce the categories to five should divide essence, apart from existence, this way: it is either substance or not substance. What is not substance is a state or feature. Such a feature is either stable or not. If it is not stable, it is motion. If stable, it either cannot be understood without reference to something else—in which case it is relation—or it can be understood on its own. In the latter case, it either entails equality, inequality, and division, in which case it is quantity, or it does not, in which case it is quality.

Quality comes last in this division and differs from each of the other four. As a feature, it differs from substance; as stable, from motion; as something that can be understood without reference to another thing, from relation; and as something that does not require division, from quantity. Its definition includes all the features that distinguish it from the other four. This, he says, is the proof that there are five categories.

He then tries to show that time, place, and the other relational categories can be understood only through relation, and makes the same case for each of them. We should remember, however, that relation is not an essence that can be understood by itself, apart from the things related. Nor can it be separated in thought from those things. Whatever cannot be separated in reality or in thought from something else cannot be considered by itself as a nature that is then predicated of its own kinds. A relation cannot be a broader kind or species for the kinds beneath it unless we include the broader kind or species of what it relates to. So a relation can be called a broader kind, species, or individual only when the thing related, considered as related, is itself one of these.[^V04-4]

With that in mind, consider place. The category of “where” may be a broader kind for different relations a thing has to places. Likewise, “when,” the relation of a thing to time, may be a broader kind for relations to different kinds of time. The seven relational categories could share a single, univocal meaning that is part of their nature only if the things they relate to also shared a broader kind—for example, if time, place, the parts of posture, the quality involved in acting or being acted on, substance as something related, and the two sides of possession, such as a person and a shirt, all had a common broader kind.

Some have made “the relative” the broader kind of the other six relational categories. The Sheikh rejects this. Being related may entail being in a relation, but being in a relation is an accident of the first and is not part of the other six categories. For example, being in a house is the relation called “where”; it is not the category of relation. If we consider the matter further, the thing located in a place can also be understood in relation to what contains it: as contained, it is related to the container. That added relation does not belong to the category “where” by itself; it arises because one thing contains another. Just as being white is one thing and being something that has whiteness is another, so being in a place is one thing and being understood in relation to another is another. The first is what the second is about. The relation concerns both the thing containing and the thing contained. That is why philosophers say that “where” applies to one side, while relation applies to both.

The third question is whether there are any categories beyond the ten. The Sheikh offered a weak argument against this and admitted that it was weak, so we have left it aside. Someone might ask whether unity, a point, the present moment, existence, thinghood, motion, common notions, simple defining features, derived terms such as “white” and “hot,” or absences such as blindness and ignorance belong to other categories.

Pure absences must be outside the categories, since we are discussing things that exist in themselves. Their positive counterparts, such as sight and knowledge, may be included, while absence is included only indirectly. The same goes for derived terms and compounds: they already imply unity and existence, which are assumed when we divide possible things into the ten categories. Existence itself is outside them because we are discussing essences, and existence is not part of an essence. In our view, unity is existence itself. A point is negative; motion is a mode of existence, as we have said. Simple defining features are really the particular existences of specific essences. Thinghood and possibility are broad concepts that become real only through particular things.

Imam al-Razi says someone might argue that unity and a point belong to quality: each is an accident whose meaning does not depend on conceiving anything outside its subject, and neither entails division or relation among the parts of its subject. The Sheikh did not address this argument. I say that we already know how to answer it. The Sheikh reports that some people place unity and a point under quantity, then rejects this because quantity is what accepts equality and inequality by its own nature, and neither unity nor a point does.[^V04-5]

Others reject this classification by saying that unity is the starting point of discrete quantity and the point is the starting point of continuous quantity; a beginning cannot belong to what begins from it, or it would be the beginning of itself. The Sheikh answers that unity is the beginning only of one kind of quantity, the discrete, and the point, if it is a beginning at all, is the beginning only of continuous quantity. Being a beginning for some kinds does not make either one the beginning of itself.

Others place unity and the point under several categories in different respects: a point is relative insofar as it is an endpoint, and a quality insofar as it is a kind of state. This is also wrong. One essence cannot be made up of one broader kind and something that is not that broader kind.

The Sheikh allows that these things fall outside the ten categories. This does not contradict the claim that there are ten most general kinds. The claim is only that anything whose essence is formed from a broader kind and a defining feature belongs under one of the ten. Simple things—the most general kinds themselves, the final defining features, simple kinds, and individual existences—may fall outside without disproving the count. It is like saying there are ten groups of citizens in a city: finding some outsiders who are not citizens does not disprove the ten groups.

The fourth question concerns how the categories are divided into kinds: by defining features or by incidental features? A division by incidental features may match the division by defining features, as when we divide animals into those that can understand and those that cannot, matching the division into rational and nonrational. Or it may not match it, as when we divide animals into male and female.

### Part One: The category of quantity: its existence, kinds, and status as an accident

#### I. Three features of quantity

There are three features by which we can know what quantity in general is.

The first is measurement, with its related ideas of equality and inequality. These are relations, but they belong to things because the things have quantity, not because they are bodies of a certain general or specific kind.

The second is divisibility, which has two meanings. The first is that we can imagine something divided into two equal parts, each like the whole, and then imagine each part divided into two more parts, without end. This belongs to magnitude in itself. It does not make the body change or move from place to place.

The second meaning is the actual separation of a body into two distinct bodies of the same kind. This requires motion and being acted on. It belongs to matter, not to magnitude, as we will explain when we discuss prime matter. Matter is ready to receive this division because of magnitude, but something that prepares matter to receive a feature is not itself thereby ready to receive that same feature. Otherwise, every preparatory condition would be ready for the thing it prepares.

Nor does the magnitude have to remain when the division actually occurs. Motion prepares a body to come to rest in a place, but the motion does not remain once the body is at rest.

The third feature is that quantity can be counted by one. We will show that a continuous body can be divided into endlessly many parts, but these divisions cannot all become actual at once. Each body can be said to have a finite number of actual divisions, and it can also be said to have endlessly many potential divisions. Both of these possibilities belong to matter through magnitude.

Once this is clear, we can say that every body can be halved without end. Halving a magnitude doubles the number of parts. In general, dividing a magnitude increases the number of things counted. Number has no upper limit, though it decreases only as far as one; magnitude has no lower limit, though it ends at a finite upper size.

Since magnitude is divisible in itself, it must also be countable in itself. Whether quantity is actually separate, as number is, or potentially separate and actually continuous, as magnitude is, it can contain a unit by which it is counted. So quantity has three features that no other category shares.

Some define quantity as “an accident that accepts equality and inequality.” This is weak, because equality can be defined only as sameness in quantity, making the definition circular. Others add divisibility, but that is a mistake: divisibility belongs to continuous quantity, not discrete quantity, except by using the word in a different sense.

The best definition is the one given by the two Sheikhs: quantity is “that which can in itself contain a unit by which it is counted.” This works for both continuous and discrete quantity. It is not circular, because “one” is as basic as existence and needs no definition, and neither does number.

#### II. How magnitude differs from corporeality

There are six ways to show that magnitude differs from corporeality. The first four rely on rejecting indivisible physical parts.

First, one and the same body can take on different magnitudes while remaining the same body. A candle, for example, can be shaped in different ways. It takes on different lengths, widths, and depths, while remaining a body. These magnitudes must therefore be something added to corporeality.

Someone might object that if a sphere is reshaped as a cube, its magnitude has not changed because its surface area is the same. We will show that equality can be actual or only potential. Different shapes like these are equal only potentially, not in reality; what is merely potential does not yet exist.

The second proof is that bodies share corporeality but differ in their particular magnitudes. This argument does not depend on one body taking on different magnitudes over time.

The author of *The Wisdom of Illumination* objects: bodies share corporeality and also share being measurable, while differing in their specific magnitudes. If sharing corporeality while differing in particular magnitudes proves that magnitudes are added to corporeality, then sharing general measurability while differing in particular magnitudes should also prove that each specific magnitude is an added accident on top of general magnitude. That would make both general quantity and each particular quantity separate accidents, leading to an impossible regress. This is a strong objection. We will show how to answer it.

Third, some bodies measure others and some are measured by them. Usually, the measuring magnitude differs from the magnitude being measured. So the ability to measure and the ability to be measured cannot be corporeality itself, which cannot differ from one body to another. This argument is also open to the objection just mentioned.

Fourth, when a body heats up, its volume increases without any matter being added or any vacuum entering it, both of which are impossible. When it cools, its volume decreases without any matter leaving it or any vacuum disappearing. The body remains the same in both states, so it is distinct from its magnitudes. This proof also depends on rejecting indivisible physical parts, since expansion and contraction rely on that assumption.

Fifth, a surface depends on matter, as we will prove. But what depends on matter cannot be the corporeality that makes a thing a body, since corporeality is prior to the surface as its cause. Therefore a surface differs from corporeality. And since surface is an accident, line is an accident all the more clearly.

The sixth proof is that line and surface are not part of the meaning of body. A body can be thought of as extending without limit; if line and surface were what made it a body, then the thought of an unlimited body would be the thought of something that is not a body, which is impossible. Since line and surface can be separated in thought from body by their very nature, even if something else keeps them from being separated in reality, they must differ from corporeality. A line can in some cases exist apart from a body, even in both modes of existence, as with a stationary sphere. In spheres that are always moving, the poles and axes are determined by that movement. The body exists before its motion, and the motion before the circles and lines are determined.

So corporeality cannot be constituted by those lines; otherwise, it would have to exist before itself.

Someone might object that the same reasoning applies to matter and form: they are parts of a body's make-up, yet someone may know a body without knowing them. Not thinking of matter and form when thinking of a body does not prove that they are not its constituents.

The answer is that someone who understands a body as made of indivisible particles has not understood what a body truly is. Someone who understands it as a continuous substance without matter has grasped its nature, though incompletely. Someone who understands it as matter without form has not grasped it in reality. Those who make magnitudes constituents of a body treat them as its form, not its matter. Also, we can know form without knowing matter, so the two must differ. In the same way, we can understand corporeality while being unsure whether a surface exists; this shows that the surface is distinct from the form.

#### 1. An Illuminationist argument

Here is an argument for the view that geometrical magnitudes are accidents added to the natural body. Anything that belongs with something else to the reality of a broader kind must have the same principles, constituents, and completing features as that other thing. The kinds of one must also fall under the kinds of the other.

So, if corporeality and magnitude were the same, what completes one would complete the other. Corporeality, taken as a broader kind, needs defining features that complete it. With the first defining features added, it forms further kinds. Taken on their own, those kinds are still incomplete and need further defining features, until we arrive at complete kinds that need no more.

Corporeality as matter also needs completing forms: first the elemental form, then the growing form, then the sensitive form, and then the rational form. These principles and completing features belong to the kinds of body because they are beings, substances, affected substances, substances shaped by a mixture, growing things, or animals.

But general magnitude, if it needs a defining feature, must need one because it is quantity. It is first completed as a solid, a surface, or a line, depending on whether division occurs in three, two, or one direction. These are the first features of something divisible. Once each is further specified, it must be through meanings and features that belong to quantity as quantity—whether continuous or discrete—such as length and shortness, measuring and being measured, sharing and differing, being commensurable or not, and so on.

The consequences are impossible, so the assumption that corporeality and magnitude are the same must also be false. This also answers the objections raised against the second and third proofs.

#### III. How quantity divides into continuous and discrete kinds

This chapter explains the kinds of continuous quantity, why number is the only discrete quantity, and why speech is not discrete quantity and heaviness and lightness are not continuous quantities.

The word “continuous” has two meanings. One describes a quantity in itself; the other describes how one quantity relates to another.

In the first sense, continuity is a feature of quantity itself. It can be defined in either of two ways. A quantity is continuous if we can imagine it divided into parts that meet along shared boundaries. A shared boundary is the beginning of one part and the end of another. Or, we can define it as something that can be divided endlessly in principle. Discrete quantity is the opposite in both senses.

In the second, relative sense, continuity has two forms. First, two quantities may share the same endpoint, as the two lines forming an angle do, or the two differently colored parts of a body.

Second, the end of one quantity may meet the end of another as they move. Discrete quantity is the opposite of continuity in both of these senses as well.

With that distinction in place, quantity in general divides into truly continuous quantity and its opposite, discrete quantity. Continuous quantity either has parts that exist together, or it does not. The first is magnitude. It divides according to how many directions it extends in: one direction gives us a line; two directions at right angles give us a surface, also called a plane; and three directions at right angles give us a solid, or geometrical body. This is the most complete kind of magnitude because it can be divided in three directions. It is also called thickness because it fills the space between surfaces. When measured downward, it is called depth; when measured upward, height.

The three magnitudes can also be described in another way. A line is what a moving point traces on a surface; a surface is what a line traces as it moves sideways; and a body is what a surface traces as it moves up or down. You will see that this is only an illustration, not a true account of what they are.

The kind of continuous quantity whose parts do not exist together is time. Its nature and properties belong to our discussion of motion, because time is so closely connected to motion. So continuous quantity has four kinds: line, surface, body, and time. Some have thought that place is a fifth kind. That is mistaken. For those who define place as a surface, it is a kind of surface considered in relation to what it contains. We will discuss place later.

Discrete quantity is number. Number is quantity because, by its nature, it is counted by units, whether those units are within the things counted or not. Its parts are separate because they have no shared boundary. If you divide five into three and two, for example, there is no shared boundary between the parts. If there were a boundary belonging to one part, the other part would contain either one fewer or one more than it should.

There can be no discrete quantity other than number. Discrete quantity is made up of separate things, and those things are made up of individual units. Each unit is one. A unit can be considered simply as one, or as a particular thing, such as a person or a triangle. That particular thing is also one. But unity means one simply as one, not as a person, triangle, or anything else in particular. Such particular features have nothing to do with a thing's being one, as we have seen in our discussion of existence.

So it is clear that units themselves make up discrete quantity, and that number consists of those units, not of the things that carry them. Those things are numbered only incidentally, just as each is one only incidentally.

This also shows why it is wrong to treat speech as discrete quantity, as some have done. They say speech is made of syllables, which give it its measure and are its parts; everything with measurable parts is quantity. The mistake is in the last claim. Something may have parts that can be measured because it has another nature and happens to take on a magnitude or number. The syllable is one, and speech is many; speech has the property of quantity because of that multiplicity, not by its own nature. If we set aside this incidental feature and consider speech simply as a perceptible quality, it is not quantity, and the syllable is not a quantity or a unit by its own nature. Otherwise, things would all be quantities and units in themselves.

People have also been misled into treating heaviness and lightness as quantities for two reasons: they can be compared as equal or unequal, and they can be divided. Neither reason works.

First, equality in quantity means that one thing's boundary and whole extent match another's. If their boundaries and full extents match, they are equal; if they do not, one is greater and the other smaller. Heaviness and lightness cannot be compared in this way. Heaviness is a power that produces motion. It is either a natural power, which belongs to substance, or an inclination that directly causes motion, which belongs to quality. A thing cannot belong essentially to two categories at once; one of them would have to apply only incidentally.

Second, heaviness and lightness seem divisible when we say, for example, that one body's weight is half another's.[^V04-6] But this is because one body travels half the distance in the same time, or travels the same distance in half the time. The comparison comes from the effect heaviness has on motion, which is connected to distance and time.

#### IV. Another division of quantity: with position and without position

The word “position” has three meanings. First, something may be pointed out by the senses, either actually or in principle. To point to it is to mark the direction it occupies in the world, as *The Healing* explains. In this sense, a point has position, but unity does not.

Second, “position” has a narrower meaning: a quantity has position if it can be pointed out in a direction.

Third, position is one of the nine categories of accident. It is the body's state that comes from the way its parts are arranged in relation to one another along different directions. In the strict sense, this kind of position belongs only to material substances.

The second meaning is what divides quantity into kinds. It seems to have been borrowed from the third meaning, which is itself one of the categories. Since the position of a body, which belongs to the category of substance, comes from the way its parts relate to one another, people may have thought that position also accompanies a body considered as quantity. A body considered as quantity, as well as a surface or a line, does not by itself have directions or a place. But a geometrical body has parts that can be joined and arranged. If we imagine them as existing, we can point to each part and say where it is in relation to the others. The same is true of a surface and a line. This meaning of position therefore resembles the third meaning and has taken the same name.[^V04-7]

With that in mind, quantity either has position or does not. The line, surface, and body have position. The other kinds do not. Time has no position because its parts do not exist together. Number has no position because its parts are not connected, even though they remain fixed. Some people have thought that a moving body has no position.

The Sheikh disproves this confusion. If they mean position in the category of accident, their claim may seem true, but it is not. There is a difference between having no position and having no fixed position, just as there is a difference between having no place and having no fixed place. On closer examination, motion does not take away a body's place, though it does take away its fixed place. The same is true of motion and position.

But the position that belongs to quantity is different. It does not change when a body moves, because motion does not undo the conditions that give quantity position: the parts remain near to or far from one another in the same way, even if their relation to the directions of the world changes.

Writers often discuss five meanings of length, four of breadth, and four of depth here. We have postponed them until our discussion of natural bodies for a subtle reason. If length, breadth, and depth mean the extensions themselves, they are quantities by their own nature and belong to the kind of line. The other meanings combine quantities with relations, and their concepts concern natural things. So it is better to discuss them with natural bodies.

#### V. What is quantity only incidentally

Something may be quantitative only incidentally in four ways.

First, it may be something that exists in a quantity, such as the features we have listed.

Second, quantity may exist in something else, whether it is continuous or discrete. Discrete quantity can be found in immaterial beings as well as material things. Continuous quantity is plainly found in material things; it is also found in immaterial beings according to those who believe there is a world of magnitude beyond this material world.

Something that is continuous by its own nature may also be continuous and discrete incidentally. Time, for example, is continuous by its own nature, but it is divided into hours and days because of its relation to distance. There is no contradiction in something belonging essentially to one category and incidentally taking on another feature from that category. Motion is continuous but does not have parts that remain together; it may be described as long, short, equal, or unequal because of the time it takes. It may also be described in these ways by reference to the distance it covers.

Third, something may be called quantitative because it exists in a subject that has quantity. For example, we may call a patch of blackness long, broad, or deep because it exists in a body with length, breadth, and depth.

Fourth, a power may be called finite or infinite because of its effects on things that have quantity by their own nature. This does not mean the power itself has quantity. It means that its effects differ in intensity, number, or duration. Intensity and duration differ in two ways. Greater intensity shortens duration, and powers that differ in intensity may not differ in duration.

Now that we have defined quantity and its primary kinds, let us turn to its properties and further divisions.

#### VI. Quantity has no contrary

Discrete quantities have no contraries for three reasons.

First, a larger number includes smaller numbers and is made up of them. Contraries do not make up one another or depend on one another in this way.

Second, there is no greatest possible difference between two numbers, because numbers have no upper limit. Two and a thousand may differ greatly, but they are not contraries: contrariety must work both ways, and a thousand is not as far from two as it could possibly be.

Third, contraries must belong in turn to the same immediate subject. Numbers do not. Three, for example, consists of three units. Its form depends on joining one unit to another and then another. The immediate subject that has the number two cannot have the number three without being changed; the subject of two is not the same as the subject of three.

The three kinds of continuous magnitude—bodies, surfaces, and lines—are not contraries either. There are also three reasons for this. First, each can receive or contain another, and what is received must depend on what receives it. How, then, could the two be contraries? Second, no magnitude is as far as possible from another. Third, their immediate subjects are not the same.

### 1. Questions and replies

**Question:** Is evenness a quantity contrary to oddness?

**Reply:** Evenness and oddness are not quantities by their own nature. Neither can be compared with another as equal or unequal, or divided, simply by being even or odd. They belong to quality. Besides, oddness is not something positive in its own right; it is the absence of evenness.[^V04-8] So the two oppose one another through presence and absence, not as contraries. Their immediate subjects are not the same either.

**Question:** Are straight and curved lines contrary quantities?

**Reply:** They belong to quality too. They are also defining features of a line, and one cannot replace the other unless the subject changes. A straight line cannot become curved unless the surface it lies on is destroyed. So the two cannot take turns in the same subject, as contraries must.

**Question:** Are continuous and discrete contrary quantities?

**Reply:** They are the defining features that distinguish the two kinds of quantity. A defining feature does not belong to its broader kind by its own nature, even though it exists within that broader kind. So continuity and separation are quantities only incidentally.

Besides, separation is the absence of continuity in something that, by its kind or broader kind, should be continuous.[^V04-9]

**Question:** Are equal and unequal, great and small, or many and few contrary quantities?

**Reply:** These are relations found in quantities, not quantities by their own nature. As we will see when we discuss relation, such things cannot be contraries.

**Question:** Are the upper and lower places contraries?

**Reply:** Their immediate subjects are not the same, so they cannot take turns in one subject. Being above and being below are contraries, but they are not places; they are relations. A place, considered simply as a place, is neither above nor below. Each is above or below only in relation to another place. Relational things cannot be contraries.

#### VII. Quantity does not intensify or weaken

Some writers distinguish increase and decrease from becoming more or less in degree in two ways.

First, they say that when one quantity is greater than another, we can point to a matching amount in it and then to what remains over. But when one quality is stronger than another, we cannot do this.

I say that this difference comes from the nature of quantity and quality, not from a difference between increase and becoming stronger. Quantity can be divided into separate parts, while quality cannot. So when one quantity is greater, its greater amount can be pointed out as a distinct part. With quality, greater strength is a matter of the quality itself. It is not that intensification by its nature must work differently.

Second, they say that increase or decrease in quantity has no limit, while becoming stronger or weaker is limited by the two contrary extremes.

I say that this too reflects a difference between quantity and quality, not between increase and intensification. One kind can keep increasing without reaching an end, while the other is limited. Yet in both cases the idea is simply that something increases. We may choose to call one kind of increase “growth” and the other “intensification,” but that is only a choice of words.

After showing that quantity has no contraries, the Sheikh says in *The Healing* that quantity also has no weakening or intensification, no decrease or increase. He does not mean that one quantity cannot be greater or smaller than another. He means that one quantity cannot be more “quantity” than another. A line cannot be more of a line—more one-dimensional—than another, even though one line may be longer than another in the relative sense.

I say that, on their view, the same applies to quality. One blackness is not more blackness than another. But one can exist more fully or intensely than another, just as a long line exists more fully than a short one. In our view, differences of this kind are differences in existence, not in essences. Existence itself can be stronger or weaker, more complete or less complete, as we have already explained.

These two properties—having no contraries and not becoming more or less in degree—are not unique to quantity. Substance has no contrary, and neither do some kinds of quality. Quantity’s distinctive properties are the three we listed earlier, together with a fourth: it can be finite or infinite. We will now discuss that.

#### VIII. Why dimensions must be finite

There are many proofs that dimensions cannot extend without limit. We will give three.

The first is the strongest. If infinite dimensions existed, circular motion would be impossible. Imagine an infinite straight line and a sphere. Draw a line from the sphere's center that runs parallel to the infinite line. As the sphere turns, this line from its center will eventually touch the infinite line, after being parallel to it. There must then be a first point of contact on the infinite line. But that is impossible. Every point on the line has another point beyond it, and the moving line can touch each of them. It always touches a point farther along the line at a smaller angle before it touches the point beneath it. So there can be no first point of contact.

Yet this motion has a beginning in the sense that it has a starting boundary, even if there is no first instant at which it occurs. The continuous series of points where the lines touch would therefore have to have a first point, but it cannot. This is a contradiction.

Some leading later thinkers objected to this proof. They said that the angle at which the lines touch, like any angle produced by motion, has no first instant. There is no need, then, for a first point of contact—just as there is no first angle. Each angle that appears in an instant is preceded by a smaller angle in an earlier instant. When one side begins to turn from the other after the two have lined up, no angle is first in the strict sense. The same, they say, applies to the points of contact on the infinite line.

I reply that even if there is no first instant at which an angle appears, there is a first boundary from which it begins to grow, as there is with other gradual changes. If the contact angle grows gradually, then the moving point where the lines touch traces a line. That point, the line it traces, and the infinite line we first imagined must all have a starting boundary from which the connected points of contact begin—even if there is no first instant at which the angle, or the corresponding point, appears.

The second proof is that if dimensions were infinite, two lines could begin at one point and spread apart like the sides of a triangle. The distance between them would keep increasing without limit. It would therefore be infinite too, even though it is bounded by the two lines. That is impossible. This is called the ladder proof. We have explained it, along with objections and replies, in our commentary on *The Guidance*.

The third proof begins with an infinite line AB, whether it extends without limit in both directions or only in one. Mark two points on it, C and D, so that segment CD is finite. Segment CB is longer than DB by the finite amount CD. Now line up D with C. Either the two infinite segments continue together without end, in which case the longer is no longer than the shorter, or DB ends before CB. In the second case, DB is finite, and CB is longer by the finite amount CD, so CB is finite too. Either way, the line cannot be infinite.

Imam al-Razi raises a difficult objection. To line up the end of the shorter segment with the end of the longer, he says, we must do one of three things. First, move the shorter segment as a whole toward the end of the longer, or move the longer segment as a whole toward the end of the shorter. Second, lengthen the shorter segment or shorten the longer until their ends meet. Third, leave both where they are, place the end of the longer at the end of the shorter, and then keep moving the extra length from one side to the other. In this last case, the extra length never disappears, since both lines are infinite.

He objects that the first method assumes what it is trying to prove, because an infinite line cannot move into a place that it never leaves. The second cannot work, because after one segment grows or the other shrinks, they are equal. The third cannot work either: the extra length remains forever, so neither segment ends.

I reply that all three ways of lining up the segments can prove what we need.

For the first, even if the longer segment cannot move as a whole, the shorter part can move. It leaves one place and takes another.

For the second,[^V04-10] even if the segments are lined up only after the shorter one grows or the longer one shrinks, that still proves the point. The change is exactly the finite length CD. If the longer segment becomes equal to the shorter after losing a finite amount, then it too is finite.

For the third, the gap between the two segments is finite, because it is equal to CD. Once the parts apart from that gap are lined up with a finite segment, the whole distance—including the gap—is finite. There are many other proofs, but these are enough. To avoid making the discussion too long, we will give the remaining objections as questions and answers.

**Question:** Our natural judgment tells us that one body cannot be in two places at the same time. It also tells us that dimensions cannot end at a boundary with nothing beyond it. If we doubt the second judgment, should we not doubt the first as well?

**Reply:** The second may be something our imagination assumes. We do not agree that it is as certain as a basic truth such as one being half of two. Nor is it clear that our imagination firmly asserts that an infinite distance exists. It may simply find it hard to picture a distance with no further distance beyond it.

**Question:** If someone stood at the edge of the world, could they reach a hand beyond it? If yes, then there would be dimensions beyond all dimensions. If no, then some body must stop them.

**Reply:** They could not, but not because some measurable body blocks them. The conditions for such a movement are absent. The body there is not naturally able to move from place to place; its state is more like things in the imaginal world.

**Question:** If the world is finite, we can imagine adding a cubit to it, making its place wider. Add two cubits and the place is wider still, and so on. So there must be real places beyond the world, either dimensions or things with dimensions.

**Reply:** This is only something the imagination makes up. It has no reality, so it proves nothing.

**Question:** Bodily nature is one universal kind, and neither sight nor proof shows that it must exist in only one individual. A universal kind can have endlessly many possible individuals. If the possibility belongs to its nature, it belongs to every individual equally. So infinitely many possible bodies must exist. God gives generously to all that can receive, and every one of these bodies has a right to exist. He must therefore create them.

**Reply:** A limit may come from outside a thing's nature. Bodily nature may allow many bodies, but the soul and form of the celestial sphere keep its kind from appearing in more than one individual.

**Question:** This is hard to accept for the parts of a celestial sphere. The sphere's nature is present in its parts, so its parts seem to be many instances of the same nature. Perhaps there can be endlessly many parts like them.

**Reply:** A part of a celestial sphere is not itself a celestial sphere. The sphere, as a whole, has no parts in the same sense as an ordinary measurable body. It is a living being whose matter is formed by a soul that has no measurable parts. The sphere's nature and soul are one in existence, though we consider them in different ways. Its measurable parts belong to its body as matter, not to its nature as a kind.

**Question:** Time has no beginning or end. So the universe has no beginning or end, and there must be an infinite amount of matter.

**Reply:** An endless history of change does not require an infinite amount of matter. The same body can take on different forms at different times.

**Question:** Magnitude can be infinite just as number can, and great size can go on without limit just as small size can. Why, then, can there not be an infinite magnitude?

**Reply:** This is only an analogy. It gives us no reason to think one follows from the other—not even a probable reason, much less a proof.

**Question:** A body can always be divided into smaller parts. The divisions remain potential and never all become actual. In the same way, it should be possible for a body to keep growing without ever reaching the largest size. So an infinite magnitude should be possible.

**Reply:** The Sheikh says that this is true in one way but not in another. To see why it is true, take a cubit and divide it in half. Keep one half, and divide the other half in two. Add one of those smaller parts to the half you kept. Then divide the remaining part again and add half of it to the growing amount. You can keep doing this without end. The amount grows, but it never reaches the whole cubit.

The comparison fails, however, because a body cannot grow without limit. Reaching any greater size would require either more matter or the body to spread out. In either case, it would need an unlimited supply of matter or an unlimited place, and both are impossible.

**Question:** Philosophers accept that motion continues without end, and that the sphere's motion is endless. Each part of this motion exists with a body and a distance. So there must be infinitely many bodies.

**Reply:** They do not consider this impossible. What they reject is infinitely many bodies existing at the same time, not bodies existing one after another through an endless series of times.

**Question:** Your proof from matching one infinite line against another[^V04-11] is refuted by the souls that have left bodies since the Flood. The souls who left earlier are fewer than those who have left from then until now, yet both groups are infinite.

**Reply:** The philosophers say that an infinite number of things cannot exist all together if they are naturally ordered or arranged in space. But where things do not all exist together, as with motions and times, or where they exist together without a natural order, as with causes and effects, or without an order of place, as with magnitudes, the fact that one group may seem larger or smaller does not prove either group is finite.

This has confused many thinkers who did not require that two groups could actually be matched before using the comparison to prove that they were finite. It led some to deny that souls survive the body: if souls survive, their number would grow without limit, since there is no reason for some souls to survive and others not to. Others accepted the return of souls to bodies, to keep the number of souls finite. Still others said that motion and change must end, even if that meant God could no longer act, because they wanted to avoid an infinite number of individuals or parts.

All of these views came from assuming that anything that can be greater or smaller must be finite. But this is not a basic truth. If it were, no one could disagree about it. Yet people have disagreed. Some have said that bodies have infinitely many actual parts, or that the world is made of infinitely many spherical parts, or that there is an infinite mixture. Muslims agree that God's knowledge and power have no limit. Some say that the kinds of things God can create are unlimited. Some say that indivisible parts can successively occupy infinitely many places; others say that there are infinitely many things that do not exist. It is also clear that the sequence of numbers has no limit. There are infinitely many thousands, and this group is a thousand times larger than the infinitely many units. Future motions—especially the motions of the people of Paradise—are endless, yet these too can be increased or decreased.

If we put these beliefs together with the philosophers' views, we find broad agreement that an infinite series can still admit of increase and decrease. So it cannot be a basic truth that anything which can be increased or decreased must be finite. That claim can be proved only by argument, and the argument works only when the things can be matched one by one.

Here is why. A proof of finitude must show that the smaller group ends, while the larger group still has something left over. That would follow only if one part of the smaller group could not be set against two parts of the larger. If a single part of the smaller group could be paired with two parts of the larger, the smaller group would not necessarily run out while some of the larger remained. This proof therefore works only where the two groups can be brought into correspondence and two parts of either group cannot occupy the place of one part of the other.

That is why two bodies cannot occupy the same place, and why a cause and its effect cannot occupy the same level in the order of causes. When one part of either group is already matched to a part of the other, it cannot also be matched to a second part. But things that cannot be matched by nature or by place, and can be paired only by the mind, are different. The mind can pair a finite number of things from each group, yet many things may remain in both groups—more, in fact, than the mind can hold in view. A contradiction appears only when the groups can be matched in the required way and the remaining parts can be brought into view. We will explain this further later.

#### 1. A guide to infinity in past and future events

When we call past individuals infinite, we cannot mean that each one is infinite. That is plainly false. We must be speaking of them taken together. But does this mean a collection that really exists, or one held in the mind? And do we mean that this collection positively has an infinite number, or only that it has no first limit? These distinctions give us four possible claims.

The first claim—that the whole collection of past individuals exists outside the mind and has an infinite number—is false. The collection itself does not exist. Its members do not exist together, and a collection of things that cannot exist together cannot itself exist as a whole. Where would it exist: in the past, present, future, or at all times? None of these is possible.

The second claim—that the mind contains the whole collection as an actually infinite number—is also false. Our minds cannot hold infinitely many individuals before them at once. So we cannot positively describe such a complete collection, either in the world or in thought, as something that exists and possesses an infinite number. We may, however, mean that for any number of past individuals we think of, others came before them, and counting backwards never reaches one without a predecessor.

The third claim is sound: past individuals are not limited by some first individual. A negative statement does not require the whole collection to exist; we need only grasp past individuals in a general way. The fourth claim, also sound, makes the same denial about what we can imagine: however many past individuals the mind brings to mind, they are not the largest number possible. More can always be considered.

Now consider future events. First, they are not actually present; they exist only as possibilities. Each can exist at its own time. That does not mean that all of them can exist together at one time. The next question is in what sense future events are finite or infinite.

As for whether future events are finite or infinite, we can say three things about events that are still coming to be. First, at any given time they are finite in actuality, because they have reached only a certain point. Second, they are always finite in potential, because we can consider some later endpoint that they may reach. Third, they are neither actually nor potentially finite in relation to a final endpoint, since there is no last event after which nothing else can happen.

In short, future events are finite in actuality up to any present point, and can always continue; but they have no final end.

#### IX. Five further points about infinity

First, “infinity” may mean the idea itself, or something described as infinite, just as “number” may mean number itself or something that has a number. Some early philosophers made infinity itself the first principle of the world. This is wrong for two reasons. First, infinity is a negative idea; it has no reality of its own. Second, it must either be divisible or not. If it is divisible, each part would have to be equal to the whole, since there is no other nature here beyond the idea of infinity. Each part would then be infinite too, which is impossible. If it cannot be divided, it is infinite only in the negative sense of “not finite,” not in the sense of a positive, unlimited nature that can serve as a first principle.

Second, what is described as infinite must be matter, not form. Infinity is a kind of lack: the potential to go on without becoming complete. This potential is found in matter, not in form, which is already actual. So an infinite thing cannot be a complete whole. A whole is either a completed form or something that has a completed form; infinity, by contrast, is incomplete.

From this, a thoughtful person can see that this world is an incomplete, dependent reality. It cannot reach its fullness through itself. It needs another world to complete it: a world that is its fulfilled form, fully actual and without potentiality.

Third, an infinite body cannot move. If it were infinite in every direction, there would be nowhere for it to move into and nowhere to leave behind. If it were finite in some directions, that would either be because of its own nature or because something forced it to be so. The first is impossible, because one and the same nature acts equally in every direction. If the body were made finite by force, that could happen either by cutting it off or by changing its density—making a finite thing smaller by compressing it or larger by spreading and heating it. In either case, the body would be infinite by nature but finite under force. We will show that this is impossible in the next proof.

Suppose, then, that the body has been made finite on one side but remains infinite on the other. As it moves toward the open, infinite side, either it leaves the opposite side empty or it does not. If it does not, it has not moved into a new place; it has only grown larger on the open side. If it does, the infinite space it enters has no limit. This cannot be natural motion, because a natural movement must aim at a definite place. A finite body cannot move toward a place with no limit. And if the motion is not natural, it cannot be forced motion either, because force acts against a body's natural movement. Where there is no natural movement, there can be no forced movement.

Fourth, an argument in *The Wisdom of Illumination* says that an infinite body cannot exist, whether moving or at rest. The natural power within a body is the direct cause of its size, shape, position, and other bodily features. Every bodily power has a limited ability to act and be acted on. If the body's size were unlimited, its bodily power would have an unlimited effect, which is impossible.

Fifth, if an infinite body existed, it would act on other things—or be acted on—without taking any time. Suppose it acted over time on something finite. A part of the effect would be acted on by a part of the cause. The larger the body, the stronger its power and the shorter the time it takes to act. The ratio between an infinite body's power and a finite body's power would therefore mean that the infinite body's action[^V04-12] takes no time. But we assumed that it acts over time, which is a contradiction.

If the thing acted on were also infinite, then the time taken to affect one part would stand to the time taken to affect the whole in the same ratio as that part stands to the whole. So every part[^V04-13] would have to be affected in no time. This would mean that a smaller part is affected faster than a larger part that is already affected in no time, which is impossible. The same reasoning applies in reverse, from being acted on to acting.

#### X. Can magnitude exist apart from matter? Other relations among body, surface, and line

We have already seen that the magnitudes found in bodies depend on matter. Suppose, however, that there were a magnitude apart from matter. Its independence would have to come either from its nature, from features that belong to its nature, or from some added feature.

In the first two cases, magnitude would not need a subject by its own nature. But in fact it does need one. So those explanations fail.

I say that this conclusion follows only if bodily magnitude is one fixed kind, with no differences in degree among its individuals. Philosophers treat stronger and weaker qualities as different kinds that share one broad kind. We have already seen that greater and lesser magnitude, like stronger and weaker quality, are differences in perfection and deficiency. If magnitude is a broad kind rather than one fixed kind, its individuals need not all have the same properties. A shared nature is not complete until its defining features are added, and a nature acts only once it is complete.

The third possibility is that magnitude becomes independent through some added feature. The feature could either exist in magnitude, have magnitude exist in it, or have both exist in something else.

If magnitude exists in another subject, then either magnitude needs that subject or it does not. In either case, magnitude is not independent of a subject.

If the feature exists in magnitude, then either magnitude needs a subject by its nature or it does not. If it does, no added feature can make it independent. If it does not, no added feature can make it dependent, because what belongs to something by its nature cannot be removed by something else.

If both exist in another subject, the same problem is even clearer. I say that these arguments also assume that magnitude is one complete kind and that any added feature belongs to it in reality. Otherwise, someone could choose the second possibility and say that this feature belongs to magnitude only in the mind, when we analyze it. The idea of magnitude is incomplete in thought; it does not by itself require either independence or dependence, except through the form that exists with it and is separated from it only in thought.

This is like natural body. Body by itself does not require that it be an animal or that it not be an animal. Each requirement comes from a further form that completes natural body. The same is true of geometrical body. In our view, these forms are united with body in existence.

#### 1. A difficulty and its answer

You might ask whether this view means that a vacuum could exist outside the mind, since a vacuum would be magnitude without matter.

If a vacuum exists, it is not mere magnitude. It is magnitude with position in the directions of the world. Magnitude by itself does not have position in that sense. We have already distinguished the position that is one of the categories from the kind of position found in quantities.

Anything a person can imagine with a clear meaning could exist outside the mind unless something prevents it. What prevents things from existing here is that this is a material world, set in directions and open to opposing qualities. We will prove that dimensioned forms can exist outside matter, just as separate intellectual forms can.

If we imagine the three dimensions without matter or its features, the result is a geometrical body. In imagination it can only be finite. To imagine a finite body is to imagine that it ends. That ending is a surface, not because it is finite—finitude is a lack, and a lack does not belong to a category—but because the surface is divided in two directions.

When this surface is considered apart from material features such as color, smoothness, and roughness, it is a geometrical surface. A geometrical line can be considered in the same way. Neither is imagined apart from geometrical body.

We have already explained the difference between considering something without requiring anything else to accompany it, and considering it while requiring that nothing else accompany it. We can imagine a three-dimensional magnitude in either way. But we can imagine a surface, line, or point only in the first way, not in the second.

The three forms—body, surface, and line—are features of natural body. We can show that body is an accident in two ways. First, a body can grow larger or smaller while remaining the same kind of substance. Second, if a simple body is divided in two, each half has the same nature as the whole but a different magnitude. If magnitude made a thing what it is, the halves would differ in nature.

Whether the other three forms are accidents depends first on whether they exist. Some deny that they exist because they are only the ends of things, and an end is where the thing stops or runs out.

The truth is that line and surface can each be considered in three ways. A surface can be considered as an end, as something in which we can imagine two directions at right angles, or as something that can be measured, shaped, and compared with another as larger, smaller, or equal.

As an end, it is not a magnitude and does not belong to a category. But because it is the end of something that can be imagined in three directions, it can also be imagined as having two directions. In this respect it belongs to relation, though relation itself is never found without magnitude. You will later see the difference between a real relation and something commonly called a relation.

As something two-dimensional, it is not a magnitude by itself. It becomes measurable only by being compared with another surface. It cannot differ from another surface simply by existing or not existing; in every respect it is an accident.

As an end, a surface is not pure nothing. It is a real boundary of a finite natural body. It exists in that body, but not as one of its parts, and it cannot exist without the body.[^V04-14] Whether it is also an accident of geometrical magnitude is open to question.[^V04-15]

The other two ways of considering a surface—and all three ways of considering a line—are as magnitude. Since surface, considered as magnitude, is an accident, it is clear that the whole surface is an accident too. Its two-dimensional nature relates to its being measurable as a defining feature relates to a broad kind; it is not that one part is an accident while the other is not. The same account applies to a line: as an end, as one-dimensional, and as a measurable line.

A point has only one of these meanings: it is the end of a line. If line and surface exist, they are accidents, because either can appear or disappear while the natural body remains the same.

We can also show that line and surface exist because bodies touch. If they touched as complete bodies, or through parts inside them, they would overlap. So they must touch at their ends. Whatever makes contact must exist. Bodies clearly touch by their surfaces, and they can also touch along a line, as with certain tapered bodies. If it seems strange to say that this contact is only incidental, remember that it is the bodies themselves that touch, but they do so through their ends. In the sense in which line and surface are boundaries, they are negative features. So bodies can truly touch directly, with nothing between them, and still touch by means of a line or surface.

The old saying that a point in motion traces a line, a line traces a surface, and a surface traces a body is either a symbolic way of speaking, as was common among early writers, or a way of describing what happens in imagination. In imagination, a moving point produces a line; moving that line in another direction produces a surface; moving the surface in a third direction produces a body. In reality, the order is the reverse: the body comes before the surface, the surface before its edge, and the edge before its endpoint. Each whole comes before its boundary.

#### XI. Further questions about magnitude

First, the particular magnitude found in this world depends on matter. The bodily form does not determine it by itself; otherwise, all bodies would share one size. Nor is it determined by the agent alone, without matter. When an agent gives a bodily form a magnitude it did not have before, it must stretch it in one direction or shrink it in another. This requires the body to be acted on, and being acted on depends on matter.

But it is not matter in general that gives the body a particular magnitude. Other conditions of matter make it ready for one size rather than another. This readiness is a kind of possibility, and it depends on a specific form that acts either by its own nature or because of an outside force. In either case, it depends on a particular kind of form together with matter that can receive it.

A particular magnitude may be separated from matter and its conditions in imagination, or in another world. But a specific individual form cannot be separated from matter unless its existence changes into a stronger and more complete kind of existence, one without many of the limits it has here.

Second, straightness and curvature are defining features that distinguish one kind of line from another. One line cannot be both straight and curved. In the same way, flatness and curvature distinguish kinds of surface. Different degrees of bending and rounding also distinguish kinds of line and surface. These defining features belong to quality, as we will explain when we discuss the qualities that belong to magnitude.

Third, a point cannot be divided because it is the end of a line.[^V04-16] If it had a part, the other part would be the end. In the same way, a line cannot be divided across its width, and a surface cannot be divided through its depth. So a collection of points cannot make a line. If the space between them prevents their ends from overlapping, that space itself can be divided. If it does not prevent them from overlapping, they all overlap. But magnitude requires parts that are distinct, in different places, and not overlapping. The same argument shows that lines cannot make a surface, and surfaces cannot make a body.

Fourth, there cannot be a numerical or measurable ratio between any two of these three: point, line, and surface. The reason is the one already given.

Fifth, Euclid defines a point as “that which has no part.” Some say this definition only distinguishes a point from magnitudes. But it also describes unity and God, so it does not distinguish a point from everything else. To make the definition unique to a point, another condition is needed. Some say a point is something with position that cannot be divided, or that has no parts. God and unity have no position and cannot be pointed out in this way. Others define a point as the end of a line.

I say that this definition is not disproved by the tip of a cone or the center of a sphere. The tip is the end only of the cone's axis, not of the whole body or its surface, as some later thinkers have assumed. The center is the end of the radii that meet there, even if those radii exist only in imagination.

We have already discussed many features of number in our discussion of unity and multiplicity. Two topics remain under quantity: angle and place. We will discuss angle with the qualities that belong to quantity, and turn now to place.

#### XII. What is place, and does it exist?

We may know something through some of its signs and features but not others. We then ask about the features we do not know, whether they exist, how they should be defined, and how they should be divided. By “place,” we mean something a body can move from or into, rest in, point to by saying “here” or “there,” measure as a half or a quarter, and share with no other body at the same time. These are four agreed signs of place. They help keep the debate from becoming a dispute about words.

Some deny that place exists. They argue that if it did, it would have to be either a substance or an accident, and neither is possible.

First, if place were a substance, it would not be immaterial, because it has position. It would therefore be a body. But every body has a place. If place were a body, it too would need a place, and the same would be true of that place, without end. It would also mean that bodies overlap.

Second, if place were an accident, it would either exist in the body that occupies the place or in something else. If it existed in the body, it would move with that body. The body would not move into or out of place; it would move together with its place. And the body would not be in its place; its place would be in the body.

If place existed in something that did not occupy it, then it would not be a feature of the body in place. The thing in place would have to be something else, because a thing is “in a place” only if place exists in it. That would make the container, not what it contains, the thing in place.

They offer a second proof. If motion requires place, then either place requires motion—which cannot be, since place exists even when nothing moves—or motion requires place. But motion must depend on one of the four causes: an agent, matter, form, or goal. Place is not the agent, since motion is caused by nature, choice, or force. Nor is it the matter, since the moving thing is the matter.[^V04-17] It is not the form, and it cannot be the goal, since a goal only needs to exist when it is reached, while place must exist before the body reaches it. A goal is either a special or shared completion. A special completion is the form of a thing, and place is not the moving thing's form. A shared completion belongs to it and to other things, but place, as you define it, belongs only to that body.

They offer a third proof. If every body is in a place, then a growing body must be in a place, and that place must grow with it. The place of that place would then need another place, and so on without end. This conclusion is impossible, so the starting claim must be false.

They offer a fourth proof. Motion is a change of where something is, a move from being near to being far, or the reverse. If movement requires place, then a point must also have a place because it can move. But a point cannot have a place.

The answer to the first proof is that place may be a dimensioned substance, but not a material body; this would avoid both impossible overlap and an endless series.[^V04-18] Or it may be an accident that exists in something other than what occupies it. Place would then be the inner surface of the container, touching the outer surface of the thing it contains. As for the word “located,” it need not be derived from “place” in a way that proves that place exists in the located thing. Many words are formed in a similar way without matching a real relation. We do not accept that “located” must come from “place”; it may come from the body's ability to occupy space. Even if the word is derived from “place,” names are often formed from accidents.

For example, “known” is formed from “knowledge,”[^V04-19] and something known exists in the person who knows it.

The answer to the second proof is that motion may depend on place without place being one of the four causes. Not everything something depends on must be one of those causes. Two, for example, depends on one, but one is not one of the four causes of two. Place is something that must already be there as a condition for movement; it is naturally prior to the movement.

The answer to the third proof is that as a body grows, it takes up one place after another. So the place does not have to grow along with it.

The answer to the fourth proof is that there is a difference between movement in itself and movement by accident. A point moves only by accident, as part of a moving line.

Some who accept that place exists say that its existence is self-evident. This is the better view. Others give proofs. One is that movement is a change in the category of “where.” We can see this change while the body itself, its size, its qualities, position, and other features stay the same. We can also see these other features change while its place stays the same. So movement from place to place is a real change in where something is—that is, in its relation to place.

Another proof is that we see one body present, then gone, and another body appear where it was. We naturally judge that the two bodies share something. That shared thing is place: it belonged to the first body and then to the second.

A third proof is that above and below clearly exist, and this requires place. Each of these arguments, however, leaves room for further thought.

One scholar has set out the different views about place in this way. The thing we know through the signs just given cannot be separate from a body and its conditions. It must either be a part of a body or not. If it is a part, it is either matter or form. If it is not a part, it is still equal to the body in size. Then it may be the three-dimensional extension matching the body; or a surface of another body that touches it, whether that other body contains it or is contained by it; or, more precisely, the inner surface of the containing body in contact with the outer surface of what it contains. These make five views, each of which has had defenders.

Those who say place is matter argue that bodies occupy it in succession, just as different forms come to exist in the same matter. Those who say place is form argue that place is bounded and contains something, and form is also bounded and contains something. But neither argument follows from its premises: both infer an identity merely from two features shared by different things. The first argument might instead be put as follows: place receives successive occupants, and whatever receives successive occupants is matter.

The second argument might be restated in this way: place has a boundary and contains something, and everything with a boundary that contains something is a form. But the second claim is false. There are three further reasons against both accounts. First, we leave a place when we move, but do not leave our matter or form. Second, we move in search of a place, not in search of our matter or form. Third, we can describe a made thing by the matter it is made from, as when we say “wooden door”; we do not describe it in the same way by its place.

#### XIII. Establishing the Nature of Place

In the previous chapter, we established that place exists. We can now ask what it is.

There is no doubt that a body as a whole occupies a place. So place cannot be indivisible, or divided in just one direction. It must either extend in two directions, in which case it is a surface, or in all three, in which case it is a three-dimensional extension. If place is a surface, it cannot be inside the body that occupies it, or it would move when that body moved. It must belong to what contains the body, and it must touch the body on every side. If place is a three-dimensional extension, it cannot be an accident, since different bodies occupy it one after another; nor can it be material,[^V04-20] since that would mean material substances could pass through one another.

So place must be either the surface just described, as the First Teacher, Aristotle, and his followers hold, or a separate three-dimensional extension matching the body's whole size, as Plato, the Stoics, and the earlier philosophers held. The scholar al-Tusi followed the latter view. On this account, place is like a substance between the two worlds. This view is supported by the proof we will give for a dimensioned world surrounding ours—not as a container surrounds what it holds, but as a nature surrounds a body or a soul surrounds a body. Place may be of this kind. The signs we will discuss also support this view.

Some who hold that place is an extension say that its existence is self-evident. Everyone, they say, understands that water is between the sides of a vessel, that half the water occupies half the vessel's place, and that each part of the water has its own corresponding part of that place, however the water is divided. Others offer proofs. Their arguments take two main forms: some establish that place is an extension, while others try to show that it cannot be a surface.

The first kind of argument begins with the fact that we clear up confusion by removing things one by one until only one remains. Place is like that. Imagine the water leaving a vessel while no air enters it. The three-dimensional space between the vessel's sides would still remain. That is the point to be proved.

A second argument is that a body occupies a place by its volume, not merely by its surface. A body has three dimensions, and so does the place it occupies.

The arguments against place being a surface are these. First, a body would be both still and moving at the same time. A bird at rest in the air, or a fish at rest in water, would be moving whenever the air or water around it flowed, because the surface said to be its place would be moving.

Second, place must be something fixed, from which a moving body departs and into which it arrives. But the boundaries of a container can move from one location to another, even as part of another movement. So a surface cannot be place.

Third, place can be empty or full. These are features of a three-dimensional extension, not of a surface.

Fourth, if place were a surface, the parts of a body would have no place. That is impossible, since each part of a body is itself a body.

Fifth, fire as a whole naturally seeks a place above, and earth as a whole seeks a place below. The place they seek cannot be a boundary, since a boundary is a kind of absence. Nor can the whole body itself touch that boundary. The place they seek must therefore be the three-dimensional extension in the relevant direction.

Sixth, this view leaves the outermost celestial sphere without a place. It also fails to account for many bodies whose places cannot be found this way. Consider a drum-shaped body with two matching circular faces, each fitted against a corresponding face on another body. Or consider a body with a hollow surface fitted against a convex one. Even around the whole circumference[^V04-21], the surface account gives these bodies no place.

Seventh, place would remain the same when the body in it became smaller, and could even grow while that body shrank. It could also grow while the body stayed the same size. The first happens with a bag full of water or air when some of its contents are removed; the second with a perforated body; the third with a compressed candle as it spreads out, or when a body is divided into parallel parts. This conflicts with the claim that place and the body occupying it are equal in size.

Those who hold that place is a surface have answers to these arguments in their books. We leave them aside because they are weak. Their familiar account is that each lower one of the thirteen celestial spheres has as its place the inner surface of the sphere above it and the outer surface of the sphere below it. The lowest place is the inner surface of the water and air, with the separation between them.
