On this view, one body does not have one continuous place, and a contained body has no part of its natural place. Answers can be offered, though they are not entirely satisfying.

The main argument for the surface view is that if place were an extension, two extensions of the same kind would occupy the same matter without anything to distinguish them. When two such things occur in one matter, there is no better reason to say that one is an accident and the other is not than to say that both are accidents or that one is an accident of the other. Each choice would be arbitrary.

We reply that the two extensions need not belong to the same specific kind. They also argue that we can imagine two separate extensions between the sides of a vessel, even though they have the same nature and occupy the same indicated space. But that would be like saying that the one person we point to could be two people—or even an unlimited number of people.

We reply that a thing's unity is shown by the unity of its effects, features, and causes. If these are one, the thing is one; if they differ, the thing is more than one. The effects and features of an extension differ from those of a body. A body can leave the extension in question and another body can enter it, while the extension remains. So they are two things.

You might object that we cannot distinguish the extension between the vessel's sides from a body in it, since it is impossible for another body not to enter when the water leaves. We reply that the extension is definite in itself, while body in general is not definite unless it takes on one particular form. The extension is therefore distinct from natural body in general.

Another argument against separate extension is this: it must be either finite or infinite. The earlier proofs rule out infinity. So it must be finite,[^V04-22] and whatever is finite has boundaries and a shape. That shape must either belong to it by its nature or come from an agent. If it belongs by nature, then a part of it must have the same shape as the whole, since the part and whole share the same kind of nature. But if so, the whole would not have a shape. That is a contradiction. If an agent gives it shape without matter, then extension itself must be able to be divided, joined, and stretched. That is impossible. So its shape must come from matter. The extension would then be material and therefore a body, which contradicts the claim that it is separate.

Another argument is that bodies resist overlapping. The source of this resistance must be what makes a thing occupy a place by its own nature. Only magnitude does that; neither matter, form, nor other accidents do.

Matter, as we will explain later, has no position or place by itself. Form does not occupy a place by itself either. A single body can spread out and occupy a larger space, then contract and occupy a smaller one, while its bodily form remains the same. So the form by itself does not occupy the space; otherwise, the amount of space occupied would not change while the form remained one. Nor do other forms and accidents occupy space in themselves. Only magnitude does. So magnitude must be what prevents bodies from overlapping. If place were also an extension, bodies would have to overlap, which is impossible.

I say that this division of possibilities leaves something out. We can reject each option except accidents, since the cause of resistance may be matter together with magnitude, or form together with magnitude, or form together with matter. A sign of this is that we can imagine a vast extension like the whole world, with everything in the heavens and on earth, and then imagine another extension, equal to it or many times greater, inside it. We can imagine one open space after another without bodies crowding or pushing one another, while these dimensioned worlds remain as they are. Many people on the spiritual path report seeing many such worlds early in their journey, with no crowding or pressure between them.

It is also reported that our leader and guide, peace be upon him, saw a garden of Paradise between his tomb and his pulpit; saw, across the width of a wall, a garden as wide as the heavens and earth; once saw Gabriel spread across both horizons; and, on the Night Journey, saw his community filling the horizon with the faces of its righteous people.

Those who say that vision happens when an image of the thing seen is imprinted in the crystalline part of the eye also face overlapping extensions. The image has magnitude, as does the material organ, yet the two overlap. They explain this by saying that the image is separate from matter, while the organ's magnitude is material. So the obstacle to bodies entering one another's place is not magnitude alone, but magnitude together with matter.

The reason is that being material means being accompanied by potentiality and readiness as such, and potentiality cannot exist alongside actuality. Continuous quantity can be divided into separate parts; when that happens, its former continuous identity disappears. Separate parts, in turn, have the potential to become continuous, and when they join, their separate identities disappear. A body behaves in the same way: it occupies a place only by leaving the place it had before. Just as one body cannot occupy two places at once, two bodies cannot occupy one place—not because place rejects them, but because material things cannot come together in that way. Even two parts of one body cannot occupy the same spot. So this is a feature of matter alone.

Anyone who studies a natural body closely will see that it is part of its nature to seek and keep its natural place. This helps preserve the body's existence as a body, not as a surface. A body does not naturally seek something it cannot reach by occupying it and fully entering it. The surface of a container cannot itself be entered by what it contains, and a body cannot touch that surface in its own right. Nor must the surface even be present when the body seeks its place.

Movement in quality also involves seeking a quality that is not yet present. But while the body is changing, it has a continuous quality of its own until it reaches the quality it seeks.

The same applies to movement in quantity, position, and other categories. If place is an extension, and the category of “where” is a body's relation to it, then place and “where” extend continuously along the body's path, from the start of its movement to the end. That continuous extension is what the body seeks through movement.

But if place is a surface, and “where” is the body's relation to that surface, neither the surface nor the relation to it can form a continuous series through movement. A separate surface at each instant would require surfaces to follow one another without end and time and place to be made up of indivisible moments. Nor could one surface last throughout the whole time of the movement. This too points to place being a three-dimensional extension.

#### XIV. Reply to Those Who Believe in a Vacuum

Most who believe in a vacuum say that it is not something that exists. Imam al-Razi describes their view without making it sound as if they think the vacuum itself exists. They say that two bodies can exist without touching and with no body touching the space between them. They claim that the apparent dimensions between the two bodies are only a false impression, just as it is false to imagine that the region beyond the world is either empty or filled. Others accept that the vacuum is a real thing.

The first group's view is false because if no body touches the two bodies in question, the distance between them can be a cubit or more than a cubit. Anything that can be equal to or greater than something else must be a real quantity, not a mere thought. So the distance between them must be an extended thing. This differs from the imagined extensions beyond the world, which cannot exist.

Two arguments also show that the second group's view is false.

First, as we have seen, a vacuum can be measured. Measurement is a feature of quantity, so a vacuum must either be a quantity or have quantity. Quantity is either discrete or continuous. It cannot be discrete, because then it would be made up of indivisible units. A body can be divided without end, so such units could not match the body. Also, discrete quantity is number, which has no position, while a body does. So if a vacuum is a quantity, it must be continuous.

If this continuous quantity has position in its own right, it is a body, contrary to the claim that it is a vacuum. If it has position only because of something else, then it is joined to a substance that has position and is therefore not empty. The same conclusion follows if the vacuum is continuous quantity in its own right: a magnitude with position cannot exist apart from matter, so it would be a body.

Someone might reply that a thing's being a magnitude does not by itself mean it has position. But if the vacuum has quantity only by accident, it will be continuous only by accident too. It could not then retain its dimensions when imagined apart from bodies and magnitudes, as a vacuum is supposed to do. This leaves three possibilities: the vacuum exists in a magnitude, the magnitude exists in the vacuum, or both exist in something else. In the first case, since magnitude exists in matter, the vacuum exists in matter too. In the second, the subject of magnitude is matter, so again the vacuum exists in matter. In the third, the vacuum is a body, since a body is simply something that can have dimensions. So what was supposed to be a vacuum turns out to be a body, and the claim is false.

Another argument is that if a body were in a vacuum, it could neither move nor rest, which is impossible. The parts of a vacuum must either be alike or different. If they are different, the difference must either belong to each part by its nature or come from something added to it. It cannot belong to the nature of the parts, since a vacuum means separate dimensions, and all parts of one continuous extension are alike in kind. Nor can the difference come from something added: any such difference could be removed, leaving the parts alike. If the parts of the vacuum are alike, no place would be naturally suited to the body while another was naturally avoided. The body would have no natural place, and so could have neither natural rest nor natural movement. Forced movement would also be impossible, since it goes against a body's natural movement. Nor could the body move by choice or rest by choice, since nothing could explain why it chose one of two identical places over the other.

I say that this argument proves only that extension by itself cannot be what draws a body into movement. The body may seek a place with a particular arrangement. And having several alike places does not stop a body from resting in one of them by nature. The parts of a basic element such as water or air rest naturally in their own region. Otherwise, no part of such an element could rest or move naturally in its place.

The next argument is the one that matters most. When a body moves through a medium, it moves faster through a thinner medium and slower through a denser one. A thin medium yields readily; a dense one resists the body more. If a body moved through a vacuum, its movement would still take time, since it crosses a distance that can be divided into parts, passing through one part before another.

Now suppose the same body travels the same distance through a medium, taking ten times as long as it would through the vacuum. Then suppose it travels through a medium ten times thinner than the first. A tenfold increase in thinness would make this third movement take one-tenth as long as the second.

That would be exactly the time taken by the first movement, through a vacuum. Movement in a resisting medium would then take as long as movement with no resistance. If the third medium were thinner in a still greater proportion, it would allow faster movement than a vacuum. That is even more absurd.

A critic objects that the contradiction comes from first giving movement a definite time of its own, then denying that movement[^V04-23] has its own time and making its duration depend on resistance in the medium. But movement is, by its nature, the crossing of a distance. A body must cross part of the distance before it crosses the whole, just as it must cross each part before it crosses that part's whole. Movement therefore requires time in itself. The sphere moves through time even though there is no resistance or obstacle to its movement. The density of the medium can make movement take longer, but it does not create the time movement takes.

If this is so, all three movements have the same basic amount of time—the time that belongs to the movement itself, including the movement through the vacuum. Resistance adds time: an hour might correspond to the movement itself and the remaining nine hours to resistance. If another medium has one-tenth as much resistance, the movement through it still takes the basic hour, plus one-tenth of those nine hours, or nine-tenths of an hour. The total is one hour and nine-tenths, so movement through a resisting medium does not take the same time as movement through the vacuum. This objection was made by the author of *The Considerations* and approved by Imam al-Razi. We will explain how to answer it when we discuss inclination.

Another argument, which will be discussed in the section on motion, says that when a stone is forcibly thrown upward, it moves because the mover gives it an upward power. The stone loses that power only through collisions with the air. If the path contained pure vacuum, the stone would face no collisions and would not lose the power; it would keep moving until it reached the sphere of the heavens. Since it does not, the path cannot be empty.

This argument is weak. It does not show that there is matter throughout the world, much less that there must be. It shows only that there is air along some of the paths through which a stone is thrown. There could still be large pockets of vacuum between those regions, and there could be pure vacuum beyond the point where the stone's motion ends.

#### XV. Observations That Point to the Impossibility of a Vacuum

First, if a narrow-necked vessel is full of water and has a small opening at the bottom, the water flows out when the top is open but does not flow when the top is sealed. This shows that, despite the water's natural weight, it cannot descend when a vacuum would have to form above it. But what about mercury flowing out, or water flowing when the opening is made wider, or water failing to flow when half the vessel contains air? These cases do not disprove the argument. Mercury is so heavy that it may force the air to move; if the air has nowhere to go, it may push the water aside and enter through the opening. If it cannot do that, the mercury remains in place, since nature takes the easier path. It is also possible that holding the water in place is easier than making the air expand.

Second, if one end of a tube is dipped in water and we suck at the other end, the water rises as the air leaves, even though water naturally falls. This response to the departing air shows that a vacuum cannot form. The same is true when flesh rises under a cupping glass. The surfaces are pulled together, but not because a vacuum has a power of attraction, as we will show later. Another sign is that if someone sucks very hard on a thin bottle or cupping glass, it breaks. If we place a cupping glass on an anvil and suck, the anvil rises with it.

Third, if we put the end of a tube into a bottle and seal the gap between the tube and the bottle's neck, then pull on a solid tube, the bottle breaks inward. If we push the tube in, the bottle breaks outward. If we suck through a hollow tube, the air inside turns to fire, and the bottle may split.

Fourth, if a vacuum were possible, water would flow out of narrow-necked vessels without air having to rise into them. We would not see bubbles.

People who defend the vacuum rely on several weak arguments. They say that without a vacuum, every movement from one place to another would have to be circular. They say that even a gnat would have to move when the heavens move from their places. They claim that expansion[^V04-24] and contraction prove that a vacuum exists. They also argue that when something grows, what it takes in must leave empty spaces inside it. Finally, they say that if every body had to touch another body at its surface, there would have to be infinitely many bodies. If this were not necessary, they say, a body could exist with no neighboring body, and so a vacuum could exist. These claims are easily answered.

Their strongest argument is this: if we raise one smooth surface evenly from another equally smooth surface, sensation tells us that they separate all at once. In reality, either parts of the upper surface rise before others, which would mean the body breaks apart—a claim our senses refute, especially for stone or iron—or all its parts rise together. In that case, the space between the surfaces is empty for a time, because the body moves from the outside toward the middle. It cannot enter through holes, since some bodies have no holes. It must come in from the sides, so it must first pass along the outer edge before reaching the middle. The middle must therefore be empty for a time. That is the vacuum they claim to prove.

We reply that such perfectly smooth surfaces cannot be lifted in this way. Surfaces that appear smooth to the senses still have roughness and ridges.

Those who defend the vacuum also point to several observations. First, if we suck hard on a bottle, cover its opening with a finger, turn it upside down, and put its opening in water, then remove the finger, a great deal of water enters. They say that if the bottle were still full of air after we sucked, no water could enter, just as it did not enter before.

Second, if we press the sides of a leather bag together until no air remains inside and tie it tightly, then lift one side away from the other, an empty space forms between them.

Third, an experiment shows that liquid can be drawn into a leather bag whose mouth is tied shut, pressing against and swelling the air inside it. Without a vacuum inside, they say, the liquid could not enter.

Fourth, we see a vessel full of ashes that can also hold enough water to fill it. If there were no empty spaces in the ashes, this would be impossible.

Fifth, fill a large jar with wine. Then put that same wine into a leather bag and place the bag, with its wine, inside the original jar. The jar can hold them both, they argue, only if empty spaces within the wine can be compressed enough to make room for the leather bag.

Here are our answers. To the first: if your explanation were right, the water would not have to rise, because the air that left the bottle had already made room for it. Since your view allows part of the bottle to be empty, this example is better evidence against your view than for it. The explanation is that sucking warms the air, and the warmth makes it expand. The air is ready to return to its natural size. When it meets the cold water, it contracts and returns to that size, and the water follows because a vacuum cannot form.

This answers the second example too. Air enters the pores of the bag; experiments confirm that the space is filled with matter.

As for the third, the bag may contract, its surroundings may expand, or one side may rise—each just enough to make room for the liquid that has entered through the tube.

The fourth example is simply false.

For the fifth, the change in the jar's size may be too small for us to notice. Or the wine may compress and give off vapor or air; it may also condense and take up less space.

#### XVI. If a Vacuum Existed, It Could Not Attract or Repel Bodies

Muhammad ibn Zakariyya al-Razi claimed that a vacuum attracts bodies. He used as evidence that water stays in vessels called water siphons and is drawn into suction vessels. Others say that a vacuum pushes bodies upward.

The first claim is false. A vacuum has no differences between its parts, so it could not pull things toward one direction rather than another.

The second claim is also false.[^V04-25] A vacuum that pushes a body must either be spread through the body's interior or surround it from outside. If it is inside, it must move either the body's parts or the whole body. It cannot move each part, since there is no vacuum within each part. The parts would have to be moved by some other cause, and then the whole body would move because those causes move its parts, not because of a vacuum.

Nor can the vacuum move the whole body without moving its parts. A cause of a composite thing must also be a cause of its parts.[^V04-26] If the vacuum surrounds a large body from outside, it plainly cannot lift it upward. So a vacuum could affect only a body whose parts spread apart, creating empty spaces between them. But that would mean some bodies naturally push their own similar parts away from one another. This is impossible: likeness draws things together. It would also mean that the parts, though all alike in nature, flee in different directions.

If there is nothing for the parts to flee from, then their flight makes no sense. If there is something, each part would have to flee from itself, since all have the same nature.

There is another difficulty. A vacuum that pushes a body upward would have to stay with the body as it moves. Then the vacuum itself would need a natural place to move toward. If it does not stay with the body, the body keeps moving through one vacuum after another and touches each one only for an instant. But nothing can move something else at a single instant, and after that instant the vacuum no longer touches it. Someone might say that the vacuum gives the body a power that moves it upward. But that conflicts with the claim that the parts of a vacuum are all alike.

#### 1. A Note

The theologians claimed that a body's place is the surface it rests on, which keeps it from falling. But after considering the matter, they saw that the lower body as a whole cannot be the place of the body above it; only its surface can be. Yet they also say that an arrow passing through the air has a place, even though nothing beneath it stops it from falling. If the body below it were its place, only part of that body's surface could be.

Some say that any surface can be place. They argue that just as the surface of a jar is the place of water, the surface of water is the place of the jar. They also argue that the outermost celestial sphere has a place because it moves, but has no surrounding boundary. Its place must therefore be the surface beneath it. This argument is weak, since the sphere's movement is not movement from place to place but a change of position.

These points also undermine the theologians' view and the view of anyone who says place is always a surface. Most people agree that a body has only one place, as we have already noted. Others say that place is a body that surrounds what occupies it. But then place is only a relation, and the outermost sphere has no place.

The soundest and most complete view is that place is an extension. This extension is not itself positioned in space, and it does not surround or align with bodily dimensions in the way that one positioned thing contains another. The proofs that show positional dimensions must be finite do not apply to it, since it has no position in its own right.

Above every material extension is another material extension, until we reach the last of the material, positioned extensions. Above it is an extension that needs no matter and has no passive power, since actuality and form predominate in it. It has no positional shape and cannot be pointed to by the senses, though it can be indicated in imagination.

Perhaps what religious language calls the Lote Tree of the Farthest Boundary is the last of the positioned extensions. The Throne on which divine mercy is established may be what surrounds all sensory extensions without itself being positioned. It would then have two sides and stand between the two worlds. On its upper side, it receives forms and images from God; on its lower side, it connects with bodily forms and their material dimensions.

The imagination within us is like this. It is a dimensioned substance, extended in space. It receives the imaginal forms that flow from God and connects with the body and its material dimensions. It is connected to the body at first because its existence is weak. When it becomes complete, it separates from the body—not as one body separates from another, but as a writer separates from what he has written, or a speaker from what he has said.

We will examine this fully when we come to the knowledge of the afterlife. The remaining questions about quantity will be discussed under time. From God come protection and right guidance.

### Part Two: The Category of Quality

This art consists of an introduction and four parts.

#### Introduction: Defining Quality and Dividing It into Four Kinds

We can define the broadest categories only incompletely. No broader kind exists above them, and there is no single feature that distinguishes each one from other members of a broader kind. A definition that combines two equally general concepts, each treated as a distinguishing feature, is only a possibility in thought. We do not know that such a definition is real; usually, we prove that it is not.

We have found no single feature shared by all qualities except that each is a property of something, rather than a thing in its own right, and is distinct from quantity and from relations. But this defines quality by something just as unclear as quality itself. None of the broadest categories is clearer than the others. If one were, the same would be true of the remaining categories. This is especially true of properties that express relations, because we cannot understand those relations without first understanding the things that have them.

So philosophers avoid naming quantity and relational properties directly. Instead, they use features of them that are easier to grasp. The familiar definition of quality is: a stable state that does not, when understood, require us to understand something outside itself and its subject; and that does not require division or a relation among the parts of its subject. Its stability distinguishes it from acting and being acted on. Not requiring us to think of something else distinguishes it from relation, where, when, and possession. Not requiring division distinguishes it from quantity; and not requiring relations among the subject's parts distinguishes it from position.

There are several problems with this definition.

First, when we speak of something acting, we mean that it has an effect on another thing.[^V04-27] The thing it affects may either remain unchanged or change. If it remains unchanged, the ability to affect it remains unchanged too, because that ability follows from the nature of what acts. Calling quality “stable” does not exclude it. If the thing affected changes, the ability to affect it is not a further property added to the nature of the thing that acts. Like other relations that follow from things, this is something the mind understands by considering the things together. So there is no need to exclude it by adding the word “stable.” The same applies to being acted on.

Second, the phrase “does not require us to understand something outside itself and its subject” already distinguishes quality from those two categories. There is no need for the word “stable” to distinguish it from them.[^V04-28] Someone might say that stability is meant to distinguish quality from time. But the condition that quality does not require division already excludes time, as time requires division in what carries it, namely movement.

Third, sound belongs to the category of quality, since it does not belong to any other category or to motion, as careful thinkers agree. Yet sound is not stable, since its parts do not exist together at the same time. This is clear in itself, and sound is also caused by motion; what is caused by something unstable is unstable too.

Fourth, the definition also fits unity and a point. One might say that unity makes us think of indivisibility and a point makes us think of the end of a line. But if the definition means that understanding a quality never makes us think of anything else, then many qualities would be excluded: we cannot understand straightness or curvature without thinking of magnitude. If instead it means only that understanding it does not require us to think of something outside its subject, the problem remains for unity and a point.

Fifth, perception, knowledge, power, desire, anger, and the other traits of the soul cannot be understood without also understanding what they are about: the perceiver, what is known, desired, or resented. Someone might say that each trait can be understood on its own, and that we understand it before we understand its object. This differs from relations, where we must first know both things that are related. The distinction is valid, but the definition does not express it unless we read its first part one way and its second part another. That reading would not fit the definition as a whole.

Sixth, suppose the definition means that understanding one thing does not depend on understanding another. Even then, it does not fit geometrical figures such as squares and triangles, or properties of numbers such as being a square or a cube, though these are counted among the kinds of quality.

Seventh, “state” has several different meanings. We speak of a state of existence, independence, or stability; of being a substance or an accident; of sitting or lying down; and of acting or being acted on. The word has no single meaning that covers them all, so definitions should avoid it.

Most of these objections can be answered. A clearer definition is this: a quality is a property whose meaning does not depend on first understanding another thing, and which does not directly require the thing that has it either to be divisible or to be indivisible. Calling it a property rules out God and things that exist in their own right. Saying that its meaning does not depend on first understanding something else rules out properties that express relations, since we must first understand the things being related. This does not mean that understanding a quality never brings another thing to mind; rather, understanding it does not depend on first understanding that other thing. This definition includes sound, since we can understand sound without first understanding something else. Saying that quality does not, by its own nature, require division or indivisibility excludes quantity, unity, and a point. The phrase “by its own nature” also excludes knowledge of things that cannot be divided: knowledge does not itself require indivisibility, but may have it because of the unity of what is known.

As for dividing quality into kinds, observation gives us four: sensible qualities, qualities of the soul, qualities specific to quantity, and dispositions. The division is based on induction. It can be expressed in several ways, depending on which feature we use to distinguish each kind. In short, if a quality has the features of the first kind, it belongs to the first; if it has the features of the second, it belongs to the second; if it has the features of the third, it belongs to the third; otherwise, it belongs to the fourth.

Some of these features are hard to understand. Imam al-Razi calls qualities of the soul “perfections.” The Sheikh describes them as qualities that do not belong to bodies, and describes dispositions as features belonging to a body in its nature. He describes sensible qualities as qualities whose effects make something else resemble them: heat makes nearby things hot, and blackness casts its image into the eye. Weight, by contrast, moves the body but does not make it heavy.

Al-Razi says that this excludes heaviness and lightness from sensible qualities. Yet the Sheikh elsewhere says in *The Healing* that they belong to this group. In another passage, he says that there is no proof that something moist makes another thing moist, or that something dry makes another thing dry. I will answer both objections later. This is also the point of his description of qualities specific to quantity as features that belong to a body because of its quantity.

Al-Razi says that this definition leaves out qualities specific to number, since number can be considered in things that are separate from matter. He uses this to challenge the claim that mathematics studies things that are free from matter in thought but not in reality. Number is one of the subjects of mathematics, and it can exist without matter in reality too. I say that both objections will be answered when we discuss how the world began. Number belongs neither directly nor indirectly to separate intellects. It belongs to souls through their bodies. Someone might answer that the numbers studied in mathematics can also depend on matter in reality, when they are used for practical tasks such as measuring, adding, separating, multiplying, and dividing. But this answer also leaves questions.

Some say that “qualities related to bodies” means qualities that concern bodies in some way without being exclusive to them. Qualities of number can be understood that way too. The problem is that then “qualities of the soul do not relate to bodies” would mean that they have no relation to bodies at all, which is not so.[^V04-29] The point is that they are not tied specifically to bodies in a way that would make them independent of souls.

The four ways of dividing quality into kinds are these.

The first is al-Razi's. A quality either belongs specifically to quantity, as roundness, squareness, and being even or odd do[^V04-30], or it does not. If it does not, it is either sensible or not. A sensible quality that fades quickly, like blushing from embarrassment, is called a short-lived sensory effect; one that lasts is called a lasting sensory quality. If a quality is not sensible, it is either a readiness for completion or not. If it is a readiness not to be affected, it is called a capacity; if it is a strong readiness to be affected, it is called a natural disposition. If it is not a readiness, it is called a passing state when it fades quickly, as anger does in a patient person, and a settled trait when it changes slowly, as patience does.[^V04-31]

This is al-Razi's division. But there could be a bodily quality that is not specific to quantity, not sensible, and not itself a readiness. So the claim that any quality which is a perfection must belong to the soul is unsupported, except by induction.

The second division is this: a quality either produces an effect by making something else resemble it, as heat makes something hot and blackness casts its likeness into the eye, or it does not. If it does not, it either belongs to quantity as quantity, or it does not. If it does not, it belongs either to bodies as natural things or to souls as things with a soul.

The third division asks whether a quality is tied to the existence of a soul. If it is, it belongs to the soul. If not, it either relates to quantity or does not. If it does not, it is either a readiness or an actuality. These four branches are, in order: temporary states and established traits, qualities specific to quantity, capacities and their absence, and sensible affections.

The fourth division asks whether a quality acts by making something else resemble it. If so, it is a sensible affection. If not, it either has no relation to bodies, as with temporary states and established traits, or it does. If it does, it relates either to their quantity or to their nature. The first gives qualities specific to quantity; the second, capacities and their absence.

The last three divisions are the Sheikh's ways of explaining the four kinds in *The Healing*. They are all weak and much alike.

### Section One: Sensible Qualities (in five books)

### Book One: General Rules for This Group

#### I. Its Shared Feature, Its Two Kinds, and Their Names

The common feature of every sensible quality is that it acts on what receives it in a way that produces something similar to itself: heat makes another thing hot, cold makes another thing cold, and colors imprint their images on sight.

Some object that this feature does not apply to every sensible quality. Heaviness and lightness do not seem to make other things heavy or light. The Sheikh also says, in his discussion of the elements in *The Healing*, that there is no proof that something moist makes another thing moist, or something dry makes another thing dry. If so, these sensible qualities do not produce their own likeness.

I say that they produce likenesses of themselves in the senses, as do heaviness and lightness. So the feature applies to all sensible qualities. When the Sheikh says that blackness casts its image into the eye, unlike heaviness, which moves a body instead of making it heavy, he means that blackness acts on the power of sight by producing its likeness, whereas heaviness acts on a body by moving it. He is distinguishing the effect on the organ of perception from the effect on a body. He is not saying that heaviness and lightness are not sensible qualities.

Likewise, when he says that there is no proof that moisture and dryness make other things moist or dry, he means that we cannot prove that they produce their likeness in other materials—not that they fail to do so in the powers of sense. He has said in many places that every sensible thing has a corresponding form in the sense. This applies to colors seen by sight and to qualities felt by touch, such as heaviness and lightness. This is the answer promised in the previous chapter.

The features that do not apply to every sensible quality are numerous, so there is no need to list them.

As for the two kinds: if a sensible quality is lasting, as with the yellow color of gold or the sweetness of honey, it is called a lasting sensory quality. It is called this because the senses are first affected by it, and because it prevents the natural mixture that would otherwise arise when the elements interact within matter. This prevention can be specific, as with qualities of mixtures such as the sweetness of honey, or general, as with qualities of simple substances such as the heat of fire. Heat, as a kind, may prevent the mixture that would result when matter and elements combine. Although the heat of fire does not arise from a change in its matter, the general quality of heat can arise through change in some material. Honey's sweetness does not arise from a change in the honey itself, but it came about when the ingredients that became honey were changed and took on sweetness.

If a sensible quality does not last, it is called a short-lived sensory effect because it fades quickly and seems to be a temporary reaction. It belongs in the first group for the two reasons just given. But since it lasts only briefly and disappears quickly, it is not given the name of the lasting kind; it is simply called a short-lived sensory effect.

#### II. Are Bodily Qualities Just Shapes or Mixtures?

Some argue that sensible qualities have no reality of their own. They are only reactions that occur in the senses. When asked why a sense responds to some things and not others—why sight responds differently to transparent things and colored things—they say that bodies are made up of particles that cannot actually be divided, though we can imagine dividing them. These particles have different shapes, arrangements, and positions, and those differences produce different effects in the senses. What spreads out our vision is called white; what gathers it in is called black. In food, the tiny particles of a sharp or pungent taste penetrate the tongue and seem to cut it in many directions; sweetness counteracts that cutting. They give the same kind of explanation for smells and for things we feel as hot or cold.

In short, they say that different shapes produce different sensations, and that the senses respond to shapes, not to any other qualities.

This view is easy to refute. The senses contain images of what they sense, and the image of a shape is not the same as the image of a taste or color. We will also show, in our discussion of coming-to-be and passing-away, that this view is false. For now, consider what distinguishes color from shape: shape can be felt by touch, but color cannot. So they are not the same thing.

They may reply that what is truly sensed is the image formed in the sense organ, and that one particular shape can produce one effect in sight and another in touch. We answer: if the effects in the senses are shapes, then, since shape can be touched, the effect formed in the eye must also be touchable. That is absurd. If these effects are not shapes, then qualities beyond shapes do exist. We can also point out that colors, tastes, and smells have opposites, while shapes do not.

Here is another argument. They say that we can perceive a shape only by perceiving its color. If color were itself a shape, then perception would depend on itself. They also say that one person can see the same body in two different colors from two different positions. A pigeon’s neck, for example, may look gray from one angle and golden from another. Sugar tastes bitter in the mouth of someone with jaundice but sweet to someone else. They conclude that these qualities have no reality beyond the senses’ reactions, and that differing sensations come from differences in the sense organs and their reactions, depending on position.

Our answer is that, in the case of the pigeon’s neck, the thing being seen is not one uniform surface. The tips of the feathers face in different directions, and each has its own color; from a given position, one may hide another. Position matters to sight because sight requires the viewer to face what is seen. But sensible qualities are not merely reactions in the senses, and differences in sensation cannot be explained only by differences in the organs and their reactions.

Some people also claim that qualities are nothing but mixtures. They say that when a mixture reaches one condition, it produces a particular color or taste; in another condition, it produces a different one. The other qualities of this kind are likewise not separate from mixtures: each is simply a particular mixture that affects touch in one way and sight in another.

This view is false. Every mixture, as long as it lies between its extreme limits, can be felt by touch. But no color can be felt by touch. So no mixture is a color. Also, colors and other sensible qualities can reach opposite extremes, while mixtures remain between extremes. They are therefore different things.

### Book Two: Qualities We Feel by Touch

These include heat and cold, wetness and dryness, fineness and coarseness, stickiness and brittleness, dampness and dryness, and heaviness and lightness. Roughness and smoothness, hardness and softness are also included here. We will discuss each of them in this book.

#### I. What Heat and Cold Are

In *The Healing*, Avicenna defines heat as a quality that separates things joined together and brings like parts together. Cold, he says, brings both like and unlike parts together. In his *Definitions*, he describes heat as an active quality that moves a thing upward by making it lighter. As a result, it brings like parts together and separates unlike ones. This second definition is better: joining and separating are not heat’s first effects. They follow from its primary effect, which is to make a thing lighter and tend upward.

Once heat has its primary effect, it can produce different results depending on what it acts on. It thins dense material, making its consistency less thick; the opposite effect is thickening. It also raises the finer particles. As they rise, they leave behind particles of a different kind, while particles of the same kind naturally come together. Heat is called active because it makes something else act in these ways, not simply because it has some effect—of any kind—on it. Heat’s primary feature is to make a thing lighter and give it an upward tendency. From that, depending on the material, come secondary effects such as joining, separating, and evaporation.

If the thing affected is a simple element, it first changes in quality. That change may then lead to a change in what it is: water becomes air, and air becomes fire. Heat may also separate unlike parts, such as fiery particles from airy ones, carrying along the tiny particles of water mixed with them.

If the thing affected is a compound and its ingredients are not tightly bound, the finer parts rise more easily. This separates unlike ingredients, while like parts come together according to their nature. If the ingredients are tightly bound, and the finer and denser parts are nearly balanced, strong heat may set them moving in a circle: the finer parts rise, then the denser parts pull them down again. If the finer parts predominate, the whole substance rises, as with sal ammoniac. If the denser parts predominate, the result depends on how strongly they predominate.

If they do not predominate too strongly, the substance may flow, as lead does, or soften, as iron does. If the denser parts predominate greatly, as in talc, heat warms the substance but does not make it flow; other methods are needed to soften it. When these effects are blocked, that does not show that heat lacks its natural power to raise fine parts, separate unlike ones, and bring like ones together.

This answers the objections raised against heat’s effects. Some say that heat does not bring like parts together: it makes water rise and separates it, and it turns wood to ash and scatters it. Others say that heat cannot separate unlike parts in talc, lime, iron, gold, or the salamander. They also point out that heat can make egg white and yolk stick together, rather than separating them.

We answer that heat does not separate water in the way claimed. When it changes some of the water into air, the air and water separate because they have different natures. Tiny particles of water are then carried upward with the air as vapor. In wood, the earthy particles are held together by the watery ones. When heat separates them, the dry particles remain as ash.

With the help of techniques used by alchemists, fire can melt talc, lime, and iron, especially when aided by substances that make it burn more strongly, such as sulfur and arsenic. Gold resists separation because its ingredients are very tightly joined. Whenever some part tends to rise, the part tending downward holds it back, and a circular movement results. If that obstacle were absent, the fire would separate it. A cause’s failure to act when blocked does not show that it lacks the power to act.

As for the egg, heat does not bring the white and yolk together; it changes their consistency. Fire can soon separate them by distillation.

Definitions like these for sensible qualities are neither strict definitions nor descriptions that let the mind infer what a thing is from an inseparable feature. They list qualities and effects so that the mind can distinguish one thing from another after the senses have shown us what it can show. Someone who has never seen fire, for example, will not infer from the idea of upward movement, or of like parts joining and unlike parts separating, that heat is what causes those effects. The point of these descriptions is to list the features by which the mind can recognize each quality once the senses have supplied the experience.

Some ancient thinkers denied that cold exists and treated it as the absence of heat. The reply is that freezing and thickening are positive actions, just as flowing and thinning are. A positive action cannot come from nonexistence or from body in general. So there must be two real qualities that produce these four opposite actions.

We might interpret the ancient view more charitably: heat exists more strongly than cold, and cold is a weaker form of existence. Heat’s action also seems more clearly positive than cold’s, since movement seems more active than rest or freezing. Heat may even be a heavenly substance, like the influence that flows from the world of souls into bodies and produces effects unlike those of ordinary, bodily heat.

#### II. The Nature of Innate Heat and Whether It Exists

It may seem that we use the word “heat” in entirely different senses when we speak of the heat of fire, the heat that flows from the stars and other heavenly bodies, the innate heat that flows from the realm of souls, and the heat caused by movement. But the word has one meaning: the sensible quality that thins and raises things. The different kinds of heat may still differ in their real nature.

The uncertainty is about whether “hot” means the same thing when applied to fire, heavenly lights, nature acting in a body, and medicines or foods that cause heat in an animal’s body. Does each of these contain the same sensible quality found in fire? Or do we use “hot” more loosely for something that produces heat, even though the quality of heat is not present in it? The question is difficult.

The right answer is this: if something produces heat directly, then it is hot. We know powers by what they do, and an effect is of the same kind as its cause. But if something produces heat only indirectly—for example, by blocking pores, gathering heat, or preventing it from spreading—then it is not itself hot. Philosophers also disagree about whether the innate heat that sustains life in animals and plants is different in kind from heat outside the body.

Avicenna says in *The Canon of Medicine* that when external heat tries to upset the body’s balance, innate heat resists it more strongly than anything else. Even hot poisons can be countered only by innate heat. This heat serves nature as an instrument: it moves the spirit so that it can drive out the harm caused by external heat. It also counters the harm of external cold by bringing the body together and tightening it. Cold has no such power. It only opposes incoming heat; it does not oppose incoming cold. Innate heat protects the body’s natural moisture from being overwhelmed by outside heat. All the body’s powers depend on innate heat, while cold works against them. That is why we speak of innate heat but not innate cold.

In *The Healing*, in his discussion of animals, Avicenna reports Aristotle’s view that the subtle heat by which the body is joined to the soul is not the elemental heat of fire. It is like the heat that flows from heavenly bodies. A balanced mixture resembles the nature of the heavens because it flows from them. We can see the difference between heavenly and elemental heat in the way the sun’s warmth can help the eye of someone with night blindness, while the warmth of fire cannot. Life follows the heavenly heat, not the heat of fire. Through this heat, the spirit becomes a “divine body.” Its relation to semen and the organs is like the intellect’s relation to the mental powers: the intellect is the highest of immaterial beings, and the spirit is the finest of bodies.

Al-Razi argues that innate heat is the heat of fire mixed with the other elements. When fire mixes with them, its heat cooks and balances the compound and helps it hold together. The elements interact and reduce the force of the fire: too much heat would destroy the body’s balance, while too little could not cook it and produce balance. This is the heat called innate. It resists outside heat because outside heat tries to separate the body’s parts, while innate heat has cooked and matured them so that they are harder to separate. On this account, the difference between the two kinds of heat is only that one is inside the body and the other comes from outside.

I say that al-Razi’s account would mean that, when fire is weakened and subdued, it performs actions it cannot perform when it is at full strength. That is impossible. There is also a difference between not being affected by something harmful and driving away harm that has already entered. Innate heat drives out external heat after it has entered the body and caused illness. Yet the elemental heat in the body grows stronger when another heat enters it—as when someone is burned by fire, exposed to hot air, or feverish.

What, then, resists the increased heat, drives it out, treats the body, and restores its health after it has begun to break down? Is it the elemental fire, now weakened and subdued, assuming it has not been destroyed like other elemental forms? Is it nothing at all? Is it cold, whose only action is to remain at rest? Or is it the soul acting alone, even though the soul works only through powers and qualities?

It is far from the truth to say that growth, nourishment, and reproduction—with all their ordered processes and adjustments—come from heat, whose nature is to burn and separate. Like the other elements, elemental heat tends by nature to pull apart. It is held together by force. What brings it together and keeps it from separating is innate heat, acting under the direction of the soul or nature. We have established elsewhere that natural actions require one of the four qualities, especially heat.

Anyone who looks carefully will see that heat relates to nature as an impulse relates to a power of movement. Just as the nature of fire is hot, the source of innate heat—whether soul, nature, or a heavenly or elemental principle—must itself be hot by its own nature, not through some extra warmth. Its own essence is a higher kind of heat. In truth, it is another, more exalted fire, which surrounds ordinary kinds of fire not merely by occupying a place around them, as the heavens surround the sphere of fire, but by ruling over them. It cannot be felt by the senses.

For us, nature and soul are forms of innate heat, and they have many levels. The spirit that carries the soul through this world is a fiery substance, but it is not, as most people think, a mixture of the elements. It belongs to the same order as the heavenly bodies. Because it is alive in itself, it cannot die or grow cold. It may cease to exist, but that is not the same as dying. There is a difference between corruption and simple nonexistence, just as there is a difference between existence and coming into being. These questions are beyond the understanding of many independent thinkers, let alone those who merely follow others.

#### III. The Nature of Wetness and Dryness, and Whether They Exist

Some early thinkers say that a body is wet when it sticks to whatever it touches. Avicenna rejects this: if wetness meant sticking, then the body that sticks most would be the wettest. But honey does not stick more than water. Avicenna says that wetness is the quality that makes a body easily take on the shape of something around it and easily release that shape. Dryness makes both harder.

Al-Razi says that what matters is how easily a body sticks to something else, and that easy sticking entails easy separation. Wetness, on his account, is a bodily readiness to stick easily to something else and separate from it easily. He does not accept that honey sticks more than water. If “more sticky” means “sticks more easily,” then wetter things do stick more easily than water. If it means stronger, more extensive, or longer-lasting adhesion, then he is not defining wetness as lasting adhesion, so it does not follow that the body that sticks longest is the wettest.

Wetness is not adhesion itself. Adhesion is a relation; wetness is a quality. Rather, wetness is what prepares a body to adhere, and it necessarily allows it to separate easily too, the opposite of difficult separation.

I say that Avicenna’s objection is not really directed at defining wetness as ease of adhesion and separation, as his words might suggest; he gives this definition in some of his books. His point is that the earlier account of wetness says nothing about ease or separation. And it is not certain that easy adhesion always entails easy separation.

One objection to defining wetness by ease of adhesion is that very dry, crushed bone—such as burned bone powder—can stick easily. By that definition it would count as wet. The reply is that this may be due to the large amount of air mixed into it. This answer works only for those who call air wet because it sticks easily—in other words, who call it damp. It does not work for those who deny that air is damp.

There are also objections to defining wetness as ease in taking on shapes. Fire is the finest and lightest of the elements, and seems to take on shapes most easily; so it would have to be the wettest. The reply is that pure fire does not easily take on unfamiliar shapes. What we see as fire is mixed with air, and that mixture takes on shapes easily.

Someone may object that if an oven is kept burning for a month or two, the air inside should turn into pure fire, while its readiness to take on shapes remains the same. We answer that even if the oven burned for a thousand years, air would still be mixed with it.

Another objection: by this definition, air would be wet. But it is agreed that mixing wet with dry makes it hold together and keeps it from scattering, while mixing air with fire does not do this. So air cannot be wet. The answer is that this argument uses “wet” to mean damp, a common use of the word, rather than wet in the sense of easily taking on a shape.

A further problem is that if dryness means difficulty in taking on shapes, it would be the same as hardness. Fire would also have to be hard because it is dry. The reply is that softness is a quality that lets a body be pressed inward while remaining coherent and not flowing apart; hardness is the opposite. In this respect, softness and hardness differ from wetness and dryness, though it seems that the ability or inability to be pressed inward may ultimately depend on wetness and dryness.

The best definition, in my view, is that wetness is a quality that makes adhesion and separation easy. As for dryness, one account says that it is the quality that makes a body break apart easily but hard to bring back together. Some bodies break apart easily because their parts do not hold together well; others do so because they consist of tiny hard parts. The first is dryness, the second brittleness. Dryness is therefore a quality that makes a body easy to break apart but hard to reunite. This distinguishes it from brittleness and from hardness.

We have already indicated that wetness is best understood as the quality that makes a body stick easily and release easily. If that is what it is, wetness is certainly a real quality, and one we can sense. The same is true of dryness, for the reasons already given.

If, instead, wetness means only that a body easily takes on shapes, this is figurative language when taken literally. “Easy” and “difficult” describe relations, while wetness and dryness are not relations. The point is that a wet body has nothing in its nature that prevents it from taking on an unfamiliar shape or releasing it; a dry body has something in its nature that resists this, though it can still take on shapes. On this account, they are opposites like absence and possession: wetness is not a positive quality, nor is it directly sensed. What we sense is simply that there is no obstacle to the body’s taking on a shape.

This is supported by another point. Whether we define wetness as readiness to take on shapes or as the cause of that readiness, and whether that readiness is positive or negative, it does not have to be a quality added to the body. If it is simply a readiness, and that readiness is negative, this is clear. If it is a positive readiness, the body has it by its own nature, since the body has this readiness along with all its other capacities. That is why even a dry body can take on shapes. If wetness is the cause of the readiness, and the readiness is positive, there is even less need to posit an extra quality: the body’s ability to take on shapes is already a feature of the body itself.

So on this account, wetness is not a real quality. It is also probably not directly sensed. That is why we do not feel the air around us when it is balanced—not hot, cold, or moving. If the air’s wetness were a positive quality, we would feel it all the time and would never think the space around us completely empty.[^V04-32]

Avicenna seems to say in his chapter on the elements that wetness cannot be sensed, while in *The Psychology* he says that it can. One scholar suggests that by “unsensed” Avicenna means wetness as ease in taking on shapes, and by “sensed” he means ease of adhesion—in other words, dampness. This is a good explanation, though God knows best.

#### IV. Fineness, Coarseness, Stickiness, Brittleness, Dampness, and Dryness

“Fine” can mean thin in consistency, as with water and air, or easy to divide into very small parts. Coarseness has two opposite meanings. In the natural philosophy of *The Healing*, Avicenna says that rarefaction resembles fineness in the first sense, but adds something more: it makes a body thinner while increasing its volume. Thinness also implies greater volume, but rarefaction includes it in its meaning, while thinness only entails it. Rarefaction can also mean that a body’s parts move apart, leaving spaces that finer material fills. We are not concerned with this last meaning here.

Avicenna then says that fineness and rarefaction in the first sense have no direct effect on action or being acted upon. In *The Categories*, he uses rarefaction to mean fluffing something up, as with wool; a change that makes a body easier to cut or shape; or matter’s ability to take up a larger volume. The first is a matter of position, the second of quality, and the third a relation involving quantity. Condensation has three corresponding opposite meanings.

An objection is raised: fineness and rarefaction in the first sense are the same as thinness, which Avicenna defines here as ease in cutting and shaping. Yet he says elsewhere that they have no direct effect on action or being acted upon. That would mean wetness—defined as ease in taking on shapes—has no direct effect either, even though he wants to show that it does. It is better to say that ease in taking on shapes is thinness or fineness, while ease in adhering to and separating from something else is wetness, which does affect how bodies act and are acted upon. Coarseness is difficulty in taking on shapes.

Stickiness is a mixed quality, not a simple one. A sticky thing can easily be shaped in any way, but is difficult to pull apart; it stretches while remaining joined. It combines wetness and dryness in a closely bound mixture. A brittle thing is the opposite: it is difficult to shape but easy to break apart, because it has much dryness, little wetness, and a weak mixture.

There are three related words here: “wet,” “damp,” and “soaked.” A wet body has a natural form that calls for wetness, meaning that it adheres and separates easily. A damp body gains moisture by contact with something wet. If the moisture has penetrated inside it, the body is soaked. Dryness is the opposite of dampness.

#### V. Heaviness and Lightness

This chapter raises several questions about heaviness, lightness, and the impulses that move bodies.

First, Avicenna says in his *Definitions* that an impulse is a quality by which a body pushes against whatever keeps it from moving. This shows that the impulse is the source of the push, not the push itself. It is also different from motion. A heavy object held still in the air and an inflated skin held still under water both have a felt tendency: one downward, the other upward. An impulse is not the body's nature either. It may come from the soul, it can be absent when a body is in its natural place, and it can grow stronger or weaker while the body's nature stays the same.

Second, someone who says an impulse is something distinct from the actual push can point to a ring pulled equally in opposite directions until it stops in the middle. Each pull acts on it and tends to move it, even though no movement or push results. This impulse cannot be the body's nature, since nature acts toward up or down, while the two pulls act in other directions. So the source of this tendency is neither nature nor a power of the soul.

Third, Avicenna defines heaviness as a natural power by which a body moves toward the center, and lightness as a natural power by which it moves away from the center. “Center” here does not simply mean the geometric center. It is the place where the center of a heavy body—or its center of gravity—coincides with the center of the world. A body’s center of gravity is the point on either side of which the weights balance: the weight on one side equals the weight on the other.

The phrase “by nature” is not redundant, as some have claimed, nor does it describe the center. It distinguishes natural motion from forced motion. The center meant here is the natural center, the center of the first body that sets directions, as al-Razi argues; a heavy body does not move toward the centers of the other spheres. “By nature” describes the motion: even forced motion is caused by the nature within the body being moved. This rules out a heavy body pushed upward and a light body thrown downward. And since Avicenna calls heaviness and lightness “powers,” impulse is distinct from nature, whether impulse means the push itself or what produces it.

Fourth, impulses are natural, forced, or caused by the soul. Natural impulses go in one of two real directions: heaviness moves downward, and lightness upward. Forced impulses go against the natural direction. Impulses caused by the soul may be circular or straight, and may change with a person’s wishes.

Fifth, a body has no active natural impulse while it is in its natural place. Al-Razi says Avicenna states this in *The Healing*, in his discussion of the heavens and the world, without even a likely argument, let alone a proof. I say it is clear as soon as we understand what an impulse is, what produces it, and what happens when nothing blocks it.

Sixth, “impulse” may mean the push itself, or its nearest cause. That cause comes from nature when a body has left its natural place and needs to return, or from the soul, which uses nature to act on the body when it wills something. Just as a body cannot have two actual motions in opposite directions, it cannot have two actual impulses in opposite directions. One motion brings a body nearer to one place and farther from the place in the opposite direction. Two such motions would make the same body move toward and away from each place at once.

This resolves a contradiction al-Razi finds between two passages in *The Healing*.

In one, Avicenna says there is a moment of rest between any two motions and rejects the claim that two impulses can come together: how could something be actively pushing in one direction and moving away from it at the same time? A stone thrown upward, he says, does not also have an active downward impulse; it only has a source that would produce one if the obstacle were removed. In another passage, Avicenna says forced motion comes from a power the body receives from what moves it. That power lasts until friction with surrounding things wears it away. As it weakens, the natural impulse grows stronger. This may sound as if natural and forced impulses exist together. But “natural impulse” here means the body's capacity to produce a push, not an active impulse alongside the forced one. A ring held still by two equal pulls has a distant readiness for both pushes, but neither push is actually present, nor is either one’s nearest cause.

Seventh, impulses can be directed not only toward places, but also toward changes in quality, size, arrangement, or even what a thing is. Since an impulse is the nearest cause of motion, it has the same range of kinds as motion. Some impulses arise from a body’s own nature; others from the soul; others from an outside force. A stone tends downward by nature. A plant tends to grow in size. A grape changes in quality, from sour or bitter to sweet, and in color, from green to yellow. An animal moves in the direction it chooses. An arrow receives an impulse from the bow when it leaves it.

In our view, nature is the source of all these impulses, whether it acts on its own, under the soul’s direction, or under an outside force. But only impulses toward places can be felt directly. Bodies also differ in how much they resist a forced impulse. Some differences come from their natural strength: a body with a stronger natural impulse resists forced movement more than one with a weaker impulse. Other differences come from circumstances. An outside force may have little effect on a tiny grain of sand, or may be unable to overcome an obstacle. A loosely packed body, like a feather, lets obstacles interfere with it more easily.

Eighth, one body can have two motions in different directions if one is direct and the other comes along with it. A person riding in a moving boat moves directly by walking, and also moves with the boat. In the same way, a stone carried by someone who is walking has its own downward impulse from its weight and also moves forward with the carrier. The forward motion belongs directly to the carrier and only indirectly to the stone. But a body cannot have two opposite impulses at once if one is natural and the other forced, just as it cannot have two opposite motions of those kinds.

An impulse can grow stronger or weaker. A natural impulse changes with the body’s size, density, and arrangement. A larger body has a stronger impulse than a smaller one; a denser body tends downward more strongly than a thinner one, while the reverse is true of upward movement. The arrangement of the parts matters too: tightly packed parts can produce a different impulse from scattered ones. External conditions can also change the result, such as how thick or thin the path is. A forced impulse changes for the reasons already given.

Now suppose a forced impulse acts on a body that already has a natural impulse. The two causes—the outside force and the body's nature—resist each other. If the outside force wins and overcomes the body's nature, a forced impulse appears and the natural one disappears. Then nature and outside resistance gradually wear down the forced impulse. As it weakens, the body's natural power grows stronger until it matches what remains of the forced impulse. The body then has no impulse. After that, nature produces its own impulse again. It grows stronger as the remaining effects of the forced movement fade. The body's changing impulse is much like the changing balance between opposite qualities.

Avicenna compares this to a stone thrown upward and then falling back down. He also compares it to water heated by an outside source. The heat first overcomes the water’s natural coldness. As outside causes slowly remove the heat, the water’s natural coldness returns. The water is never both hot and cold at once. It always has one temperature between the extremes: at one point it leans toward heat and is called hot; at another it leans toward cold and is called cold; in the middle it is called neither. This change comes from the interaction between the outside heat and the water’s nature, along with other conditions, such as cool air around it. In the same way, a body has only one impulse at a time, strong or weak, natural or forced. When the natural and forced causes balance, the impulse seems to disappear altogether.

There is one difference between impulse and heat or cold. A body can have no impulse: it has none when it is in its natural place, while it rests between a forced upward motion and a natural downward motion, or when a ring is held equally from both sides. But water cannot be free of every degree of heat and cold. Some opposites allow the thing that can have them to be free of both; others do not.

Ninth, can a body have two impulses toward the same place, one natural and one coming from outside? For a perfect body created at its fullest level, with nothing blocking its natural impulse—such as the celestial spheres in their turning, or the elements in their motion if we imagine them outside their natural places in an empty world—the answer is no. The principle that the highest possible level comes first shows that their natural powers are already at their limit. No outside force can add to them. With other bodies, however, an outside force may help a natural impulse that is being resisted. Air resists a falling stone, for example, so an extra push in the same direction could make it fall faster.

Tenth, the Mu‘tazilite thinkers call an impulse a “pressure” and divide it into a lasting kind, such as a heavy body’s downward pressure or a light body’s upward pressure, and changing kinds that go against nature. Some say there is only one pressure in a body, called heaviness when considered toward the ground and lightness when considered upward; toward other directions it has no special name. Others say there are several opposite pressures, but that one body cannot have two at once.

Al-Jubba’i says that no pressure, whether lasting or changing, remains in the body. Abu Hashim says that a lasting one remains, as we see with colors and tastes. Al-Jubba’i says pressure does not produce motion or rest; motion produces both. If someone opens a door or throws a stone, the key or stone cannot move until the person’s hand moves. That movement then produces further movement in the key or stone, and finally produces rest when it reaches its goal. Abu Hashim says that pressure produces movement and rest.

Al-Jubba’i also argues that the thrower’s hand moves after the thrown stone. Before the stone leaves its place, he says, the hand cannot move into that space because two bodies cannot occupy the same place. This is a weak argument. If “after” means later in time, the fact that two bodies cannot overlap does not prove that their motions occur at different times; one can move out as the other moves in, as with the parts of a turning ring. In fact, if they did not move at the same time, the hand and stone would become separated. If “after” means later in the order of cause, the order is the reverse: the hand must move before the stone.

A better view for those who say one thing produces another is that either motion or pressure can produce movement and rest. Pressure produces some things directly, without any further condition, such as movement, for which it is the nearest cause. It produces other things only when further conditions are met: different positions when the body moves; return to a natural place if the body has left it; pain if it causes a body’s parts to separate; and sound if it causes bodies to strike one another.

This is the Mu‘tazilite account of impulse. According to the philosophers’ principles, the impulse does not remain in the cases we have discussed. It can grow stronger or weaker, and a change in strength is a change in the impulse itself. So a particular impulse does not have to remain in each body. What remains in each individual body is the basic nature that makes it the kind of body it is.

Some scholars argue that an impulse remains until a body reaches its goal: the impulse causes the arrival, and the cause cannot disappear before its effect. This is right only up to the instant of arrival. It does not show that the impulse lasts beyond that instant, any more than it shows that the motion lasts. The impulse and the motion are caused by the body’s being away from the place it naturally seeks, is forced toward, or is directed toward by choice. Once the body reaches that place, the cause is gone. The impulse cannot continue to move it toward a place it has already reached.

Eleventh, an impulse is a settled condition, even though some impulses arise gradually. It is not like motion, which must happen over time because it concerns things with parts, such as place or size. An impulse must exist at the instant a body reaches a goal that has no parts. Anything present at such an indivisible point is present in an instant, even if it also lasts before or after it.

Twelfth, heaviness and lightness do not act on each other. Heaviness makes a body move toward the center; lightness makes it move away from the center. Each makes its body move away from the other, so they cannot meet and interact.

As we have seen, the pause between a forced upward motion and a natural downward motion comes from the interaction of nature with the forced upward impulse, not from two opposite impulses acting together. They do not coexist. During the pause, both impulses are absent. Only at the edges do they appear: at the first instant of the pause, the natural force finishes overcoming the forced impulse; at the last instant, the body's natural impulse begins again, once the effects that blocked it have worn away.

#### VI. Qualities Often Mistaken for Qualities of Touch

Roughness, smoothness, hardness, and softness are often mistaken for qualities we feel by touch because people do not distinguish what belongs to a thing in itself from what comes along with it. Roughness means that a body's parts are unevenly arranged; smoothness means they are even. These are conditions of arrangement. But what we feel is not arrangement alone. We also feel hardness or softness, heat or cold, and other qualities, so roughness and smoothness do not belong to this group of qualities.

Softness has two features: it can be pressed inward, which is a kind of movement, and its surface can become hollow. The latter belongs to qualities tied to size and shape. But softness is not either of these features. It can exist without them, and they can be seen, while softness cannot be seen. Softness is a body's full readiness to give way when pressed. Hardness also involves several things: not giving way, which is an absence; resisting pressure, which we can feel; and keeping its shape. Hardness is none of these by itself.

The absence of yielding is not a quality. Keeping a shape is a matter we have already explained. And resistance alone cannot be hardness: if it were, the air inside an inflated skin would be hard, as would gusts of wind. Hardness is instead a natural readiness not to be changed by pressure. Readiness and lack of readiness are not things we sense directly, let alone by touch. So hardness and softness belong to the class of qualities that describe what a body can or cannot readily undergo.

The other qualities that result from mixing the four basic tactile qualities—heat, cold, wetness, and dryness—belong in another discussion, when we examine natural bodies. Taste would fit after touch, since taste follows it, but we have postponed that short discussion. Here we have tried to follow the qualities we see, asking the source of all good and generosity to help us gain knowledge and deepen our understanding.

### Book Three: Qualities We See

#### I. Establishing That Colors Are Real

Some people say that color is not real, and that all colors are illusions, as with the rainbow, halos, and similar things.
