On their account, white appears when air mixes with tiny transparent particles. The many surfaces that face one another reflect light from one to another. This is how snow looks white: the only cause is air mixed with transparent particles, with light passing through tiny frozen parts and reflecting many times. The same explanation is given for foam on water, powdered crystal, and clear glass.

Black, they say, comes from light failing to penetrate a dense body whose parts are packed closely together. In short, white comes down to light and black to darkness; the other colors are imagined effects of different mixtures of transparent material and air. Some also explain black by water pushing out the air, so that light cannot reach the body's surfaces fully. That is why wet cloth tends to look dark.

Those who study the subject carefully hold that colors are real qualities, not mere illusions, though they can be imagined in some cases. Their seeming to arise from the causes just described does not show that they are unreal; colors can also arise when matter changes in other ways.

Avicenna says in *The Healing* that he does not know whether white can arise in any way other than the one described. But in the third part of *The Psychology*, he firmly says that it can. He gives several examples. First, boiled eggs become whiter even though the fire has not made them more porous or filled them with air; it has removed air, which is why they become heavier. Second, a preparation called “milk of the virgin” is made by cooking vinegar with lead oxide until it dissolves, then filtering it until the vinegar is very clear. The lead oxide is cooked again in water used to boil potash, and filtered well. The two liquids are mixed, and the clear dissolved lead oxide settles out as a very white substance, like sour milk. It then dries while remaining white. Its whiteness cannot be caused by transparent particles spread apart with air mixed between them, or it would not dry and stay white. Likewise, plaster turns white when fired, not when ground or rubbed, even though grinding separates its parts and lets air enter them more clearly.

Third, colors follow different paths as they change from white to black. A color may turn gray and then brown before becoming black; another may turn red and then dark; another may pass through green and indigo. This suggests that colors have different causes. If all color came only from mixtures of white and black, there would be just one path between them, with differences only in how strong or weak the two are. Fifth, red, green, and other colors can be reflected. If they differed only because transparent material mixed with air, their reflection should always be white, since black is not reflected in experiments.

This summarizes what Avicenna says in *The Healing*. These last two arguments show more strongly that the different causes of color are not simply different mixtures of black and white. They are less conclusive about whether white must come from air mixed into transparent particles. There is room to question both arguments: black and white might combine in different ways, and black might be reflected when mixed with other colors rather than by itself. It is surprising that the author of *The Stations* misread Avicenna’s explanation of how whiteness appears in these cases as a denial that white really exists. Avicenna is explaining how white is seen in these particular cases, not denying the reality of white.

Some people deny that white is real but accept black. They argue that white can be stripped away, while black cannot. The reply is that if they mean black cannot be changed into another color, they are wrong: hair turns white as people age. If they mean black cannot be removed by staining, that is because black dyes contain a tightening power that lets them mix with and penetrate the material, while whitening agents do not penetrate in the same way. Alchemists are also said to change large amounts of copper by using calcined lead and sublimated arsenic, which would disprove their claim.

They may also argue that the material beneath white can take on other colors, but the material beneath black cannot. They say this shows that what lies beneath white has no color of its own. The reply is that the real color may be removed while an illusory appearance remains, because the cause of that illusion remains.

I say there is no doubt that a body cannot have a second color while it still has the first. This applies to white, black, and every other color. If they say that black cannot be removed from its material, that simply returns us to their first argument. Whether something can or cannot be removed is one of the weakest ways to prove whether it exists: real qualities can be removed, and things that are absent can remain, as blindness does in a person who cannot see.

Another argument says that the material beneath white can receive all colors, and anything that can receive a feature must be free of it. So the material beneath white must be free of every color. But the first claim is false: it can receive every color except the white it already has. If “receive” means being able to take on a color while remaining colored, the second claim is false too. A thing can receive a quality when it does not have it; it does not follow that it cannot have that quality at another time.

One further argument says that if something must lose a quality in order to receive it, then it could never have that quality. But this confuses a condition with an absolute rule. It only means that a thing must be without the quality while it is receiving it, which is true.

To complete the discussion: colors produced by artificial cooking, natural ripening, or any other change in matter are natural colors. Colors that appear at once in a place, even after movement, are not natural colors. These include rainbows, colors in glass, and colors in powdered transparent materials, such as crushed ice. Both kinds are real. One comes about when matter changes; the other comes from different forms of light and from the way light appears on different surfaces.

Among those who say black and white are real qualities, some hold that all other colors are mixtures of the two. They offer several weak arguments for this. Others say the basic colors are black, white, red, yellow, and green, and that the rest are mixtures. Observation shows that a particular mixture can produce a particular color. It does not show that this color can arise only from that mixture, or that it has no distinct nature of its own.

#### II. Visible Light

If “light” means what appears by itself and makes other things appear, then it is the same as existence—or existence itself. It is one simple reality, divided according to the different levels of existence. There is a light that exists through itself and rules over all else; there are also intellectual, soul, and bodily lights. God is the Light of Lights, with no limit to His intensity. All other lights have a limit, meaning that something brighter can be imagined above each of them. Some, such as intellectual lights, may have effects that reach without limit. All are glimmers of His light. Even dense bodies have light insofar as they exist, though their existence is mixed with the darkness of nonexistence and possibility, as we explained in our commentary on *The Wisdom of Illumination*.

If “light” means what lets bodies appear to sight, people disagree about what it is. Some say it is a quality we can perceive with the senses.

Others say that light is a physical substance. Those who say it is a quality should understand that it does not arise through a change in matter. It appears all at once, flowing from its source into a place able to receive it, either because a bright object faces that place or because the place shines by itself.

Those who say light is a physical substance should also accept that it is unlike ordinary material bodies, which can be changed by other things. If light were a body, it would not be made of matter with a built-in readiness to change. It would not have passive qualities such as wetness, dryness, heaviness, lightness, softness, or hardness. Nor would it have active qualities such as heat and cold, which produce motion, separation, joining, and similar effects. It would have to be a body that appears all at once, without changing or moving from place to place.

Still, those who say light is a body often claim that it consists of tiny particles that are emitted by a bright object and travel to the object that is lit. This is impossible. Most bright objects are stars and planets that shine continually. Their parts cannot keep breaking away, or they would shrink and lose their proper size. Nor can they continually replace what breaks off, because that would make them material bodies that constantly change and decay. That is impossible for the heavenly bodies.

The arguments usually given against treating visible light as a body are these. First, if light were a body that moved, its natural motion would have to be in one direction. But light falls on a body from every direction.

Second, light enters a room through a small opening. If we close the opening at once, the light particles must either remain or disappear. If they remain, the room should stay as bright as before. If they leave through the opening before it closes, that cannot explain why closing the opening stops them; the closure must already have cut off their path. If they do not remain, then one body passing between two others has caused one of them to disappear, which is plainly impossible.

Third, being light must either be the same as being a body or different from it. If the two are the same, the argument goes, that cannot be right: “light” means something different from “body.” We can imagine a dark body, but not a dark light. If instead these particles are bodies that carry the quality of light and travel from the bright object to the lit one, they must either be sensed or not. If they cannot be sensed, they should block what lies behind them, and the more they gather, the more they should block. But the opposite happens: the stronger the light, the more it reveals.

Fourth, when the sun rises, the whole face of the earth lights up at once. It is unlikely that particles travel from the sun’s sphere all the way to the earth in that brief moment, especially since the heavenly spheres cannot be broken through.

I say these arguments are very weak, as we have explained in our writings on *The Wisdom of Illumination*. The first argument fails because a body of light need not move or come into being through motion. It could appear all at once without moving.

The second argument also fails. A thing made without matter exists only because its maker continues to bring it into being and no barrier blocks that act. When a barrier appears, the source stops producing it, and it disappears at once. Since light did not depend on matter to exist, it does not need matter in order to cease existing. This is true whether light is a quality or a substance. The reason is that light in general does not come about through matter being changed and taking part in its formation, as most substances and qualities do. If a barrier stood between those things and their source, they would not disappear at once; they would take time and undergo change first.

The third argument can be answered by saying that different meanings do not prevent two things from being one in reality. The idea of existence differs from the idea of body, yet a body exists through its existence. The argument against light being a body would also rule out existence itself: we can think of existence and body as different ideas, and imagine a body that does not exist, but we cannot have an existing thing with no existence. In both cases, the solution is that light and body have different meanings, but different meanings can belong to one thing in reality, without multiplying its existence.

As for the fourth argument, it assumes that particles must separate and travel across a distance. It does not follow simply from light being a body.

#### III. What Light Is and Its Different Kinds

Light needs no definition, like other things we sense. Defining it as a quality that is the first completion of something transparent, or as a quality that lets us see without needing to see something else first, is to define it by something less clear. These descriptions are meant only to point out some of its features.

Even those who say light is a quality disagree about what it is. Some say it is simply color appearing.[^V04-33] On their account, full appearance is light, full hiddenness is darkness, and the state between them is shadow. These states differ in how close they are to full appearance or full hiddenness. When the senses have become used to a certain degree of darkness and then encounter something more visible, they imagine that it has a separate shine or beam. But that is not so; it is due to weakness in the senses. We can see some things that shine in the dark more clearly at night than during the day because sight is weaker in darkness.

They then claim that shine is an added quality. If a lamp makes our sight stronger, we no longer see the night-time shine. The lamp's shine disappears when the moon appears, and the moon's when the sun appears. They say all this comes from what was just described: weakness of sight. Some take this idea further and say that sunlight is nothing but the full appearance of the sun’s color. Its brightness overwhelms the eye, so its color seems hidden, though it is not hidden in itself. At night, we can see shining objects but not their colors because weak sight is overwhelmed by the strong appearance of those colors. During the day, sunlight strengthens sight, so the eye is no longer overcome and can see the colors. That is their view.

I say we must first be clear about what the disagreement is: is light a quality added to color, or is it color appearing? If those who say it is appearance mean only that it is a relation, their view is false. Light would then be only an idea in the mind, falling under the category of relation, and not something we sense. But sight is affected by light and can be harmed or even destroyed by too much of it. Ideas in the mind cannot have this effect. So light is a real feature that makes things visible. The question remains whether it is the same as color or different from it.

We discussed this in our notes on the *Principles of Illumination*, when answering the arguments made there, and reached this view: light as we sense it is a way in which a visible thing exists in the soul, present to it in another world. Outside the soul, light adds nothing to a thing beyond its color. The arguments used to show that light and color are different are these.

First, when a color appears, something new seems to happen. Is that new thing the color itself, or a quality related to it, or some other quality? If light is the new appearance of color, then either it is color itself or it is color coming into existence. If it is the coming-into-existence of color, a thing would be lit only at the instant its color appeared. If it is the color that has come into existence, light would be the same as color, leaving no meaning to the claim that light is color appearing. If, instead, they say light is a real quality added to color and call this “appearance,” they are only arguing over words. And if they say it is a new relation, that is wrong, because light is not a relation.

Second, white can be bright, and black can be bright too. If the light of each were identical to its color, some light would be the opposite of other light. That is impossible, since darkness is the opposite of light.

Third, colors can exist without light: black may not be lit, and the same is true of other colors. Light can also exist without color, as when it falls on water or crystal. So light and color must be different.

Fourth, when an illuminated red object reflects light toward another object, sometimes only the light is reflected; at other times, both light and color are reflected strongly enough to make the second object look red. If light were only color appearing, the first kind of reflection—a plain shine without color—could not occur. They may answer that the shine is simply the color appearing on the surface that receives it. But then why, when the color of the first object is very strong, does it hide and cancel the light on the second object and give it the first object's color?

I answer that the first argument is mistaken. The appearance of color is its existence, a real feature that can be related to the power that senses it. In this sense, it can arise at a particular time. Does this make light the same as color? Yes, but they differ in how we consider them, just as existence and a thing’s nature are one in reality but different in how we think of them. Light means a particular existence that belongs to some bodies. Darkness means the complete absence of this existence; shadow means its partial absence. Color is a mixture, in different ways, between what carries this light and what lacks it.

As I have already explained, the arguments against treating colors as mixtures of light and darkness are weak. So it is right to say that light is color appearing. Yet it is also right, in another sense, to distinguish light from color: light considered in itself is not mixed with absence or darkness, whereas colors differ according to such mixtures.

The second argument is also answered by what we have established. Even if colors are not different from light in reality, lights differ in strength. They can also differ through many kinds of mixture: different amounts of light and darkness, different degrees of possibility and actuality, strength and weakness, and what is basic or added. Most colors are material or tied to matter, and matter is where existence, absence, and possibility can be divided and mixed. So it is not surprising that the combinations of light and darkness produce the colors we see. Once they form in their proper places, light from another bright source falls on them.

Those who say that light is the same as color do not mean that every light is the same as every color. That would be like saying existence is the same as a thing’s nature, then claiming that every existence is the same as every nature. It would also imply that existence cannot come upon something that already exists, or that one existence cannot oppose another. Colors differ in how they behave, and some are opposites as colors, not as lights. In the same way, existing things differ, and some are opposites as natures, not as existences. Existence and nature are one in reality; so are light and color. This will be clear to anyone who follows our argument carefully.

The third argument is easily answered by what we have said. The fourth is also easy to answer with a little care. Color may fail to appear because the light on something is too weak, or because it is too strong. When light reflected from a colored, bright object reaches another object, it may show only light if the light or color is weak, or if the receiving object cannot take on the color. When both are strong, and the receiving object is ready for them, both light and color can appear. Reflection is a long subject. Sometimes a polished object has its own color and light, but what it reflects is only light from another bright source, depending on the position of the two objects. What appears in the receiving object is then only the light of that source, not the color and light of the polished object. If the receiving object is polished too, it may reflect an image of both, or of only one.[^V04-34]

#### IV. The Difference Between Radiance, Light, Rays, Glints, Shadow, and Darkness, and Why Colors Become Actual Only When There Is Light

Light that comes from a source by its own nature, rather than by receiving it from another, is called radiance. The sun gives radiance. Light that comes from another source, as the moon’s does, is called light. This follows the Qur'anic verse that says God made the sun a radiance and the moon a light: the sun gives off radiance, while the moon receives light.

A gleam is light that hides the color of the body shining. Gleams can also be direct or reflected. The direct kind is called a ray; the reflected kind, as in a mirror, is called a glint. Light received directly from a self-luminous source, such as the moon’s light or sunlight on the earth’s surface, may be called first light. If light comes from something that is itself lit by another source—for example, light on the earth before sunrise, or light entering a room after reflecting from air that faces the sun—it is second light, then third light, and so on, depending on how many things lie between it and the original source. These successive reflections are called first shadow, second shadow, and so on. Each shadow is one step farther from the source of light. When light has ended altogether, there is darkness. Darkness is an absence, not a quality. If we close our eyes, our condition is the same as when we open them in a dark place: we perceive nothing. So darkness cannot be a quality in the dark body. If a body had no light and no other quality or added capacity, its state would be exactly this darkness. Darkness is therefore a sheer lack.[^V04-35]

Avicenna and his followers say that colors do not actually exist while they are in darkness, because we do not see them there. Either they are absent, or something blocks us from seeing them. The second option is false: darkness is an absence, and air itself does not block sight. In a dark cave, for example, air fills the space, but once an object is lit we can see it through the same air. Someone may object that these are not the only options: perhaps the needed condition for sight is missing.

The reply is that if color is, by its nature, something seen by sight, then a healthy eye should see it. Otherwise it is not visible by nature. Someone may answer that color has its own nature and only has the capacity to be seen; light may be needed for that capacity to become real. A body has three stages: it can be ready to have a certain color; that color can exist in it; and it can be in a state where it can be seen. Why must light be needed for the color itself to exist? It may be needed only for the third stage, when the color can be seen.

I say the better approach is to connect this question to the earlier one: is color the same as light or something different? If color is one of light’s levels, then it does not exist in darkness. If it is different, it may exist in darkness, though we cannot see it because a condition for sight is missing.

#### 1. Addendum

Some think darkness is needed in order to see certain things, such as objects that shine at night. Avicenna denies this. Darkness cannot be a condition for seeing shining things, because a bright object can be seen whether the viewer is in darkness or in light. We see a fire in either setting. We cannot see the sun in darkness because when it rises, darkness is gone. Stars and other things that shine are seen at night but not by day because sunlight is stronger than their light. When sight is affected by strong light, it does not respond to weaker light. At night, no brighter light overwhelms theirs, so we see them. They are not invisible by day because darkness is needed to see them; the reason is simply that their light is overpowered. So darkness is not a condition for seeing these things.

### Book Four: Qualities We Hear

#### I. What Causes Sound

The nearest cause of sound is a wave or vibration in the air.[^V04-36] This wave is caused either by a forceful impact or by a forceful separation, as when a drum is struck or cotton is pulled apart. In either case, nearby air is driven forcefully to the sides—by the striker in one case, and by the spreading cotton in the other. The air farther away is then made to follow the shape and wave of the air nearby. This produces one collision after another, with a pause between them, until the wave reaches the air beside the ear.

Sound is not the wave itself, as some people think, nor is it the striking or pulling apart that others identify as sound. The wave can be felt by touch; when it is strong, it can hit or block the ear. Striking and pulling apart can be seen by sight, through color. But no sound is felt by touch or seen by sight. So the wave, the impact, and the separation are not themselves sound. We can also know that an impact, a separation, or a wave has occurred without knowing what sound it made; and we can hear a sound without knowing which of those three caused it. They are not the sound itself.

#### II. Showing That Sound Exists Outside the Ear

Someone may say that sound does not exist outside us. It is created in the sense of hearing when moving air touches the ear. They argue that when we hear a sound, we also hear where it comes from. But the direction of the sound cannot remain in the moving air beside the ear. If that were so, we should not be able to tell which direction a sound comes from. A hand, by contrast, feels only what it touches and cannot tell whether it came from the right or left. Hearing lets us tell direction, and also whether a sound is near or far. So we must hear sounds where they are, outside the ear.

The reply is that we sense direction because the air that strikes the ear comes from that direction. We tell near from far because a nearby impact is stronger and a distant one weaker.

The first answer fails because a sound may come from the listener’s right and still be heard by the left ear if the right ear is blocked; the listener can still tell its direction. The second answer also fails. If loudness alone told us distance, we could not tell the difference between a loud distant sound and a quiet nearby one. And when two sounds are equally far away but differ in loudness, we would have to think they were at different distances.

I say the difficulty remains. What is perceived must be present to the one perceiving it at the moment of perception, and what is present to a sense must come into contact with it. The shape of the sound wave and the air’s movement are present to the ear, but distance and direction are not. The truth is that the soul’s connection to the body also connects it to what touches the body, such as the air around it, almost as if body and air were one thing. The soul can then perceive what happens in that air—its shape, the distances between things, their directions, and other features—as they are.[^V04-37]

#### III. Why Sounds Are High or Low, and What Echo, Ringing, and Letters Are

A high sound can be caused by the hardness and smoothness of the struck object; by its short length and strong tension, as in some instruments; or by a narrow air passage and a point close to where the air is blown. These causes produce a sound that reaches the ear as a high pitch. A low sound comes from the opposite causes. Both high and low pitch can vary by degree: as the causes increase, the effect increases in proportion, and as the causes decrease, so does the effect.

An echo occurs when moving air meets a solid object, such as a mountain or wall, that does not let the air through. The air is turned back in the opposite direction, keeping the shape of its first wave, much as a ball bounces away from a wall. This produces an echo. If the sound keeps bouncing between two sides, as when one side of a basin is struck, the repeated sound is called ringing or resonance.

A letter is a feature of sound that lets us tell one sound from another even when they have the same pitch. Avicenna calls the feature itself the letter. Some scholars call the sound that carries it the letter; others mean both the sound and the feature together.

I say all these accounts make sense. The feature relates to the sound as a distinguishing part relates to a broader kind. They exist as one thing, though the mind can analyze them as two; there is no separate feature sitting on top of the sound in the outside world. Saying that the sounds have the same pitch rules out pitch itself as the difference, since high and low sounds are already distinct. Saying that they are distinguished “in what is heard” rules out differences such as length, shortness, pleasantness, and similar features, which do not make one letter sound different from another. Some question whether the feature that distinguishes a letter must itself be heard, or whether it only needs to make the heard sound different. For example, nasal tone or hoarseness can vary while the sound remains the same, and a sound can change without either of them changing. These definitions, like other definitions of things we sense, explain what is already clear to the mind by pointing out its features.

#### IV. How Letters Divide into Consonants and Vowels, and into Brief and Sustained Sounds

The three short vowels are counted among the letters. They are called short voiced sounds. The letters alif, wāw, and yā’ when they have no vowel of their own and follow a matching short vowel are called long voiced sounds: alif follows the short “a” sound, wāw follows “u,” and yā’ follows “i.” In Arabic these are the letters that lengthen and soften the vowel. All other letters are consonants. Wāw and yā’ are also consonants when they carry a vowel, or when they have no vowel and are not preceded by the matching short vowel. Alif is only a long vowel; the name is also used for hamza, but that is a different use of the word.

Here, “movement” and “stillness” do not mean the movement and rest of physical bodies. A movement is a feature formed in a consonant when its place of articulation shifts toward the place of one of the long vowels: toward alif for the “a” sound, toward wāw for “u,” and toward yā’ for “i.” Everyone agrees that a long vowel cannot begin a word. They disagree about why. Some say it is because the long vowel is still, and so a still consonant cannot begin a word either. Others say it cannot begin a word by its nature: it is a lengthened version of a matching short vowel, so it can only come after a consonant with that short vowel. This second view is right. Anyone with healthy hearing can start a sound with a consonant that linguists classify as still, even though Arabic does not allow it at the start of a word.

Letters can also be divided into brief and sustained sounds. A sound that can be prolonged, such as “f,” is sustained; one that cannot, such as “t,” is brief. A brief sound may come at the start of an exhalation, as in *ṭalaʿa* (“it rose”), or at its end, as in *ghalaṭ* (“a mistake”). A brief sound in the middle of a word may be either. When a brief sound is at the edge of a sustained sound, it is like an endpoint on a line. Some brief sounds, such as “ḥ” and “kh,” seem to last over time, even though they cannot be prolonged. This is because several identical instances occur together when we pronounce them, but our hearing cannot separate them in time, so they seem like one sound.

A consonant followed by a short vowel forms a short syllable, such as *la*, *lu*, or *li*. A consonant followed by a long vowel forms a long syllable, such as *lā*, *lū*, or *lī*. Sometimes a consonant followed by a short vowel and a still consonant is also called a long syllable, as in *hal*, *qul*, or *biʿ*, because it has the same rhythm as a long vowel syllable.

The twenty-nine sounds of Arabic, and the sounds of other languages, differ in kind. Each sound also has variations. Some variations sort sounds into groups, such as whether a consonant is still or vowelled, rounded or open, doubled or doubled onto another sound. Others distinguish one instance from another: this “b” spoken by one person now, the same sound spoken later, or spoken by another person in a particular place and way. These questions belong more properly to the study of nature.

### Book Five: Taste and Smell, and Why They Are Properties of Things

#### I. Tastes

Bodies are either tasteless or have a taste. Tasteless bodies may truly have no taste, or they may only seem tasteless to us. Copper, iron, and similar metals do not release particles that enter the tongue and can be tasted. But if they are dissolved and refined, they break into very fine particles and a strong taste appears.

There are nine basic tastes: pungent, salty, bitter, oily, sweet, bland, astringent, puckering, and sour. A body that has a taste may be fine, dense, or somewhere between the two. The force acting on it may be hot, cold, or moderate. When a hot force acts on a dense body, it produces bitterness; on a fine body, pungency; and on a balanced body, saltiness. A cold force produces astringency in a dense body, sourness in a fine one, and puckering in a balanced one. A moderate force produces an oily taste in a fine body, sweetness in a dense one, and blandness in a balanced one.

Pungency is the hottest taste, followed by bitterness and then saltiness, because pungency breaks things down more strongly than bitterness. Saltiness is like bitterness softened by cool moisture. It forms when burnt, dry, bitter, earth-like particles mix in moderate amounts with watery moisture that has little or no taste. If there is too much of the bitter earth, the mixture becomes bitter. Borax and bitter salt are hotter than table salt, which shows that saltiness is less hot than bitterness.

Astringency is cooler than these tastes, followed by puckering, then sourness. Sweet fruits begin with a strongly astringent, cooling taste. As the sun ripens them, they turn sour, as unripe grapes do. Between these stages comes a slight puckering taste, not full astringency; then the fruit becomes sweet. Astringency and puckering are similar, but puckering tightens the outer surface of the tongue, while astringency reaches the inside as well.

Two tastes can also combine in one body. Bitterness and puckering come together in *ḥuḍuḍ*, producing a harsh taste called *bashāʿa*. Sweetness and pungency come together in cooked honey; bitterness, pungency, and puckering in eggplant; and bitterness and blandness in chicory. These mixed tastes may arise because some things affect both taste and touch. Their flavor combines with the sensation they cause on the tongue, and we feel the two as one distinct taste.

Among the tastes between the extremes, one breaks things apart and heats them: pungency. Another breaks things apart without heat: sourness. A third tightens and dries: astringency. This is as much about tastes as natural philosophy needs to say. Anything beyond it belongs in a book on medicine.

#### II. Smells

We name smells in three ways. First, we name them after their sources: the smell of musk, ambergris, or dung. Second, we call them pleasant or foul, without giving a precise account of what makes each smell what it is. Third, we borrow words from tastes and speak of a sweet or sour smell. We do this because certain smells often occur with certain tastes, and we recognize one by its familiar connection to the other.

This completes the account of the qualities we sense, following the approach in books such as Avicenna’s *Healing*. I will discuss how sensation works later, when I turn to qualities of the soul. There is also a deeper question about these sensed qualities. When I discuss life after death and the place of human souls after they leave their bodies, I will return to them and show that they exist as forms without matter in another world, beyond the world of opposites and change.

### Section Two: Power and the lack of power

#### I. Three Kinds of Power

This group of qualities has three kinds. The first is a strong tendency to be affected, as in a tendency to become ill or in softness. This is called lack of power. The second is a strong tendency to resist being affected, as in robust health or hardness. The third is a strong ability to act, as in wrestling. The second and third are called power.

Two accounts have been given of what the three kinds have in common, the feature that makes them one kind of quality. One says it is a body's complete readiness to act on or be affected by something outside it. The other says it is a bodily source that helps bring about an outside event by making it more likely. The second account is better. Readiness always relates one thing to another: something is ready for something. So how could readiness itself be a kind of quality? This second account also covers all three kinds, since both the thing that acts and the thing affected help bring about an event.

The tendency to be affected makes that change more likely; the tendency to resist makes resistance more likely; and the ability to act makes action more likely. All three are bodily sources that make particular events more likely.

In his *Categories*, Avicenna says that this group of qualities is a body's strong readiness, in some respect, for something outside it. It is not the raw potential of prime matter—the basic matter that can take on any form—or mere possibility. Every person, for example, might be healthy or sick. But a fully developed readiness makes one side of a pair of opposites more likely than the other. It is not enough that someone could become ill or throw another person in a wrestling match. There must be a real tendency toward illness rather than health, or toward resisting a throw rather than being thrown. Robust health, a tendency to illness, wrestling ability, being easy to throw, hardness that resists being pressed in, and softness that gives way to pressure all belong to this group.

There is no disagreement that the tendency to be affected and the tendency to resist belong to this kind. The disagreement is about whether the ability to act belongs to it. Most say it does. Avicenna excludes it, and he is right, as will become clear.

If we want a definition that covers the first two kinds but leaves out the third, we can say: it is a quality that makes something more likely to be affected or to resist being affected.

To show why the ability to act does not belong here, we first need a general point: how something acts follows from what it is. Every thing has a nature that produces its proper effect when nothing blocks it. It does not need a second power in order to act. If another power were added, then the thing would receive that power, not produce it. And if we consider the thing and the added power together, their action must follow from what they are; it cannot wait for some further readiness to act. If they did need such readiness, they would first need a capacity to receive whatever made them able to act. So the supposed readiness to act would really be a readiness to be affected.

This shows that a thing needs no separate readiness, by its own nature, in order to act. Readiness belongs directly to being affected and to receiving something one did not have before. If we speak of readiness to act, we mean it only in a secondary sense.

#### II. A Closer Look at These Kinds

Avicenna says that someone might ask whether wrestling belongs to this group because a wrestler is hard to throw, or because he can throw others. If it belongs because he is hard to throw, the answer is easy: this group would be a stronger tendency toward one side of a passive ability, either being affected or not being affected. But that explanation creates a problem. The wrestler’s readiness is being counted here because he moves someone else, and it is hard to fit that under the other kinds of quality. If wrestling belongs because the wrestler can throw others, the original problem becomes even stronger.

By wrestling ability, however, I do not mean the soul’s basic power to set the body in motion. That power belongs to the soul itself and does not grow stronger or weaker. I mean the bodily support that helps it work well.

The body's support for that power is like sharpness of understanding in a rational soul. Wrestling ability involves three things: the body, the power that moves it, and the power that perceives and understands.

The part related to understanding is knowledge of wrestling as a craft, just as someone may know how to dance or play a lute. In general, it is know-how for actions that involve movement, like building or writing. The part related to the power that moves the body is a settled skill in using the muscles well to achieve the aim in a wrestling match. When these two abilities are weak, they are passing skills; when they are strong and well established, they become lasting habits. Neither is purely bodily.

The third part is bodily: the limbs are strong and hard to bend or move. This belongs to the group we are discussing. The difficulty is now resolved. This kind of quality is a body's strong bias toward one of two outcomes. It may be strongly inclined to leave its natural, healthy state, as in a tendency to become ill, or to resist change, as in robust health. In short, it either makes the body more likely to change or helps it stay as it is. That is what Avicenna says.

Someone may still say that an ability to act has two sides: the ability itself, and the strength that makes acting easy. On this view, the ability itself is a state or habit, while its strength or ease belongs to the group we are discussing.

Avicenna answers in his *Healing* that the person who is more easily thrown still has some ability to throw others, though it is weak. And the person who is better at throwing others still has some tendency to be thrown, though it too is weak. So each person has both abilities, but one is stronger and the other weaker. This difference must either lie in what the abilities are, or in features added to them from outside.

If the difference lies in what the abilities are, then their greater or lesser strength is not something added from outside. The stronger ability is one thing, and by its nature differs from the weaker ability. If that kind of ability belongs to one group, it cannot belong to the other. But if the difference lies only in added features, that cannot be right either. Rejecting this explanation also supports our conclusion.

Here is why. If one and the same ability remains unchanged while a separate feature makes it stronger and more active, then a quality other than the ability itself must accompany it and make it more effective. That is impossible. And once this is ruled out, it follows that a stronger ability cannot belong to this kind of quality if the weaker ability does not. Something cannot belong to a group if the same kind of thing does not.

There is another reason to reject the view. Heat has a strong ability to burn. If that ability belonged to this group as well as to the group of qualities that act on other things and are acted on, heat would belong to two basic kinds of quality at once. That is impossible. So a strong ability to act does not belong to this group.

#### III. Where Softness and Hardness Fit Among the Qualities

We have already said, when discussing qualities sensed by touch, that hardness is a natural tendency to resist being affected, while softness is a natural tendency to be affected. We cannot say that one is simply the absence of the other more than we can say the reverse. So they seem to be two positive qualities, not opposites where one is merely the absence of the other.

But someone might object: this natural tendency seems to involve three things. One is a lack—the body's not being pressed in. The other two are positive: the resistance we feel, and the body's keeping its shape. The tendency itself cannot be a lack, since it gives rise to two positive effects. A cause of something positive must itself be positive.

Also, as we said earlier, being pressed in is a movement along the body's surface, together with a particular shape taking form in it. A body can be moved because it is a natural body, and it can take on shape because it has size. If being a natural body with size caused this ability by itself, there would be no need for another quality to give it that ability. What belongs to a thing by its own nature does not need a second cause.

But if the tendency to be affected is not an added quality, then the tendency to resist must come from some positive cause. It cannot come from the matter itself, since matter is what makes the body ready to be affected. Nor can it come from the loss of some feature in matter: the absence of a feature is not a positive cause of the tendency to resist. These points make it seem that softness and hardness are opposites where one is present and the other absent.

I can answer this difficulty by pointing out that we sometimes define things within a group by using a negative, a relation, or a feature that belongs to another group. Here, saying that something hard is “not pressed in” defines a positive quality by describing what it lacks. Saying that something soft “is pressed in” defines it through another kind of feature: its ability to be affected. So softness and hardness can both be positive qualities within this group of power and lack of power, even though one is defined by a negative and the other by a positive feature from a different group.

I also reject the claim that a soft body can be pressed in simply because it is a natural body with size. Being a natural body with size may make a thing able to move in general or take on a shape. It does not by itself explain this particular movement and shape: being pressed in. If the body, its basic matter, and its size are not enough to explain why it can be pressed in, another positive condition must be involved. This is my answer to the difficulty.

There is still another problem. “Readiness” and “lack of readiness” are ideas in the mind, like possibility, impossibility, and necessity. They seem to name either an absence or a relation. But absences do not belong to any group of qualities, and relations belong to a different group. So how can readiness and lack of readiness belong here?

We know that when a body combines with qualities such as heat, cold, moisture, and dryness, these tendencies arise. Yet there may be no added quality that is itself called a “readiness.” The only way to resolve this is to say that a body's potential to accept or resist an effect can come close to being real because of a quality, or because a quality has grown stronger. This is different from mere possibility as the mind understands it. That kind of possibility cannot be closer to or farther from reality, or favor one side over another. This bodily potential can. At the same time, it is not the quality itself, and it does not belong to any of the other groups.

### Section Three: Qualities found in souls

These qualities are generally divided into temporary states and settled habits. Some say the difference between them is only accidental, not a difference in kind. On this view, a feature of the soul is called a state while it is first taking shape. Once that same feature becomes firmly established, it is called a habit. The same individual can have a state first and later a habit, just as one person can first be a child and later an adult.

I say that anyone who wants to see why this account fails should look at the difference between a state and a habit in knowledge. A state is a mental form present in the soul, an attribute whose subject is the soul. But when knowledge becomes a habit, the soul must unite with an intellectual substance and, through it, become able to produce such forms and others. How can what acts be the very same thing as what is acted upon? This stability is a perfection of what was first an unstable state. And since, on their own view, stronger and weaker forms differ in kind, they should differ at least as distinct individuals.

If the meaning of “habit” includes some power or ability, then lasting forms of knowledge in the souls of the heavens, and other qualities such as their power and will, would fall outside both states and habits. The same would be true of the knowledge of the separate intellects, which later philosophers and some before them described as added forms within those beings. These do not fade quickly or slowly, and they are not powers that are near to or far from being used; they are already active forms. Nor do they fit the other kinds of quality in the traditional four-part division. To make the division broad enough to include them, one could replace “qualities of the soul” with “non-sensible perfection,” and define a habit as a stable form without defining its general kind. Or one could add another group.

Or we may divide these qualities into a temporary state, a settled habit, and a third kind distinct from both. This third kind includes the universal will of the heavenly souls and their permanent forms of knowledge. It would be a lasting perfection that is not a mere readiness for something else and does not pass away. In this way it would stand beside states and habits as a separate kind.

States and habits include countless kinds. Books on this subject mention only a few. Sufi books, by contrast, count many stages on the seekers’ path and stations of the knowers—some give the number as seven hundred or so. Here I will discuss the kinds usually covered, each in its own chapter. Knowledge is treated separately because it is so important and its questions are so difficult. Other qualities, such as powers and character traits, are discussed in chapters.[^V04-38]

#### I. Power

We have already explained power when discussing what a capacity is. In an animal, power is a state of the soul that makes it possible for an action to come from the animal if it wills, and not to come from it if it does not. The opposite is inability. Both power and inability can vary from one action to another.

To say that something is capable does not mean that it can do anything it wants. If it did, only God could be called capable. An animal or other being may be able to do only a small number of things—or none at all.[^V04-39]

An animal’s power is a possibility that is equally open to acting or not acting. It can perform an action or leave it undone. But when will—meaning intention—is added to this power, the balance shifts toward one side. The action becomes necessary if the will reaches a firm decision, or more likely if some uncertainty remains.[^V04-40]

The power that is identical with will, and will that is identical with knowledge of what is good and of the best possible order, lies beyond mere possibility and reaches necessity. This is the case with God. His power is not one of the soul’s qualities that may or may not bring about some other possible thing, since there is no cause-and-effect link between them. Nor is God’s power related to all beings as mere possibility. They all came to be through God’s power and were made necessary by it.

His power is fully active because it is identical with knowledge and will. The power of an animal is incomplete. If an animal’s power were identical with its awareness and its will to act or refrain, then the action would follow necessarily. That would be an action already taking place, not a mere capacity for action. So the animal’s power stands to the power joined with knowledge and will as the incomplete stands to the complete. Everything found in the incomplete is found in the complete, except for what comes from weakness, deficiency, or mere possibility.

For this reason, it is right to speak of God’s power as meaning that if He wills, He acts, and if He does not will, He does not act—even though His will is identical with His power. The same is true of His perfect and complete knowledge. One single reality is knowledge, will, and power, all present in one self-sufficient existence.

If power had to be separate from will, and could not by itself entail will, then there would have to be some time when power existed without a will for a possible effect. In that case, the effect would neither exist nor fail to exist. Or else the will that brings about this effect would have to be different from the will that brings about another effect, and the general meaning of power would not apply. We would have to give it an entirely different meaning, or one that only partly matches the definition already given. Yet the familiar definition applies to both complete and incomplete power.

If power were defined as existing without will, or as willing that an effect not occur, it would have to be incomplete. When a power has reached the full strength of its ability to bring about an effect, it must be joined to will, and the effect must follow without delay. This is complete power.

#### II. Will

In animals, will is a quality of the soul. Its meaning seems clear when we act, though it is hard to put into words in a way that captures it exactly. Will differs from appetite, just as reluctance differs from aversion. A person may will what they do not desire, such as taking a bitter medicine that will help them. And a person may desire what they do not will, such as eating delicious food that will harm them.

Theologians have defined will as a quality that settles which of two possible outcomes will occur. Some say that, in an animal, it is a strong longing for the thing willed. Others say it differs from strong longing: will is a firm decision, a settled resolve. A person may desire something without willing it, or will something without desiring it, as we have seen. On this account, will is a chosen inclination, while desire is a natural inclination.

Some say this is why a person who is responsible for their actions is punished for willing a wrong, but not for desiring it. Those who hold this view describe five beginnings of an animal’s voluntary actions: imagining the act; believing it will bring benefit or prevent harm; desire; firm resolve, which they call will; and the power that moves the body. The other group leaves out resolve and treats it as nothing more than strong desire.

But there is a problem with treating intention and will as voluntary actions in their own right. If they were voluntary, they would need another intention to bring them about, and that would lead to an endless chain. To say that one part of this chain is voluntary and another is not would be arbitrary and contrary to experience. It seems, rather, that when desire becomes strong enough, resolve follows necessarily.

The beginnings of voluntary actions therefore lead back to things that happen necessarily, without choice. A belief that something will bring pleasure or benefit arises without our choosing it. Desire follows, and the moving power obeys it. These steps follow one another by necessity. In an animal, choice consists in its awareness and the desire that follows from it, which together cause the action. Its power is that cause of action, such as the power that moves the limbs.

As for God’s will, the philosophers say it is His knowledge of the world’s order in its most complete and perfect form.

That knowledge is sufficient for the most complete order to exist, and it gives existence the advantage over nonexistence. In this sense, it is will. Knowledge in us, too, can become so strong that it causes something to happen outside us. For example, someone walking along a narrow, high wall may become so convinced that they are about to fall that this thought causes them to fall. The influence of some souls through concentrated intention, and the evil eye, belongs to the same kind of event; we know of their effects from experience and the reports of a truthful witness. So it should not seem impossible that eternal knowledge is the cause of all that comes to be.

#### III. What Character Is and Its Kinds

Character is a settled trait from which actions flow easily, without first having to think them through. It is not simply the ability to act, because an ability is equally open to opposite actions, as we have already said. Nor is character the action itself. Rather, it is a condition of the soul that lets a person perform a craft without having to consider every step: writing without thinking over each letter, or playing a lute without thinking over each note.

In fact, a person may become less skilled when they stop to think through each part of an action. Once the trait is well established, the action does not begin in reflection. Its relation to reflection is more like the relation of nature to thought. Nature is different from deliberation, though in many cases it is not separate from knowledge—for example, the nature of the heavenly spheres is knowledge and awareness without deliberation.

Character seems to stand between nature and deliberate will. It is usually acquired through effort and practice. The heavenly spheres and first principles therefore have no character traits; the sources of their actions are nature or intellect. Nor does a character trait have to be the direct source of an action. It is enough that, when a person chooses to act, the action comes easily and without effort or reflection.

In the same way, knowledge as a habit does not mean that all the things known are always present to the mind. It means that a person can bring them to mind without having to think them through again.

Every state and every habit is a real quality. And every real quality, simply because it exists, is a kind of perfection—whether people call it a virtue or a vice in everyday speech, philosophy, or religion. Some qualities take away other perfections that belong to noble souls; others increase their dignity and beauty. The former are vices in the human rational soul, and the latter are its virtues.

Yet every trait that is a vice for human beings—greed, sexual excess, recklessness, or cunning—may be a virtue for a lower kind of soul. Excessive appetite is a perfection in animals, but a vice in a human being because of the rational soul.

Wisdom, in the sense of understanding universal and intellectual truths that exist in their own right, is a human perfection. The more of it a person has, the better. Other character traits have two extremes, one of excess and one of deficiency, with a middle point between them. Virtue lies in the middle, not at either extreme.

Excess is harmful because it damages other virtues, especially the habit of knowledge, which is the root of the virtues. Deficiency is harmful because having too little—or none at all—means losing what is needed to pursue lasting good, which is intellectual perfection.

The chief virtues and sources of good human actions are three: courage, self-restraint, and practical wisdom. Together they make up justice. This practical wisdom is different from wisdom in the first sense, where more is always better.

Each of these three virtues leads to actions between two extremes, both of which are vices. Courage leads to actions between recklessness and cowardice. Self-restraint leads to actions between uncontrolled desire and a deadening of desire. Practical wisdom leads to actions between crafty scheming and stupidity.

Some people have confused this practical wisdom—a virtue of character—with the branch of wisdom that is opposed to theoretical wisdom. That is a mistake. Practical wisdom as a virtue is a trait of the soul that leads to actions between cunning and stupidity.

When philosophers say that wisdom is either theoretical or practical, they do not mean a character trait. A trait is not one of the branches of philosophy. They mean knowledge about character traits: how many there are, what kinds they are, which are good and which are bad, how they can be gained or removed from the soul, and how to manage a household or a city. In general, practical wisdom is knowledge of the things we can bring into being or take out of being.

This knowledge is not inborn. Once we learn it, we know it even if we do not act on it or acquire the traits it describes. So it is not the same as the actions that practical wisdom, in the other sense, produces.

In short, “practical wisdom” can mean the character trait itself, knowledge about character, or the actions that flow from a character trait. The practical wisdom that stands opposite theoretical wisdom is knowledge of character and of the actions that come from it. It can be a virtue to have more of this knowledge, as explained above. But practical wisdom as one of the three virtues is a particular character trait, different from other traits; both too much and too little of it are vices.

The distinction should now be clear. The three balanced virtues together—and the harmony among them—make up justice. Its opposite is injustice, which lies at any of the extremes. This is the straight path stretched over Hell, or the path that leads to deserving its punishment.

#### IV. What Pleasure and Pain Are

Philosophers define pleasure and pain as the awareness of what suits us and the awareness of what conflicts with us. Some doctors, including Muḥammad ibn Zakariyyā al-Rāzī, claim that pleasure is a shift out of an unnatural condition, and pain is a shift out of a natural one. On that view, no pleasure or pain could last forever. Everyday experience seems to support this: pleasures are strongest when they first arise, and fade once they settle.[^V04-41]

Someone with wealth, status, or fine food may take less pleasure in it than a poor person takes in a small and ordinary thing. The same seems true of pain: most pains, perhaps all of them, stop troubling us if they last without any change. We see people with wounds, misfortunes, or illnesses who still feel joyful during much of the time they live with them. We must find a way to resolve this difficulty.

The reason for this view, I say, is that people mistake something that happens alongside a thing for its true cause. Pleasure requires awareness. Sensory pleasure requires sensation, and the sense of touch in particular works only when the sense organ is affected by a quality opposite to it. Once that quality settles in the organ, the organ is no longer affected by it. There is no sensation, and so no pleasure of touch—or of another sense—unless the condition changes. For this reason, some have thought that pleasure itself is this change in the sense organ.

But this view is false. A person may take pleasure in seeing a beautiful image they did not know existed and had never longed to see. So we cannot say that seeing it relieves a prior longing or the pain of being separated from it. A person may also understand a difficult idea without having wanted or longed to understand it, such as when a puzzling argument is finally resolved after much effort. We cannot say that the pleasure lies only in relief from the strain of thinking.

The same is true when someone unexpectedly receives great wealth or high status, without having expected or sought it. These things still bring pleasure. So this account is wrong.

Avicenna says in his *Treatise on Heart Remedies* that joy is a kind of pleasure, and that every pleasure is awareness of a perfection belonging to a particular faculty: tasting sweetness, smelling a pleasant scent, feeling the satisfaction of revenge through the part of the soul that becomes angry, or anticipating some future benefit through hope or expectation. Every perfection is natural to the faculty, and the reverse is also true: whenever a faculty is aware of something natural to it, it takes pleasure in it.[^V04-42]

Some faculties may take pleasure only when they move out of an unnatural condition, as if staying in their natural condition were not itself pleasant. The mistake comes from treating an accompanying feature as the cause. Aristotle identifies this as one of the fallacies in his book on Sophistical Refutations.

Here is how this applies to our question. Some things can be sensed only while the sense organ is changing. This is true of touch: a quality can be felt only while the organ is opposite to it and being affected by it. Once the organ has been affected and the quality settles in it, the quality becomes part of the organ’s own condition and is no longer sensed. Every sensation involves some change, but a thing does not change from itself.

That is why a person with a wasting fever may not be troubled by extreme heat, even when it is greater than the heat of a severe burning fever, while the person with the burning fever is troubled by less heat. In a wasting fever, the heat has become settled in the organs like their usual condition, though the organs’ own condition is still different from it. The heat of a burning fever, by contrast, has newly arrived. Doctors call the first a settled imbalance and the second a changing imbalance.

We have seen that a settled sensory perfection brings no pleasure because it is not perceived. Pleasure arises when we first move toward a natural condition, because we become aware of the perfection. Since this awareness happens while we are leaving an unnatural condition, people mistake that change for the cause of pleasure. But the real cause is the perfection itself.

At the end of the eighth book of *The Healing*, Avicenna says that pleasure is the awareness of what suits us precisely because it suits us. In his discussion of the afterlife in the ninth book, he says that each faculty shares in the good and pleasure proper to it when it is aware of what agrees with it. What truly suits each faculty is its perfection—the perfection that it actually has in relation to itself.

Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī says that pleasure and pain are facts we know directly, without needing them defined. Just as conclusions we learn through reasoning rest on truths that need no proof, ideas we learn through explanation rest on ideas that need no definition. We know through the senses that we feel pain or pleasure, so the meanings of these experiences should be even clearer.

But there is a question that still needs an answer: Is the experience we call pleasure simply awareness of what suits us, or is it something different? If it is different, does awareness cause it, or does something else? It is possible that it always occurs alongside awareness without being caused by it. Rāzī says he has not been able to prove which account is right. He thinks pain is probably not the same as awareness of what conflicts with us. Medical experience, he says, shows that a damp imbalance can be sensed without causing pain. If every unnatural condition were itself pain, pain could not be absent in such a case. So awareness of what conflicts with us is not enough to produce pain.

I reply that his first claim—that pleasure and pain are self-evident and need no definition—conflicts with his later admission that he does not know whether either is the same as awareness of what suits or conflicts with us. His third point, that pain is neither awareness of what conflicts with us nor something that necessarily follows from it, is surprising from a thinker of his learning.

The proof that pleasure is awareness of the presence of a perfection, and pain awareness of what opposes that perfection, is very clear once we understand awareness, existence, and perfection.

Existence is not, as Rāzī and other later thinkers imagined, an abstract idea that the mind draws from things, like “thingness” or “possibility.” It is real and present; it drives out nonexistence. It is the reality of a thing and makes each thing with an essence distinct from others. Existence differs among things with different essences: the existence of a person is not the existence of a horse, and the existence of the sky is not that of the earth. Just as existence differs across kinds of things, it can also become stronger or weaker, more complete or less complete.

A thing’s existence is good for it, and the completeness of that existence is a further good. Losing existence is an evil and a harm; losing some perfection is also an evil, though a lesser one. This is true whether or not the thing is aware of its existence or perfection. Inanimate things, for example, have existence and perfection even though they are not aware of them. Existence or perfection is one thing; awareness of it is another.

Awareness itself is not, as some have claimed, merely a relation between the knower and what is known, with no mental form involved. If that were so, awareness could not divide into concepts and judgments, nor could it be about something that does not exist. We could not know ourselves either, since there is no relation between a thing and itself, or between a thing and what does not exist.

Rather, awareness is the mental form itself: a form that exists without matter, at some level of separation from matter. The mind can grasp forms at different levels. A sensed form is separated from matter; an imagined form is separated from matter and place; and an understood form is also separated from size and measurable quantity.

This form resembles what it represents. Sometimes it also resembles the knower, as when a person knows themselves through a form in the mind that is distinct from them. Sometimes it is identical with the knower, as in the case of a self-existing, immaterial form. Such a form is the knower, the known, and knowledge all at once, since all three descriptions apply to it.

A mental form may also resemble one of the knower’s faculties. This is what happens when a person senses what suits their senses: bright colors, pleasant sounds and scents, delicious foods, or soft clothes. Or the form may oppose the knower or one of their faculties. This is what happens when someone becomes aware of their own ignorance, foolishness, or bad character.

The second case occurs when we sense dull or dark colors, unpleasant sounds, foul smells, bitter or astringent tastes, or rough clothing.

With these points in place, we can see that existence is good and beautiful in itself, while nonexistence is evil in itself, as our natural judgment tells us. When something exists, its existence is good for it. Awareness is one way something can exist for another, so it too is a perfection—unless it harms or opposes some other perfection.

If heat could exist alongside cold and be present in it, heat would be a perfection for cold. But heat drives out the cold that belongs to the thing by its own nature. In that case, heat brings about nonexistence for the thing. Any quality whose presence in something would require that thing itself to cease existing cannot be its perfection. It is a harm and an evil—not simply because it exists, but because it removes that thing’s existence. Evil in itself is nonexistence.

So any awareness that does not oppose the being of the one who is aware is good for them. As I have said, this good is pleasure, since existence is good whether or not it is perceived. But when the good itself is awareness of existence, awareness is pure good—and therefore pleasure and joy.

Anyone can see that if a thing has a power or perfection suited to its kind of being, and it is aware of that perfection without any harm, it takes pleasure in it. If it is not aware of its perfection, it has no pleasure in it. I am not trying to prove this by repeating the claim in a circle. Rather, from the points already established—that existence is good, and that awareness of a good is another good for the one who is aware—I argue that every awareness that is itself good and beautiful for the knower, or for one of their faculties or parts, is pleasure and joy for them.

In the same way, if something that exists causes a being, one of its perfections, or one of its faculties to cease to exist, and that thing appears in the being as a mental form, then its awareness is the presence of something harmful and opposed. Whenever something harmful and opposed exists for a being, it damages that being. Awareness of what harms us is pain.

If the harmful thing were not perceived, it would not itself be pain, even though it would still be bad for us. For example, if a body were broken apart and its continuity lost, that would be harmful, but it would not be pain. Yet when the sense of touch receives the form of this separation, the form opposes the continuity that is a perfection of the organ. Since the perceived form of separation is the separation as it is known, it is itself a harm and pain.[^V04-43]

The form of separation is not simply the separation itself, though it is the same in one respect. If it were the separation itself, there would be no pain, since neither matter nor absence feels pain. If the form did not resemble separation in meaning, it would not oppose continuity, which is the organ’s perfection. Nor would it be knowledge; it would be an object outside the perceiver. In short, pleasure is a perfection that belongs to a perceiver precisely as they are aware of that perfection. Pain is the opposite of such a perfection, present to a perceiver through awareness.

The proof therefore shows that pleasure is awareness of what suits us, and pain is awareness of what conflicts with us.

Rāzī’s argument against this—that a damp imbalance can be sensed without causing pain—can be answered even if we grant that it is not painful. We do not accept that it is perceived by a faculty for which it is harmful. Pain means awareness of what conflicts with the faculty that perceives it, not of what conflicts with some other faculty.

A person has many different faculties. Something that harms one may be perceived by another without harming it. And something that harms one faculty may go unnoticed by another. In the latter case, there is no pain. A cold imbalance may destroy the sense of touch. If touch is gone, there is no pain for that sense, even if another faculty can imagine or understand the imbalance. Thinking about or imagining the imbalance does not hurt the imagination or intellect. Pain occurs only when the faculty that bears the imbalance—the sense of touch—is aware of it and still able to function. A person with paralysis may not feel pain because the sense of touch has been impaired.

This also resolves another objection. A sick person may enjoy sweetness even though sweet food worsens the illness, and may reject bitter medicine even though it will help. But sweetness suits the sense of taste; it is not yet harmful to that faculty. Bitter medicine does not suit taste, even if it benefits the body. Sweetness may increase the harmful bodily mixture, while the bitter medicine may remove it, provide nourishment, or strengthen the body. The harm and benefit come from these effects, not from the two sensations themselves. If we sensed sweetness without any further effect, it would be wholly good and pleasurable.

If we sensed bitterness without any further effect, it would be wholly bad and painful. The extra effects are what make the medicine harmful or helpful to the body; the sensations themselves do not.

#### V. Does All Pain Come from a Break in Continuity?

Galen, whom physicians regard as their leading authority, says that the direct cause of pain—the pain felt by the senses—is a break in continuity. Heat hurts because it breaks continuity. Cold also hurts because it brings about a break, in the opposite way from gathering and compression. Black looks painful because it gathers things too much; white because it spreads them too far. Bitter and sour tastes hurt because they separate too much, while astringent taste tightens things too much and so leads to separation. Loud sounds also hurt because the movement of air strikes the eardrum with force and breaks continuity. In short, physicians agree that a break in continuity is the direct cause of pain.

I say that ordinary people use the words pleasure and pain only for bodily sensations because they do not recognize other ways of experiencing them. Every sense has a bodily organ, a subtle body with a continuous form and a balanced mixture of qualities. Pain may arise either when that continuity breaks or when the organ’s balanced mixture is disturbed by an opposing quality. This is why physicians disagree. Galen and most doctors say that the direct cause is the break in continuity, while a disturbed mixture is only an indirect cause.[^V04-44] Others, including the later Imam al-Rāzī, reverse the explanation. Avicenna says that either can be a direct cause, or can cause pain indirectly. Each view has arguments, which I leave out to avoid needless length. Their details can be found in al-Shīrāzī’s commentary on the *Canon*.

In *Eastern Investigations*, al-Rāzī gives several arguments against the physicians’ view.

First, a break in continuity means separation, which is an absence. An absence cannot cause pain, since pain is something real.

Second, if separation caused pain, we would be in pain all the time. Our bodies are always changing through nutrition and breakdown. Growth happens when food enters the body’s parts, and breakdown happens when some of those parts separate and are lost.

One might answer that these tiny separations do not hurt because they are too small to feel, especially since we have grown used to them. But, I reply, there are countless such changes. Nourishment and growth occur throughout the body, not just in one part. If breaking apart the body’s parts were not painful, then cutting off a limb would not hurt either, since the same kind of event should have the same effect. But a severed limb does hurt. So separation itself is not painful; it hurts only when joined by a disturbed bodily mixture.

Third, if separation directly caused pain, pain should arise at the same time. But it does not. When a limb is cut with a very sharp blade and the cut happens quickly, the pain is felt only after a moment—enough time for the bodily mixture to become disturbed.

Fourth, if a break in continuity were painful, a large wound would hurt more than a scorpion’s sting because the break would be greater. I will answer these arguments in turn. The first is answered by this: separation and similar events, which occur in matter after certain conditions are in place, are not pure absences without any share in existence. They have some degree of existence, and their forms are present in the external world, as are other things whose existence is weak.

Their existence consists in their subjects taking on negative descriptions and concepts because of the deficiencies and limitations that accompany them—not because of what those subjects are in themselves. So they are qualities that happen to things, not essential features, and they are evils in themselves.[^V04-45] Awareness of them is evil in the same way. Pain can therefore rightly be counted among evils in themselves.

This also resolves a problem some later thinkers raised against the philosophers. They said philosophers claim that evils in themselves are only absences, yet we know that pain—awareness of what conflicts with us—is an evil in itself, and awareness is something real.

The answer is that awareness of a thing is the same as that thing’s existence in the mind: if it exists in the mind, its awareness is in the mind; if it exists outside the mind, it exists outside. A person’s existence is the same as the reality of being human in the world. Likewise, the worldly existence of absences such as separation, blindness, deafness, and ignorance is those very absences. The awareness of these absences is their presence to a faculty that perceives them. This awareness belongs to the class of absences themselves. That is why pain is evil in itself, even though it is one kind of existence.

The existence of these privations—things that lack a perfection they should have—is, in the world, precisely their being absent.
