The evil of pains, which are present to the senses, is likewise one with that presence in reality, though the two differ in meaning, as essence and existence do. Understand this point and keep it in mind; it is rarely found elsewhere.

There are other possible answers. One could say that separation has two meanings. In one sense it is an absence—the loss of continuity. In another, it is the presence of many separate parts or connections. It is this second sense that hurts. Or “separation” might mean parts moving away from each other, which is not the same as simple discontinuity. If so, what causes pain is the organ’s loss of the proper form that completes it, and the sense perceives that loss directly, without any need for a disturbed mixture.

A further reply is that “cause” here may mean a condition that prepares the organ to feel pain, not the force that produces the pain itself. There is no impossibility in saying that the absence of continuity, once it occurs, makes pain possible, just as a disturbed bodily mixture does. These are arguments that can be made in debate, though they are not the final answer.

The true answer to the second and third arguments is that we do not mean the break itself is painful, or that it is the complete cause of pain that must always produce it. We mean that the image of separation, when it occurs in a sense organ and the soul attends to it, is painful if it is not already familiar. As we have said, what exists becomes familiar through repeated experience.

A break is painful in itself when it is perceived as conflicting with the organ’s quality or continuity. It does not need a disturbed bodily mixture to cause pain; it hurts because the organ’s form or matter has lost a perfection that belongs to it. So it is possible that the separation involved in nutrition and bodily breakdown does not form an image that the senses perceive. Or perhaps the part that is perceived has become familiar and no longer harms us. Or perhaps we perceive it as beneficial to the body because it preserves health and strength or clears away what the body does not need.

The claim that all separation has the same effect is plainly mistaken. The separation involved in nourishment is a natural and ongoing process in very small parts, and it brings many benefits to the body. Losing a limb is not like this. In any case, our real view is that nourishment and growth do not necessarily involve food being inserted as particles between the body’s own parts, as I will show later when discussing the powers of the soul.

I would add that from the beginning of life until the present moment, a person has known only this changing form of existence. It may be that we are in pain all the time, but because we have never known another kind of existence without this kind of separation, we assume we are not. If someone could experience the way beings in the lasting world exist—with no absences or separations—and were then made to stay in this world, they would die from pain and suffering.

In short, the separations that occur as the body breaks down are like the substantial change I have shown to be present in natural things. Their existence is a process of change: each moment brings a new state and ends the previous one. Yet we do not feel pain from this separation in our present kind of existence. As I have explained, our power of perception exists in this same changing way, and a thing’s existence is not painful to it.

A bodily mixture that is painful to one person may be normal to another. What hurts one person may even please another. As for the claim that pain from a severed limb is delayed by a moment, this does not prove that the sensation of cutting is not painful. If the cut is felt and the soul attends to it, it must hurt. If it happens without awareness or attention, it does not prove what the argument claims.

A person may turn their attention elsewhere and fail to feel hunger, thirst, or other discomforts. They may be focused on something important, such as a difficult question; something trivial, such as a game of chess; something stronger, such as a more severe pain or the danger of a battle; or something urgent, such as a serious worldly concern. The same is true of things that feel pleasant.

As for the fourth argument, it would follow only if a scorpion’s sting caused pain by a break in continuity alone. But the poison may disturb the body’s mixture and have a stronger effect than a large wound. The comparison challenges only those who say that pain is caused by separation and nothing else, as is commonly attributed to Galen and his followers.

They could also reply that poison causes many small breaks in the organ while the sense remains intact. Cutting off a limb makes only one break, and the removed limb no longer has its sense of touch. The part left behind, where the cut’s effect reaches, may be smaller than the part affected by the many breaks caused by the poison.

Know that each of these views has something right about it.

The physicians’ view can be defended by saying that what hurts is not the break in continuity itself, but the perceived form of that break. As we have said, bodily existence depends on continuity, and existence is unity. A break is the opposite of that unity, and the perceived opposite of existence directly causes pain.

The opposing view—that pain is caused by a disturbed bodily mixture—can be explained this way: the bodily mixture relates to continuity as a form relates to its matter. A thing is what its form makes it, not what its matter is. An animal is an animal because of the form of its bodily mixture, not because of the matter of its body, except indirectly. If the form of an animal’s mixture could exist without matter, it would still be an animal. So the direct opposite of an animal, considered in terms of its form, is another mixture unlike its own. Considered in terms of its matter, the opposite is a different pattern of continuity. The form is primary, so the disturbed mixture is the direct cause of pain, not the break in continuity.

The view of Avicenna and his followers is the soundest. Matter and form are joined as one in a body, so the health or corruption of either is the health or corruption of the other.

#### VI. Which Bodily Imbalance Causes Pain?

Avicenna says that an imbalance in the body causes pain only when it is hot or cold, not when it is damp or dry. It must also be an uneven, unsettled imbalance, not one that has become part of the body’s usual condition.

He gives this reason for excluding dampness and dryness: they are qualities that are acted on, not qualities that act. But this raises a problem. If the direct cause of pain must be something that acts, dryness can cause parts of the body to separate, and dampness and dryness can both cause many illnesses. They could then cause pain without any break in continuity. So pain would not be caused only by heat, cold, or separation.

If “direct cause” means a natural force that produces an effect, there is no proof that heat, cold, or separation has that role. But if the point is that pain is a sensation, and sensation is something that happens to us through a cause, then the objection still stands. Avicenna says that dampness and dryness are not active qualities. Yet he also says elsewhere that they are qualities we can sense, and the books call them among the first qualities felt by touch. When they go beyond the body’s normal balance, they conflict with it. Feeling them in that condition should be painful.

Avicenna also says that a dry imbalance can cause pain indirectly, because severe tightening may lead to a break in continuity, which causes pain directly. Someone objects that dampness can also lead to separation: too much moisture makes the body press against its limits and need more room. The reply is that, in this case, the cause is the material that carries the moisture, not the moisture itself.

As for the second condition, an imbalance that has settled into the body does not cause pain. It is called settled because it has become part of the organ’s own condition and has worn down the organ’s resistance. The sense is no longer affected, so it feels nothing and has no pain. For two qualities to conflict, both must remain present. When something new enters the body, the original condition must still be there for the sense to feel the clash between them.

A wasting fever, for example, can be hotter than a fever that comes and goes. It may require stronger cooling treatments and can damage even hard parts of the body. Yet the person with the wasting fever may feel less burning. Its heat has settled into the body, while the heat of the other fever is new. This, they say, is why the settled imbalance is not felt.

The same thing can happen in a winter bath. At first, lukewarm water feels unpleasant against cold skin. After the body adjusts, it feels pleasant. Hot water then feels painful, but after the body adjusts, that too becomes pleasant. If the person returns to the first water, it now feels cold and painful.

The reason is that a conflict can be felt only while two conditions remain together. If the new quality completely replaces the old one, only one remains, so there is no conflict and no pain. But if the new quality cannot replace the old one, both remain, and the sense feels their conflict. This, they say, is why a settled imbalance does not hurt but an uneven one does.

I have a question about this explanation. An organ is held together by its own particular bodily mixture. If another quality of the same kind, but opposed to it, enters the organ, it must either replace the first or remain alongside it. If it remains alongside it, two opposed qualities occupy the same place. If it replaces the first, then what power senses the new quality after the first has gone?

Avicenna’s answer is that the first quality may have disappeared from the organ, but its sensed form remains with the soul and has become familiar to it.[^V04-46] The organ is sustained at each moment by a new bodily mixture. The soul, not the organ, preserves the body’s identity through these changes.

Know, too, that an uneven bodily imbalance may cause no pain and may not even be sensed if it develops little by little. The first change is too small for us to notice. Then each new increase is only slightly different from what came just before, so we do not notice those changes either. A sudden change is different: because it is greater, we feel it, and we continue to feel it as long as the imbalance remains.

#### VII. Kinds of Pleasure, and Why Some Are Better

Pleasure and pain can be divided according to the power that experiences them. There are four kinds: pleasure and pain of understanding, of our judgments and expectations, of imagination, and of the senses.

Sensory pleasure is easy to understand. It includes the sense of touch responding to a pleasant texture, or taste responding to sweetness. Imaginative pleasure comes from picturing pleasures already gained, or hoping to gain them through victory or revenge. Pleasure arising from judgment and expectation includes beliefs about what may benefit us and hopes for things we want. As the saying goes: “If our hopes come true, they are all we could want; if not, we lived happily with them for a while.”

An intellect, too, has a perfection of its own. As far as it can, it should understand what it needs to know and hold a true picture of all the things that exist and how they fit together. When this picture is complete and free of guesses or false beliefs, the intellect has reached its full development. Some say that the soul then becomes one with the Active Intellect, which contains all existing things. In that state, the soul both knows and takes joy in what it knows; it is not only the source of the pleasure but also the one who enjoys it.

This intellectual perfection is good for a rational being, and the being is aware of it. So it takes pleasure in it. Intellectual pain occurs when someone who can reach this perfection instead gains its opposite and recognizes it as an opposite. Someone who could never reach that perfection does not suffer this kind of pain.

Compared with pleasure in the other powers, especially the senses, intellectual pleasure is greater in amount and strength. It is greater in amount because the things reason can understand are far more numerous, almost without limit, and they last. It is stronger because reason can reach what a thing is, while the senses reach only its outward features. So intellectual perfections and the awareness of them are greater, more lasting, and more complete. The same is true of pain when we notice that we have lost these perfections.

When intellectual pleasure reaches its full form, it no longer belongs to the group of qualities of the soul. At that point, the soul is an intellectual being, not a mental quality.

Someone may object that sensory pleasure and pain should also be counted as qualities that we sense, rather than as qualities of the soul. The reply is that the sense organ perceives the quality that brings pleasure or pain, such as sweetness or bitterness. It does not perceive pleasure or pain themselves. They are the acts of awareness and feeling, and the outward senses cannot sense them.

This is what some learned scholars have said, but I do not think it is sound. If pleasure and pain meant only a relation in the mind, they would belong to the group of relations. But we mean by them what the soul enjoys or suffers, and these belong to the same kind as awareness. As I have explained, awareness is a mental form, not a relation.

What we call qualities that we sense are the qualities that exist in the matter of bodies outside us, not the forms that exist in our sense organs as tools of perception. We use the same name for the quality and for the image of that quality in the sense. For example, when the mind knows a substance, we call that knowledge a “mental substance,” even though it is a quality of the soul. In the same way, the forms present in the senses are qualities of the soul. None of the five senses can sense these forms themselves; only the soul can. A sense does not sense its own act. It senses the object outside it.

What, then, should we say about the pleasure of sight? We see an object outside us. If seeing meant that an image of the object entered the eye, then a very large object would have to fit inside a very small organ, along with other problems. My view of sight is different from the familiar accounts: the visible form appears to the soul in a realm beyond this world. That form is not one of the qualities that we sense in bodies. Its color and shape are qualities of the soul, and it exists in the soul as an effect exists in its cause, not as a feature sitting inside the eye. Understand this point deeply. It is especially valuable for understanding life after death, and I will explain it elsewhere.

Galen is reported to have said that pleasure and pain occur in all the senses, and that the denser a sense organ is, the more it resists what acts upon it.[^V04-47] He concludes that its pleasure and pain are stronger. Sight is the finest sense because it works through light, which is like the finest element, fire. So sight has little pleasure or pain. Hearing is less fine because it works through struck air, so it has more. Smell works through vapor, which is denser than air, so it has still more. Taste works through moisture like water, so it has more than smell. Touch is the densest sense, like earth, and offers the strongest resistance to what affects it.

I say that Galen did not understand the soul, nor did he have a perceptive heart or a sharp mind. His account would make the pleasure of imagination weaker than every sensory pleasure, because imagination involves no such resistance. The same would be true of the pleasure of understanding, which he seems never to have experienced. But resistance has nothing to do with whether pleasure or pain arises in the first place. At most, it could affect how long the pleasure or pain lasts. Each power takes pleasure in a form that suits it and feels pain from a form that opposes it. The different powers have different levels of existence: some are stronger and some weaker, some finer and some denser. The strongest kinds of existence bring the strongest pleasure.

We should not accept that what is dense must be strong, or that what is fine must be weak. Nor should we accept that fire is the weakest element and earth the strongest. On the contrary, the existence of jinn and devils is stronger than that of animals and human beings. They perform difficult and powerful acts beyond even a small fraction of human ability. Reports of this are, in their general outline, so widely transmitted as to be certain, even though particular details rest on individual accounts. The heavenly spheres are also finer than the elements, yet they are stronger and more stable. Their awareness is therefore stronger and brings greater pleasure.

Why, then, do the senses differ? The animal body is made mainly of earth, then water, vapor, air, and fire. Above these come spirits, the heavenly spheres, and angels. Because the body is made mostly of the lower elements, most animals first sense touch, then taste, smell, hearing and sight. After these come imagined images and probable beliefs, then intellectual truths and certain knowledge. Their pleasures and pains follow the same order.

#### VIII. Avicenna on Pleasure in the Senses, and a Reply to an Objection

In the third chapter of the sixth book of the psychology section of *The Healing*, Avicenna says that some senses feel neither pleasure nor pain in the things they sense; other senses feel pleasure or pain through them.

Sight, for example, takes no pleasure or pain in colors. The soul does. The same is true of the ear. If a loud sound hurts the ear, or an intense color or light hurts the eye, the pain does not come from hearing or seeing. It comes from touch. The sound or light causes pain in the sense of touch, and when that pain goes away, the sense of touch feels pleasure.

Smell and taste, by contrast, can feel pleasure or pain when they are affected by a quality that suits or opposes them. Touch can feel pleasure or pain from a tactile quality, or from a break in continuity and its repair. That is Avicenna’s account.

A commentator on the *Canon*, known as al-Masīḥī, says this is extremely difficult to explain. He raises four objections.

First, Avicenna believed that the senses perceive individual things. Either he still holds that view here or he does not. If he does, this claim contradicts what he says about sight and hearing. If he does not, then what he says about the other senses is wrong.

Second, each sense has its own special object, which no other sense can perceive. How, then, could touch in the ear or eye perceive a loud sound or a harmful color?

Third, Avicenna’s account seems to conflict with his definition of pleasure as awareness of what suits us because it suits us. What suits sight is seeing colors, not feeling them through touch.

Fourth, either sensing these qualities is pleasure or pain for the senses, or it is not. If it is, then seeing beautiful colors must bring pleasure and seeing harmful colors must bring pain. If it is not, then touch, smell, and taste cannot feel pleasure or pain either. And if pleasure and pain belong to some senses but not others, that seems to be an arbitrary difference, since all five senses are ways for the soul to perceive individual things.

Al-Masīḥī then gives his own answer. What suits sight is beautiful color, what suits hearing is pleasant sound, and the same applies to the other senses. When a sense perceives what suits it, it feels pleasure, because the image of the object is present to the perceiver. Pleasure is nothing more than awareness of what suits us because it suits us. Pain works in the same way.

Al-Rāzī, in his *Investigations*, also calls Avicenna’s account correct. He then poses the objection: if seeing is what suits sight, why does Avicenna say that the eye does not enjoy it? Al-Rāzī replies: I do not accept that the eye itself perceives. It is the soul that sees and hears, using these organs as tools. This removes the difficulty for my view.

Avicenna’s answer is different. Colors themselves cannot be present in the power of sight, so they do not suit it in the same way that color suits a body. What suits sight is the act of seeing color. But Avicenna says that pleasure is not simply having what suits us; it is being aware that it suits us. Sight sees a color but does not know that it has seen it. The soul is aware of both the object and the fact that it has perceived the object, and so the soul feels pleasure.

Al-Rāzī then raises an objection against his own view that the soul, not the senses, perceives, and against the answer he has just given on Avicenna’s behalf. Either we accept that colors and sounds suit the senses, or we say that what suits the senses is the act of seeing or hearing them. If the objects suit the senses, then seeing a suitable object should itself be pleasure, contrary to Avicenna’s claim about sight and hearing. If the act of sensing is what suits the senses, then either merely having that act is pleasure, in which case sight must feel pleasure, or being aware of the act is pleasure. But then touch cannot feel pleasure, since it is not aware of its own act. This, he says, is the difficulty; he leaves it unanswered.

Al-Shīrāzī, in his commentary on the *Canon*, responds that al-Masīḥī’s first objection is not sound. Avicenna does not say that the senses themselves perceive or judge. According to him, only the soul truly perceives, judges, feels pleasure, or feels pain. We speak of the senses doing these things only as a figure of speech. Later thinkers, such as al-Rāzī and those who followed him, wrongly treated this way of speaking as literal.

The soul perceives some things directly, such as general ideas. It perceives individual sensory things through the sense organs. The sense organ changes when the object specific to it affects it, and the soul perceives the object through that change. Awareness is the presence of the object’s form in what perceives it. So a general idea is present in the soul itself, while an individual sensory form is present in the sense organ.

For this reason, every sense organ must be affected by the kind of object that belongs to it. But the senses differ in whether the soul feels the organ’s change. When the tongue, nose, or skin changes through taste, smell, or touch, the soul feels that change and can take pleasure or pain in it.

A person feels the pleasure of sweetness in the mouth, a pleasant smell in the nose, and softness in the organ of touch. But they do not feel the pleasure of a beautiful image in the eye’s lens or at the meeting point of the optic nerves. Nor do they feel the pleasure of a sound in the auditory nerve, even though the eye and ear are affected by what they sense.

Some say this is because taste, smell, and touch affect their organs over a period of time, while sight and hearing affect theirs in an instant. This is not convincing. All five senses perceive in an instant, and no one has proved that some take time while others do not. Sometimes an image remains in the eye after looking at a bright light or strong color, and sometimes a sound seems to remain in the ear. But these are rare cases.

Al-Shīrāzī’s point is that the first three organs are affected long enough for the soul to feel their change and take pleasure or pain in it. The eye and ear, he says, are affected too briefly for this to happen. This explanation is weak, because it rests on an unproved difference in how long the senses take to perceive.

Avicenna does not deny that the eye and ear take on the qualities they see and hear. He says that they do not feel pleasure or pain as seeing and hearing, whereas the other three senses take pleasure or pain as they taste, smell and touch. The explanation just given locates the difference in the soul’s awareness of each organ’s change.

Al-Shīrāzī next answers al-Masīḥī’s second objection. Avicenna does not say that touch in the eye or ear perceives color or sound. Sight perceives color and hearing perceives sound. The organ’s sense of touch suffers instead from the bodily disturbance caused by excessive light or loud sound. The powers that perceive the color or sound are not the same as the power that suffers the bodily injury.

The organs of sight and hearing do not suffer from what they see or hear simply because they see or hear it. This is not because their perception happens in an instant rather than over time; that explanation has already proved unsound.

Al-Shīrāzī answers the third objection by pointing out that it assumes two things Avicenna does not accept: that what suits sight is the act of seeing an object, and that touch in the eye perceives color. Avicenna says that what truly suits the powers belongs to the soul, not to the senses as physical organs. The eye’s touch is hurt by an intense color; sight itself perceives the color.

The fourth objection assumes that, if some senses feel pleasure or pain and others do not, the difference must be arbitrary. Al-Shīrāzī denies this. The soul feels pleasure or pain from taste, smell, and touch where their organs are affected by what they sense. Sight and hearing do not cause pleasure or pain in the same way.

Al-Shīrāzī rejects al-Masīḥī’s own answer because suitability belongs to the soul, not to the sense powers as such. The powers do not perceive, so we cannot say that their perception is their pleasure. He also criticizes the way al-Masīḥī answered the four objections.

I have several replies to al-Shīrāzī. First, it is not always true that only the soul feels pleasure and pain. The soul has different levels and can become one with the intellect or with the senses. When it becomes one with the intellect, it acts as intellect does; when it works through the senses, it acts as they do. Each sense has its own kind of activity, and pleasure comes from sensing what suits it. So sight and hearing should also take pleasure in the objects that suit them, just as the other senses do.

Second, al-Shīrāzī’s difference between the first three senses and sight and hearing does not explain why pleasure belongs to some senses but not others. He says the soul feels pleasure when the first three organs change, but not when the organs of sight and hearing change. Even if this is true, it does not explain the difference. Pleasure follows awareness. If the soul is aware, pleasure belongs to the soul; if the sense is aware, pleasure belongs to the sense. The length of time the organ changes does not settle the question.

Third, the senses perceive by taking on the form of what they sense. Al-Shīrāzī agrees that the eye and ear also take on the forms of what they sense. If this change is perception, then sight and hearing perceive their objects and should take pleasure in them. What reason is there to say they do not?

Fourth, it is wrong to deny that what suits sight is the perception of what is visible. What suits each power is what matches it. Color and light are a perfection for the transparent part of the eye. The eye itself has both sight and touch. Excessive light or color can affect it in two ways: sight perceives the light or color, and touch feels the bodily effect. There is no contradiction, because these are two different powers, perceiving different things.

Fifth, al-Shīrāzī’s final attempt to reject al-Masīḥī’s correct position is not right. Al-Masīḥī was right, and the attempt to overturn his answer was wrong. Avicenna was also right, but al-Masīḥī could not understand his meaning or resolve the difficulty. I am astonished that these three accomplished scholars—al-Masīḥī, al-Rāzī and al-Shīrāzī—and the later commentators on the *Canon* could not resolve what Avicenna said, despite all their careful examination. Praise belongs to God for granting us His grace and wisdom.

Here is how I understand the issue. An animal’s body is made what it is by its bodily mixture, formed from the basic qualities felt by touch: hot and cold, wet and dry. Each animal, and each of its organs, has a particular balance of these qualities. Its health and its breakdown depend on whether that balance is kept. The sense of touch is spread through the body and its parts, including the organs of sight, hearing, taste, and smell.

Pleasure is awareness of what suits us because it suits us; pain is awareness of what opposes us because it opposes us. What suits a thing is a perfection for it. A thing’s perfection must be of the same kind as its own being, and its opposite belongs to the nearest kind of being that can oppose it.

Feeling a touch that suits the body’s balance is a perfection of the sense of touch, which runs through the body. So the sense itself feels pleasure, and the soul feels pleasure through it. Feeling what opposes that balance causes pain in the same way.

After touch, taste helps keep the animal body alive and lets it grow. The body depends on food with tastes that suit it, and the sense of taste has its own kind of perfection and pleasure. Unpleasant tastes oppose that perfection and cause pain. Above these two comes a further perfection in the things we smell. The body’s dense organs do not need smell as much for their survival, but its finer parts, including its vapor-like spirits, receive nourishment through it. The sense of smell therefore finds pleasure in sweet scents and pain in foul ones. All three qualities—touch, taste, and smell—belong to the animal body and its organs.

Sight and hearing are different. An animal’s body and its organs are not made of colors, light, or sound. Light and its colors belong only to the body’s outer surfaces. Sound is even further removed from the body that produces it. So color and sound are not bodily qualities whose presence completes the eye or ear as physical organs.

But the soul can take pleasure in beautiful colors and sounds through its powers of sight and hearing. It can also feel pain from darkness, unpleasant colors, and harsh sounds. These qualities complete the soul’s powers of sight and hearing, not the eye and ear as parts of the body.

An animal dies if it is deprived of touch for an hour or goes without food for a day or two. Going without smell for a time also harms it. This is not true of darkness or silence alone, except in the human case. A human being’s soul belongs to a world of light and to the ordered relations of number and sound. So it takes pleasure in seeing light and hearing measured music, and suffers in darkness and from harsh colors.

The eye is harmed by intense light or color, on this account, because the intervening air touching it carries extreme heat or cold. The ear is harmed by loud sound because the force of the sound strikes the air against it. Avicenna hints at these causes. The harm comes through touch, not through sight or hearing as such.

In short, what suits or opposes the sense of touch, taste, and smell also suits or opposes the body and its organs. These senses therefore have pleasure and pain in their own objects. What sight and hearing perceive does not suit or oppose the eye and ear as bodily organs. It suits or opposes the soul through its powers of sight and hearing. So pleasure and pain in sight and hearing belong to the soul through those powers, not to the eye and ear as organs. This is my conclusion on the matter, and God is the giver of grace and gifts.

#### IX. Health and Illness as Qualities of the Soul

Avicenna defines health at the start of the *Canon* as a state or settled condition that lets the actions of the thing it belongs to come out sound and undamaged. He uses “or” here not because he is unsure, but to show that health is a quality of the soul whether it is temporary or firmly established. Some people mistakenly say it must be a settled condition. In *The Healing*, Avicenna does call it a settled condition of an animal’s body that allows its natural and other actions to be sound. But we need not take the word “or” as uncertainty about what health is. Al-Rāzī argues that any uncertainty concerns only the accidental difference between a lasting habit and a passing state, not what constitutes health itself. Yet, as we have seen, this distinction too needs examination.

The definition covers humans and other animals. Al-Rāzī says it also covers the health of plants, whose actions of drawing in nourishment, digesting it, and getting rid of waste may be sound. This is not right. “State” and “settled condition” in this definition are qualities of animals, which have souls; they are not meant to include plants. Otherwise, the definition in *The Healing* would repeat itself.

One could escape the difficulty about plants by using “state” and “habit” for any quality, whether lasting or temporary, or by including plant souls within the meaning of “soul.” But both uses depart from the accepted meanings of the terms.

Elsewhere in the *Canon*, Avicenna defines health as a condition in which the human body’s mixture and structure let all its actions come out sound. This definition is based on health as it is discussed in medicine: health in the human body. “Sound actions” means actions that follow the natural course and have no defect. So defining the health of a body or organ by the soundness of its actions does not define health by health itself. Some accordingly point out that soundness in actions is something we can perceive, while health in a body is not directly perceived; there is no difficulty in explaining what is not directly perceived by what is.

Some object that the definition says actions “come from” the body, as if the state or settled condition caused the actions. But it also says that the actions come “from the thing it belongs to,” as if the body itself were the cause. There are two possible answers. One is that health is the highest-level cause, while the body is the thing able to act. The meaning is: a quality that lets the sound actions belonging to the body come from it. Another is that the body acts as an instrument through which health produces the actions.

The second answer is not satisfactory if it makes the body an active cause and the soul’s power merely its tool. The body, simply as a body, receives effects; it does not act. The better explanation is that powers that work through the body act together with the bodily matter, because their actions, like their existence, depend on that matter. Fire, for example, warms through its own nature. Health is the cause that lets the body produce sound actions. This meaning is clear in the second definition in the *Canon*, and even clearer in Avicenna’s wording in *The Healing*.

Avicenna defines illness as a condition opposed to health: a state or settled condition that makes the body’s actions faulty. In *The Healing*, he says that illness, strictly speaking, is an absence. He does not mean an imbalance or pain, but the loss of health. This seems to make health and illness opposites in two different ways.

Some scholars resolve the problem by saying that illness has two meanings. If it means the absence of health, illness and health are like absence and presence. If it means the faulty condition that causes actions to fail, it is a real quality opposed to health. In that case, Avicenna uses the word “illness” in two related ways. Otherwise, the problem remains.

Another explanation is that health and illness are opposites in two different senses: in strict philosophical use, health is present and illness is its absence; in common speech, they are two qualities that oppose each other. Avicenna seems to support this when he says that, in ordinary accounts, one opposite can be the absence of another. For example, stillness can be the absence of movement, and illness the absence of health. Yet he also says that illness is a condition opposed to health, which sounds like a real quality. Since they differ greatly, it is possible to treat them as two contrary qualities within the same general kind, the qualities of the soul.

Al-Rāzī objects that the main kinds of illness are bodily imbalance, faulty structure, and a break in continuity. None belongs to the group of qualities called states and habits. If illness is not in this group, then health cannot be its opposite within the same group either.

His account of these kinds is as follows. Bodily imbalance is either a foreign quality that changes the body’s usual mixture, such as a fever, or the body’s being affected by that quality. The first is a felt quality; the second is a bodily change. Faulty structure may involve size, number, position, shape, or a blocked passage that interferes with the body’s work. These belong to different groups: size and number are quantities; position is its own group; shape is a quality of size; and being affected is another group. A break in continuity is an absence and so does not belong to any group of qualities. Since illness does not belong to states and habits, al-Rāzī argues, health cannot belong to them either, since it is illness’s opposite.

That is his full argument. It is not adequately represented by the shortened account in *The Positions*, which says only that faulty bodily mixture, faulty structure, and broken continuity belong to sensed qualities, position, or absence. The shortening leaves out essential parts of the division. Nor does it help to say that the other possibilities were omitted because they were clearly false. Size, number, position and a blocked passage are not merely possible answers that may be dismissed; they are distinct kinds of faulty structure.

The answer is easy. This way of dividing illness is loose. It names the causes of illness, not illness itself. Illness is a quality of the soul that arises from one of these causes, and its kinds are named according to what caused them. People also call a body’s balanced mixture “health,” even though that mixture is a quality we sense by touch, not a quality of the soul. The word “health” in that case names the cause of health.

#### X. The State Between Health and Illness

People disagree about whether there is a state between health and illness. This is not about whether there are states, such as knowledge, power, or life, that are neither healthy nor ill. Those clearly exist. The question is whether a body can be in a condition that is neither healthy nor ill—that is, whether we can say that it is neither healthy nor sick.

Galen says there is such a condition. Examples include people recovering from illness, older people, children, or a person who has an injury in one part of the body but not another.

Avicenna replies that those who find a middle state have forgotten the conditions needed to decide whether two states can have a middle. We must look at the same subject, at the same time, in the same part, and from the same point of view. If we look at one person, one organ, and one time, it may be balanced in its bodily mixture but faulty in its structure, or unbalanced in its mixture but well formed in its structure. In either case, all the actions that belong to that organ may still be sound—or they may not. This gives us a middle state. But if we define the organ as either balanced or unbalanced in its bodily mixture, with no third option, then there is no middle.

Avicenna’s definition of illness assumes that not all the actions of an organ are sound: either health is absent, or some actions are faulty. On that definition, there is no middle. But if illness means a condition in which all the organ’s actions—natural, animal, and mental—are faulty, then there clearly is a middle when some actions are sound and others are not. A middle is even clearer if we consider all the body’s organs. The disagreement therefore depends on how illness is defined.

Al-Rāzī says the disagreement may be only about words. Those who deny a middle define health as an organ, or several organs, working properly at a given time or over several times; illness means that they do not. Those who accept a middle define health as all the organs working properly, and illness as all of them working badly. On this second definition, a middle exists when some organs work well and others do not.

Avicenna’s opening remarks in the *Canon* suggest that the disagreement depends on how health is defined. He says we should not add a second condition unless we are defining health as we please and adding requirements that are not needed. These include requiring every action to be sound, every organ to be sound, the condition to hold at every time, or health to have no near danger of ending. These restrictions exclude people whose actions are sound in some ways but not others, people with some healthy organs and some unhealthy ones, people who are healthy in winter but ill in summer, and the health of older people, children, or people recovering from illness.

#### XI. Joy, Grief, and Other Emotions

The soul can take on states that follow from its response to things its powers recognize as helpful or harmful. Joy and grief are two such states. Joy is a state of the soul that moves the bodily spirit outward, seeking what gives pleasure. Grief moves it inward, out of fear of harm that has come upon it. Desire moves it outward to draw in what suits us and gives pleasure. Anger moves it outward to push away what opposes us and seek revenge. Fright draws it inward from something harmful, whether real or imagined.[^V04-48]

Sadness moves the bodily spirit inward little by little. Worry moves it both inward and outward when something happens that may bring good or threaten harm. It combines fear and hope. Whichever one weighs more heavily in the mind draws the soul in its direction: toward the outside for expected good, and inward for feared harm. That is why worry has been called a struggle of thought. Shame also moves the spirit inward and outward. It begins like fright, with the spirit drawing inward; then the person thinks the matter will cause little harm, and the spirit opens out again.

These descriptions point to the effects that follow each state. The meanings themselves are clear to the mind. People often speak loosely and call the bodily reaction itself the feeling: joy is the heart opening, grief is its tightening, anger is the blood boiling in the heart, and happiness is the heart and blood expanding. This is mistaken. Each feeling is a state of the soul. It brings about changes in the body through a fine, vapor-like substance that responds to the soul.

A lasting grudge occurs only when anger lasts, and when revenge is neither extremely easy nor extremely difficult. Anger must last. If it quickly fades, the image of the injury will not remain in the imagination, and so it will not keep drawing the soul toward revenge.

Revenge must not be too easy for two reasons. First, if it is easy, the soul turns to the act of revenge. While it is occupied with action, it cannot hold on to another image. Second, if the desire for revenge becomes strong and the person has nothing to fear, an easy victory can seem already won in the imagination. We do not long for what seems to have already happened. That is why people feel little desire to take revenge on someone weak, when doing so would be easy.

This shows that imagination responds to appearances and likenesses, not only to what is real. A person may recoil from honey if it is compared to a bitter emetic, or from food and drink if their appearance resembles something filthy. In the same way, what is easy to obtain can seem already obtained, and then we no longer long for it.

But revenge must not be impossibly difficult either; it must seem within reach. If the person who caused the harm is very powerful, like a king, despair and fear can prevent the desire for revenge from taking hold. Resentment, then, arises when anger lasts and revenge seems neither too easy nor beyond reach.

#### XII. Causes of Joy and Grief

God created, by His power, a subtle spiritual body before these dense, dark bodies. It has levels that differ in refinement, like the levels of the heavens. At its three levels it is called the mental spirit, the vital spirit, and the natural spirit. Because it is fine and lies between intellects and material bodies, it serves as a vehicle for the soul’s powers, carrying them through the body’s organs. The soul’s powers first connect to this spirit, then reach the bodily organs through it.

The spirit is made from the fine, vapor-like part of the bodily fluids, just as the body is made from their dense, earthy part. The spirit relates to the refined part of these fluids as the body relates to their coarse part. The body’s organs take shape from the fluids when they mix and form one balanced state. That balance prepares them to receive conditions that they do not get from the elements alone. In the same way, the refined part of the fluids forms the spirits so they can receive powers of the soul that the simple elements do not provide.

God’s generosity has no limit. He does not favor one person over another or one moment over another. The difference comes from what is ready to receive His gift—the condition of each body and the causes acting on it—not from God. So we must learn what prepares the soul for these states, without imagining that God or the attributes that are His by nature change.

Philosophers and physicians agree that joy, grief, fear, and anger follow changes in the bodily spirit. It rises from the left chamber of the heart, with its finer part moving upward to the brain and its denser part moving down to the liver and other organs.

Each of these feelings grows stronger or weaker according to the readiness of the person who experiences it, not simply according to what caused it. A power may be present but still far from being put to use; readiness is nearer to action. Everyone has the power to feel joy and grief, but some people are more ready for joy and others for grief. Readiness is a power brought close to one of two opposing states. We will now look at what prepares someone for joy.

There are three such conditions. The first is that the bodily spirit be in its best state, in both amount and quality. It must be abundant. This strengthens its power and leaves enough of it at its source while the rest spreads out during joy. If it is scarce, the body’s natural power quickly uses it up or keeps it from spreading. In quality, it should be neither too fine nor too thick, and it should be very bright. This makes it resemble the substance of the heavens. These qualities prepare a person for pleasure and joy, just as pleasure is the broader kind and joy one of its forms. When the spirit in the heart is abundant, balanced, and bright, a person is strongly disposed to joy.

The opposite conditions prepare a person for grief: too little spirit, as in people recovering from illness, people weakened by disease, and older people; too much thickness, as in those described as melancholic and in older people, which makes it hard to expand; too much thinness, which does not sustain expansion; or darkness, as in those described as melancholic.

The second condition is the influence of things outside us. These are many. Avicenna says that some are strong and some weak, some familiar and some unfamiliar. We may stop noticing a thing if we experience it often. The strong, obvious causes of joy or grief need no explanation. Less obvious examples include seeing the bright world around us. Avicenna argues that this must be pleasant because its opposite—living in darkness—is distressing. He also mentions seeing a pleasing form, with loneliness as its opposite.

This argument is not sound. One thing’s having a certain quality does not prove that its opposite has the opposite quality. Avicenna himself says in his book on argument that this is a common claim, but false. So the fact that darkness is distressing does not prove that seeing the bright world is pleasant.

Other sources of joy include getting what we want at the right time and being able to carry out our plans without interruption; making plans and hoping for things; remembering the past and looking forward to the future; daydreaming; conversation; finding things strange or unfamiliar; wonder and admiration; having a good listener nearby; receiving help; deceiving or misleading others; and winning, even in a small matter.

The sources of grief are the opposite of these: remembering dangers and pains we have suffered, old resentments, and upsetting dealings with others; imagining dangers ahead, especially the certainty that we will leave this world; thinking about the duties of the next life that we must prepare for; being cut off from our work; unexpected thoughts; and failing to get what we want. Many other things can cause grief too.

These things bring grief to a soul that is already disposed toward it. In people described as melancholic, imagination can add to the grief by presenting likenesses of frightening or painful things. In this account, imagination is stronger in them because the bodily spirit is dry and moves slowly, while the mind turns away from the inner powers because the spirit that carries them is in poor condition. Their movements then follow the pattern set by that dark, harmful state.

The third condition is repetition. Repeated joy prepares the soul for more joy, and repeated grief prepares it for more grief. Avicenna says that when we repeat an action that has an opposite, our power to do it grows stronger; as the power grows, so does our readiness.

He supports this in two ways. First, by looking at familiar cases: a body heated again and again becomes easier to heat, just as repeated cooling, loosening, or tightening prepares it to happen again. The inner powers also form a lasting habit through repeated actions and feelings, which were at first only passing states. This is how we acquire character.

Second, he reasons from what we observe. Every change that happens to a thing suits its nature, and what suits one thing works against its opposite. When that change happens again and again, it weakens the thing’s readiness for the opposite and strengthens its readiness for what suits it. This is the reasoning from observation and comparison.

A fuller proof would take us beyond this kind of argument. The question is not so important that we must settle it with the strongest possible proof, even though such a proof could be given. Evidence that makes the conclusion likely is enough here. Avicenna offers a natural explanation: joy is followed by two things. First, it strengthens the body’s natural power. Second, it expands the bodily spirit, because joy moves the soul outward through the spirit that carries its powers.

The strengthening of the body’s natural power has three effects: the spirit becomes more balanced; the body produces more to replace what is used up; and the body is protected from losing too much. The expansion of the spirit has two effects. Because the spirit is fine, it becomes ready to move and spread. As it spreads away from the source of nourishment, it draws along the material behind it. Every movement of this kind pulls what follows it, because the layers of bodies remain joined and empty space cannot form between them. In general, what comes after is drawn along as what came before flows outward, as happens with wind and water.

So repeated joy prepares the spirit for more joy. Repeated grief strengthens the power to grieve because it brings about the opposite of the two effects of joy: it weakens the body’s natural power, and the spirit thickens and cools as its inborn heat fades under strong inward contraction. The other effects are the opposite of those caused by joy. We can therefore conclude that repeated joy prepares the spirit for joy, and repeated grief prepares it for grief. Someone disposed to joy is affected by grief only when its cause is strong, but even a slight cause can bring them joy. For someone disposed to grief, the reverse is true.

#### XIII. Weakness and Strength of Heart, Distress and Vigor

There is a state we can call weakness of heart, and another we can call distress or heaviness of heart. They are similar, but not the same. There is also strength of heart and a state of vigor or openness of heart. These, too, are similar but different. It is hard to tell them apart because each pair often goes together. The first two may seem like passive feelings, and the second two like active states.

First, they do not always go together. Not everyone with a weak heart is sad, and not everyone who is sad has a weak heart. The same is true of strength of heart and joy: not everyone strong-hearted is joyful, and not everyone joyful is strong-hearted.

Second, they differ in what they are about. Weakness of heart concerns a feared danger and a person’s ability to bear it. Distress concerns something that troubles the soul and a person’s ability to bear that. The feared danger is a physical harm; what causes distress is a harm to the soul.

Third, their effects on the soul differ. Weakness of heart inclines a person to flee. Distress may instead lead them to push back or fight, the opposite of fleeing. Strength of heart often fades when a person is afraid, but distress can stir them to action. Weakness of heart involves two reactions: suffering the threat and wanting to move away from it. Distress involves only the suffering. It does not, by itself, create a natural urge to flee. A person may choose to withdraw for another reason, or may choose to resist and fight.

Fourth, their effects on the body differ. When a person with a weak heart faces what they fear, the body’s inborn heat fades and cold takes over. Distress, by contrast, often causes the inborn heat to flare.

Fifth, the bodily conditions that prepare a person for each state differ. Weakness of heart may follow from a spirit that is too thin and a cold bodily mixture. Distress may follow from a thick spirit and a warm bodily mixture.

#### XIV. How Emotions Affect the Body

The different worlds reflect one another. Whatever happens in one gives rise to something like it in another.[^V04-49] The human being is a small world with three levels: the soul at the top, the body at the bottom, and the spirit in between. The body includes the blood, which is the fitting bodily mixture that sustains it. The soul affects the spirit, and through the spirit its effect reaches the body. In the other direction, bodily changes reach the soul through the spirit. The soul and body mirror each other in this way because each can be a cause or an effect of the other. Just as their substances reflect one another, so do their qualities, feelings, and changes. The spirit stands between them.

When a state of the soul arises, such as pleasure—whether from understanding or imagination, and therefore a complete idea or image—the balanced spirit in the brain expands. Through it, the body trembles.

The clear blood then becomes visible, and the face turns red. When fear or pain arises in the soul, the spirit contracts inward. Through it, the body tightens and the face turns pale. The same pattern applies to the other states of the soul and their effects on the body.

The spirit carries the soul’s powers, and the blood carries the spirit. The blood moves with the spirit, sometimes outward and sometimes inward, either all at once or little by little. Outward movement may come from pleasure, vigor, anger, hope, or expectation. Inward movement may come from pain, sadness, fear, the wish to flee, thought, or similar states.

These inward and outward movements differ in speed, in how much the body opens or contracts, and in qualities such as the heat and redness of the blood. Their changes also depend on how strong or weak the feeling is. In intense anger, the spirit and the blood that carries it may rush outward at once. The spirit’s supply may be cut off, or its heat may be smothered by the congestion, and the person may die. With moderate joy, the movement is gradual. With overwhelming joy, it may be slow but so great that the spirit is lost altogether.

The same applies to inward movement, in reverse. Fright may draw the spirit inward all at once, while sadness draws it in little by little. A person may also move in both directions at once if two feelings arise together. Worry, for example, may involve both anger and sadness, pulling the movements in opposite directions. Shame first draws the spirit inward. Then the mind returns to the matter, judges that it is not so harmful, and releases the spirit outward again, making the face red.

#### XV. How Emotions Relate to the Blood

Blood carries the bodily spirit, which carries the effects of these feelings. Avicenna says that abundant, clear blood with a balanced consistency and temperament prepares a person for joy, because it produces plenty of bright spirit. If the blood is still abundant and clear but is too hot, it prepares a person for anger, because it catches fire easily and moves quickly. If it is abundant and clear but thin, cold, and watery, it prepares a person for cowardice and weakness of heart. The spirit it produces moves outward slowly and catches fire less easily because it is cold and moist. So it is less ready for joy or anger. Its thinness also makes it easy to lose, and its coldness means the body produces less of it.

Murky, thick blood that is too hot prepares a person for grief and lasting anger. It produces a murky spirit, which brings grief. Its heat makes it flare into anger, and its thickness means it does not cool quickly once heated.

Anger from thin, yellowish blood rises and fades more quickly, because the spirit produced from it is hotter but not thick. If the blood is bright and clear, the person is also more cheerful. In rare cases, thick blood that is not dark and is too hot makes a person neither sad nor fearful, but brave and strong-hearted. Their anger is less strong because cheerfulness tempers it. Grief, by contrast, can prepare a person for anger: anger pushes away what threatens us, while cheerfulness and pleasure draw us toward what we like. Even so, this person may become very angry over serious matters as the spirit heats up, and for the same reason is often not easily frightened.

Thick blood that is not dark but too cold makes a person neither sad nor cheerful. Their anger is not intense, and they may be somewhat timid and dull, but safe from harm. Their spirit resembles their blood. Thick, murky blood that is too cold makes a person withdrawn and sad, with anger that stays quiet unless something serious happens. Their anger lasts less long than that of someone with a warm temperament and otherwise similar traits, but longer than that of someone whose blood is thin. They are also inclined to hold a grudge.

Avicenna explains why wine brings strong joy and why people described as melancholic feel strong grief. The first reason is that a moderate amount of wine produces abundant spirit with a balanced consistency and great brightness. This is the first cause of joy. When the spirit is abundant, balanced, and bright, even a small cause can stir it to joy. A person ready for something needs only a small cause, just as tinder catches fire from a tiny flame when wood needs much more. That is why someone who drinks may seem to rejoice for no reason. But an effect cannot happen without a cause.

The second reason is that the spirits in the brain become very moist and turbulent from the rising, unsettled vapors. Because of this moisture, they do not respond well to the mind’s fine movements; because of their turbulence, they do not take shape well under the mind’s direction. The mind then finds it hard to use them for thought and turns away until their condition improves and their movement settles.

When the mind uses these spirits less, they are more open to outside influences. This is why the drinker responds more to things that bring pleasure than to what is truly good, more to immediate benefits than to future ones, and more to what seems good than to what reason judges good. The third reason is that the outward senses can move the inner spirit more easily than the mind can. When the mind cannot direct the inner spirit, it uses the senses to guide it, as we do when we use diagrams in geometry. So future, moral, and intellectual pleasures have less effect on the drinker, while immediate pleasures of the senses take over. Because the spirit is so ready, even a small cause is enough, as it is for a child. The drinker may seem to rejoice without a cause, but that is impossible. Three things come together in the drinker.

First, the spirit reaches its full development in amount and quality. Second, rational thoughts that might bring grief are driven away. Third, imagination and thought turn toward outside sensations, which bring pleasure. These three things complete the drinker’s joy and strengthen their energy.

A person of weak judgment may use these facts as a reason to drink. A thoughtful person may see some or all of them as reasons to avoid it, even without knowing the divine law. The law states plainly and strongly that drinking is forbidden and shameful. Human beings are made for the next life. The mind reaches its perfection by leaving this life behind and returning to God and His unseen realm. It does so by contemplating lasting truths and taking joy in them, not by pursuing passing sensations and pleasures.

The second case is the grief of a person described as melancholic. Their condition is the opposite. The spirit is small in amount and weak in quality, so it is readily affected by harmful and distressing things. Their imagination turns toward distant future events because the spirit in the middle chamber of the brain moves more slowly as it dries. This dryness comes, in the account given here, from the bodily humor called black bile. Because the imagination is strong, it builds likenesses of frightening and painful things, as if they were happening before the person’s eyes. This makes the grief grow stronger.

I have taken most of this discussion from Avicenna’s account of heart medicines. These questions may fit more naturally in the study of bodies, since they concern what happens to the body when it is affected. But I have discussed them here because I am tracing the nature of the soul’s states, their features, and their bodily counterparts. This concludes the discussion of this group of qualities.

### Section Four: Qualities of quantity: three articles and two preliminary questions

This section has three articles. Before beginning them, I will take up two questions.

The first is what this kind of quality is. It is a quality that belongs first to size or number, and through them to a body. Someone might object that a body’s overall appearance is made up of its shape and color, and that appearance belongs first to a natural body. Without a body, there would be no appearance. The answer is that some features belong to quantity itself, while others belong to quantity because it is the size or number of a particular thing. The hollow shape of a particular body part, such as the nose, is an example. Many philosophers count both kinds as features of quantity.

I take a different view. States of the soul plainly do not belong to size or number. Nor do a thing’s readiness or lack of readiness belong to its size. They belong to matter as it receives a form. The qualities we sense also arise when material bodies are affected simply as matter. So the qualities that belong specifically to quantity must be ones that involve no such physical change.

This view raises two problems. First, overall appearance seems to have no single, real unity, because it combines two things: shape, a quality that belongs to size, and color, a quality we sense. Second, color is first found on a surface. A body is not colored by itself; to say that it is colored means that its surface is colored. So color and light seem to belong among the qualities of quantity, since their first subject is a surface. But they are also counted among the qualities we sense. Then one and the same thing would belong to two kinds, which is impossible. Some thinkers have found this problem difficult. I say that color and light belong to a surface because of the particular condition of the body beneath it, not because it is a surface as such. If they belonged to a surface simply because it is a surface, every surface would be colored and bright. That is not the case.

The second question concerns the kinds of quality that belong to quantity. They are commonly divided into four groups. The first includes shape and angle. A shape is fully enclosed by one or more boundaries. An angle is formed by two lines that meet at a point, or by surfaces that meet at a point or along a line without fully enclosing a space. The second group includes features that are neither shapes nor angles, such as straightness and circularity. The third is what people call overall appearance, made by combining color and shape. The fourth concerns number: whether it is even or odd, is a square or has a square root, or is a cube.

There are six important questions to consider here: what all these qualities have in common; whether the usual definition is sound; whether shape is a quality rather than a position; what kind of quality an angle is; how overall appearance can belong to one kind when people say it has several parts; and how to classify anything that seems to belong to two kinds at once.

We have already touched on the first question. The definition includes shape, straightness, curvature, flatness, convexity, cube-like form, hollowness, and being even or odd. We have also answered the objection based on color. The objection based on overall appearance can be answered by saying that it has no single, real unity: anything with no real unity may be counted under more than one kind. But Avicenna did not take this route, since it would mean that overall appearance itself belongs to this group, even though one of its parts belongs to another group.

Avicenna says that some features belong to quantity itself, whether or not it is the quantity of a particular thing. Others belong to quantity only because it is the quantity of something. In that case, quantity is still the first subject of the feature, and the thing whose size or number it is comes after. The fact that a feature belongs to quantity only when quantity is in a body does not mean that it belongs first to the body. For example, forgetfulness occurs in the soul only while the soul is in a body. That does not mean forgetfulness belongs first to the body and reaches the soul through it. By contrast, motion belongs first to the body and is then attributed to some powers of the soul. Color, in the usual account, belongs first to the surface.

Natural science has shown that a body is not colored in itself. To say it is colored means that its surface is colored. Overall appearance, then, combines two things. Shape belongs first to the surface itself, or to what the surface encloses. Color belongs to the surface when it is the outer boundary of a natural body. Both therefore belong first to quantity, and are attributed to the body through it. That is Avicenna’s argument. I have already explained the problem with it. The remaining questions will be discussed in later chapters.

### Article One: Straightness and Circularity

#### I. What They Are

A straight line has been defined as one whose every point lies in the same direction, with none of its parts higher or lower than another. This definition is attributed to Euclid. It has also been defined as the shortest line between two points, a definition attributed to Archimedes.

The problem with Euclid’s definition is that it seems to define straightness by repeating the idea of straightness: saying that every point lies along one direction means that the line is straight. The problem with Archimedes’ definition is different. A straight line cannot become curved. If it cannot become curved, the two cannot be laid exactly on top of one another. And if they cannot be matched in this way, how can we say that one is shorter or longer than the other? I will return to this point.

The truth is that what we are trying to define is already clear. These definitions only draw attention to some of its features. Another definition says that a straight line is one whose parts match one another in every position. Parts of a curved line may match in one position but not in another.

A fourth definition says that if the two ends are fixed and the line is twisted, its position does not change. A curved arc behaves differently: twisting it changes which way its bulging side faces. A fifth definition says that a straight line is one whose middle can hide one end from a viewer looking with one eye while the other eye is closed. The objections to each definition have already been answered. All these tests, except the fourth, also apply to a flat surface.

#### II. What a Circle Is, and How We Know It Exists

Qualities that belong to quantity can be divided into those that belong to separate numbers and those that belong to continuous sizes. Some number features are obvious, such as being even or odd. Others must be proved by arithmetic. They are features of number, which is itself an attribute; a feature of something that is already an attribute is even more clearly an attribute.

Features that belong to size are not all obvious. A geometer cannot prove that every one exists. The geometer may take some from the philosopher as a starting point, then prove others from them, as happens in arguments where one premise has already been established elsewhere. Other shapes are proved by assuming that a circle exists. The triangle, the first plane shape, exists if a circle does; so do the square, the pentagon, and other plane and solid shapes.

A sphere can be formed by turning a circle inside another circle. A cylinder can be formed by moving a circle while its center follows a straight line. A cone can be formed by moving a right triangle around one of its sides while the end of that side stays at the center of the circle and the other side traces its boundary.

A circle is a flat surface enclosed by one line, with a point inside it from which every straight line to the boundary has the same length. The point need not be inside the circle for the definition to work. It would also be correct to say that a circle is a surface enclosed by one line such that a point can be found from which all straight lines to the boundary are equal.

There is no doubt that straight lines exist. But many people who believe that bodies are made of indivisible parts have denied that circles exist. So philosophers, not geometers alone, must prove that they do. They have three arguments.

First, imagine a flat surface with a line drawn on it. Keep one end of the line fixed and move the other around until it returns to where it began. The moving end traces a circle. The path it traces has no width, so it is a line; and every point on it is the same distance from the fixed end, since the line stays the same length. This proves that the circle exists.

Second, the natural shape of simple bodies, as we will see, is spherical. Whenever a sphere is cut by a flat surface, the cut forms a circle.

Third, imagine a heavy body that is heavier at one end than the other. Stand it upright on a flat surface, balanced on its lighter end, and then tilt it until it falls. Its lower end touches the surface at a point. Ask whether that point stays fixed as the body moves. If it does, every point in the upper part of the body traces a quarter circle.

If the contact point does not stay fixed, then either the upper end rises as the lower end moves downward, or it does not. If the upper end rises, every point in the two halves of the body—the rising half and the falling half—traces a circle. These circles surround one another and share a center at the point between the two halves. If it does not rise, it must slide across the surface while staying some distance from the lower end. Then the upper end would trace a curved line that is not a circle.

That last possibility cannot happen by itself, and it cannot be forced. The only force here is the weight of the upper end, which moves downward. It does not push the lower end sideways. If it did push it while keeping the body together, it would push it against the upper end’s downward motion and make it rise. That would return us to the previous case. So the only possible paths are circles.

Once a circle is established, so are triangles, right angles, squares, rectangles, and other plane shapes. Solid shapes follow as well: a sphere from a circle, cones from triangles, cubes from squares, and cylinders from rectangles. A curved shape can also be formed by cutting a cone or cylinder with a sloping flat surface, one that is neither parallel to its base nor at a right angle to it.

Proving that circles exist also challenges those who say bodies are made of indivisible parts. Geometry can show that the square of a square’s diagonal equals twice the square of its side. The ratio between them cannot be expressed as a ratio of whole numbers. This kind of irrational ratio appears in many sizes, but cannot be accounted for if every size is built from indivisible units. Geometry can also divide a line so that its total length multiplied by one part equals the square of the other part. No whole-number ratio can meet that condition in every case; it works only for certain numbers.

#### III. Straight and Curved Lines Differ in Kind

We must now ask how straightness and circularity differ, and in what sense a quality can distinguish one kind of quantity from another. Straight and curved lines clearly differ. Their difference must either come from some outside feature, or from what each line is in itself. It cannot come from an outside feature.

A line that is straight either can or cannot remain the very same line when its straightness disappears and curvature takes its place. There is no third option. It cannot remain the same line in both states. A line is the boundary of a surface, just as a surface is the boundary of a body. A boundary cannot change without the thing it bounds changing too. So a line cannot change from straight to circular unless the shape of its surface changes; and the surface cannot change unless the body spreads or contracts. A sphere is not a cube, and a circle is not a square. When the underlying thing changes in number, its feature changes in number as well.

Since the same line cannot remain while its straightness disappears, straightness must either define the kind of line it is or follow from what defines it. The same is true of a curved line. Its curvature, and the degree of that curvature, must be part of what it is or follow from it. Circles of different sizes therefore differ in kind. The same reasoning applies to flat and curved surfaces. Their flatness or degree of bulging is part of what they are, not just a feature added from outside. Spherical surfaces of different sizes differ in kind too.

When a body bends after being straight, its continuity must break. As it spreads or contracts, the surface is divided into surfaces; and when a flat surface is twisted, the line is divided into lines. Dividing a size destroys its unity. The same surface cannot be both spread out and drawn in, nor the same line both long and short, nor the same mathematical body large and small. What we call rarefaction is matter taking on successively different sizes.

The second question is what it means to call a quality a defining feature of a quantity. Basic defining features have no names of their own, apart from the names of what follows from them. A defining feature cannot have a nature separate from the broader kind it helps define. In truth, these features are particular ways of existing. The broader kind is present in them as a shared meaning. It is not an outside or mental object that exists separately and then attaches to them. Rather, the feature itself is one way the broader kind exists. The mind separates the two through analysis, as it distinguishes a particular existing thing from the general idea of its kind.

Every particular way of existing has its own necessary feature, along with a shared feature that gives it its general meaning. Since particular ways of existing have no names of their own, we refer to them by the features that follow from them. The names we use are for ideas in the mind and general concepts. So straightness, circularity, spherical and cube-like shapes, and other shapes and angles are not, in the deepest sense, the defining features of continuous quantity. In the same way, evenness, oddness, common divisors, and prime numbers are not the defining features of separate number. They follow from those features and serve as signs of them. This does not mean that one thing belongs to two different kinds.

It follows that circles of different sizes differ in kind. As we have seen, a line cannot change from one particular curve to another while remaining the very same line. Each particular curve belongs to a different kind. So when geometers say that a right angle in every circle is one quarter of the full turn, or that half of it is always forty-five degrees, they are not saying that quarter-circle arcs in circles of different sizes have the same length. The arcs are alike because they have the same angle and the same proportion of the full circle. They are similar or proportional, not identical in kind. The same applies to other proportional arcs.

#### IV. Straight and Curved Are Not Opposites

Straightness and curvature are not opposites for two reasons. First, they do not have the same individual subject, and two opposing qualities must belong to one and the same subject. Second, opposites differ as much as possible. A straight line is very different from a curved one, but no particular curve is the one that differs most from a particular straight line.

One thing has one individual opposite. If straightness as a whole were opposed to curvature as a whole, each particular straight line would have one particular curved line as its opposite. But any particular straight line can be the chord of infinitely many arcs, and no two of these arcs are alike. None of them differs from the straight line more than all the others. So none is its unique opposite. If no particular curved line is the greatest possible opposite of a particular straight line, straight and curved are not opposites as kinds either.

#### V. Can a Straight Line and a Curve Be Compared?

Some say that a straight line and a curve cannot be equal, or compared as longer and shorter. Their reason is that a straight line cannot become curved. So the two cannot be laid exactly on top of one another. If they cannot be matched, they cannot be called equal, or one greater or less than the other; nor can one be half, a third, or a multiple of the other.

Others object that we know a chord is shorter than its arc, and that arcs with the same chord can have different lengths. Some answer by refusing to accept that comparison, just as we cannot compare a line with a surface or a body. Others accept that one can be longer or shorter but deny that the two can be equal.
