For example, they say an angle formed by two straight lines is greater than one formed by a curved arc and a straight line tangent to it, but smaller than the angle between a circle’s diameter and circumference. Yet, they say, an angle formed by two straight lines cannot equal either of these other kinds of angle.

Al-Rāzī favors the first answer. He says we should deny that an arc is longer than its chord. To call one thing greater than another, he argues, the greater must contain something equal to the smaller. But a curve cannot contain a straight line of the same kind. The idea that a curve could become straight and contain the same length plus more exists only in the imagination. Since a curve cannot actually become straight, the difference is only imagined, not real.

One could reply that people do say a circle and a straight line can be laid against each other over time. A rolling sphere meets a flat surface at one point at each moment, but over time its curved surface moves along the straight one. If this is possible, then the two may be compared as equal, greater, or less. And the fact that one thing cannot turn into another does not prove that they cannot be laid against one another.

But the objection can be answered this way: to say that one length is greater, the extra part must be close enough in kind to the smaller length to count as a matching piece. A curve has no part that is straight in that sense. Unless we say that they match only at a broader level—as lengths measured in one direction, leaving aside their straightness and curvature. Geometers and our imagination do this when they treat mathematical objects apart from the physical bodies they belong to. The imagination can picture a curve as straight and a straight line as curved, though it cannot picture size with no direction or shape at all, because that idea is too general.

That is why we can speak of a relation between different kinds of lines or surfaces in some ways, such as by their movement over time or by comparing them in the imagination. We cannot lay them together in the physical world at one instant. This is all that can be said here. God grants success.

### Article Two: Shape

#### I. Its Definition

Geometers define a shape as something enclosed by one boundary or several. A shape may be flat or solid. If it is flat, its boundary is a line; if it is solid, its boundary is a surface. A point is not a boundary of a shape: a line marked off by two points is not called a shape, even though the points fully mark its limits. So the phrase “fully enclosed” does not rule out the line for the reason some have suggested. A circle and a sphere have one boundary; a square and a cube have several. The same is true of a semicircle or hemisphere when its boundaries are of different kinds.

Every shape has three parts: the thing that has the shape, its boundary or boundaries, and the form that results. A square, for example, consists of a surface, four boundaries, and a particular form called squareness. Squareness is different from both the surface and its boundaries. None of these can be substituted for another. We cannot say that squareness is what the four boundaries enclose, or that the four boundaries are squareness. Nor can we say that squareness is the surface, or that the surface is squareness. A square is a surface with a certain form. Even when we describe a surface with many conditions, it remains a surface.

So these bounded amounts are not qualities. Nor can something made up of an amount and a particular form be a quality, or an amount by itself, since one thing cannot belong to two entirely different categories. We have also seen that the true features that distinguish kinds of size and number are not these qualities. They are unknown in themselves and are known through the features that follow from them. One category can follow from another in the way things exist. So what can be counted as a quality here is the form that belongs to the amount.

It seems, then, that the definitions of shapes describe what geometers use, rather than the quality itself. The same is true of their comparisons. When they say that one shape is equal to, greater than, smaller than, half of, or a third of another, they mean the amount that has the shape, not the shape itself. For example, a hexagon drawn around a circle is larger than the circle, while one drawn inside it is smaller. And in an isosceles triangle, the right angle is twice either of the other angles, and each of those is half the right angle. These comparisons concern the amount, not its shape. What the boundaries enclose is a measurable size. Size is a quantity; its shape is a quality. A square is not the same as squareness, just as a round surface is not the same as roundness.

I should add one more point. We can mean unity itself, or a thing that is one. Likewise, we can mean relation itself, or something else—a substance or an amount, for example—that stands in a relation, as a father does. The same distinction applies here. By “triangle,” we may mean the triangle itself, rather than something else that has triangularity, just as “white” can mean the quality of whiteness. If we mean the triangle itself, there is no difference between shape and the thing shaped. Also, if we imagine a triangle’s amount apart from matter, it does not need an added quality to make it a triangle. It is a triangle by its own nature.

If, instead, we use “shape” to mean an abstract idea taken from these forms, then it is only an idea the mind makes, like “thinghood” or “being a substance.” Geometers use “shape” for the bounded amount itself, which is both a shape and something shaped. Others use it for the quality that appears in a natural body because of its amount. This completes what I can establish about the second question. Shape can also be confused with position, the third question, so I will turn to that now.

#### II. Is Shape a Quality or a Position?

The reason shape may be confused with position is that shape is a form an amount has because its boundary or boundaries enclose it.[^V04-50] One meaning of position is the form something has because of the way its parts are arranged in relation to one another. A square, for example, seems to get its form from the way its sides and boundaries relate to one another. This makes it seem like a position.

We can clear this up by noticing that the word “position” has several meanings. First, it can mean simply that something is somewhere. Second, it can mean that one thing is beside another on a certain side, as when one line is to the right of another. That is a relation: here, it means simply that the two are next to each other.[^V04-51] Third, it can mean a body's posture, which comes from the way its parts are arranged in relation to one another.

This third meaning is the category of position. The particular ways in which the parts lie next to one another are relations belonging to the parts; position belongs to the body as a whole. Sitting, for example, is a position of the whole body. The fact that each limb is next to another is a relation between the parts. But the body's position must also relate to something outside it. If the parts kept their arrangement while the body's relation to everything outside it disappeared, the person would no longer be sitting.

So anyone who says that shape is position is mistaken for three reasons. First, they confuse boundaries, which matter to shape, with parts, which matter to position. Second, they treat position as a separate category, when this meaning of it is a relation. Position in the strict sense is the arrangement of a thing's parts in relation to something outside it, not the way its parts are arranged in relation to one another. Third, they assume that if something depends on a category, it belongs to that category. But a square depends on there being four lines; that does not make it a number. It is an amount. Squareness may arise from the arrangement of its boundaries, but that does not make it a position.

Someone might object: “Isn't a quality defined as something we can understand without understanding anything else? But to understand squareness, we must understand how the sides of a square relate. We cannot understand those relations without first understanding the surface and its boundaries.”

We answer that shape is a stable form a thing has because of the relations among its boundaries. It is not those relations themselves. The relations—right, left, above, below, and so on—are features of how the boundaries are arranged. Shape is the form that arises from them. So shape is linked to position, in the sense of relation, by way of cause; it does not belong to the category of position.

A circle does not depend on position either. What makes it a circle is the special curve of its boundary. The circumference of a circle, the surface of a sphere, and similar forms are simple forms of this kind. They are not usually called shapes because they do not fit the usual definition, unless that definition is made broader. Some therefore define shape as the form an amount has because it is enclosed by a boundary, or because it forms the boundary of something else.

#### III. What Kind of Thing Is an Angle?

An angle differs from a shape because it is formed where two or more boundaries meet. On a flat surface, two lines meet at a point; around a solid angle, two or more surfaces meet at a point or along a line. A plane angle, for example, is a surface with two boundaries that meet at a point. The surface may also be enclosed by a third or fourth boundary, or it may not. If no third boundary encloses it, the two boundaries may meet again at another point, or they may not meet again even if extended without end. Ellipses, crescent shapes, and horseshoe shapes are examples.

If a surface is considered as lying between two boundaries that meet at a point, it is an angle. If it is considered as enclosed on all sides—by a circle, for example, or by two lines as in a semicircle—it is a shape. The same surface can be viewed in either way: as an angle because two of its boundaries meet, or as a shape because it is enclosed.

The boundary, amount, and form have their counterparts in both cases. When geometers say “shape,” they mean the amount that has the shape; when they say “angle,” they mean the surface that has the angle. The same applies when they call an angle bisected, doubled, large, or small. This is also true of solid angles.

Someone might think that the dispute over whether an angle is a quantity or a quality is just a difference in how geometers and other philosophers use words. But there is a difficulty: a plane angle can be divided only in one direction, the direction between its two sides, not in the other. A solid angle can be divided in only two directions, not the third, the direction between the cone's tip and its base. This raises a question: when geometers speak of an angle, do they mean the surface or body itself, or something else? And is the form that belongs to it a form of the surface or body, or a different thing?

Because of this uncertainty, some have tried to make an angle a new kind of quantity between a line and a surface, or—in a solid angle—between a surface and a body. They imagine that a point moving forms a line, and a line moving sideways as a whole, at right angles to itself, forms a surface. If one end stays fixed while the other moves, perhaps it creates something between a line and a surface, and likewise between a surface and a body. This is mistaken. The amount between two lines is necessarily a surface, since a surface can be divided in two directions. The amount formed where surfaces meet at a point is necessarily a body, since a body can be divided in three directions. The fact that an angled surface can be divided only in one direction, or an angled body in two, concerns it as an angle; it does not change what kind of amount it is.

The truth is that for a geometer a plane angle is an actual surface, considered as bounded in some directions but not others because of its particular form. For those who study nature, it is the form itself that belongs to the surface in this way. The same is true of solid angles. There is another question: should we call the form of a solid amount an angle when it lies between two surfaces that meet along a line, whether one surface stands straight against the other or slopes toward or away from it?

Avicenna says in his discussion of the Categories that there may be a common meaning of “angle” that covers an angle formed by two lines, several surfaces meeting at a point, and two surfaces meeting along a line. That common meaning would be an amount with several boundaries that end at one shared boundary. If we use “angle” in this broad sense, that is reasonable. A solid angle does not end at a point because it is an angle; rather, its boundaries meet at one shared boundary, which happens to be a point when the boundaries are lines. If someone insists on using “angle” only for an amount whose boundaries end at a point, I will not argue. The word then has a narrower meaning, and there are amounts with boundaries that do not fall under either “angle” or “shape.”

#### IV. Is an Angle a Quantity or a Quality?

Those who say an angle is a quantity point out that it can be divided and can be equal or unequal to another angle. But qualities that belong to quantities can also be divided or compared in this way. The difference is that one has these features in itself, while the other has them through the amount it belongs to. So the mere fact that an angle can be divided or compared does not settle the question.

Ibn al-Haytham argues that an angle cannot be a quantity because doubling it once or several times destroys what it is, while doubling an amount never destroys what it is. A right angle, when doubled once, is no longer a right angle. An acute angle, when doubled several times, also stops being acute. So, he concludes, an angle is not an amount.

I answer that an angle may be a kind of amount. Doubling it can destroy its identity as a particular kind of angle without destroying it as an amount. For example, three is a kind of number. Double it, and the result is six, not three. It has stopped being three, but it is still a number. Likewise, everyone agrees that a semicircle's arc is a line. When doubled, it is no longer a semicircle, but it is still a curved line.

Those who say that an angle is a quality argue that it can be similar or dissimilar to another angle, and that this does not come from the amount it belongs to. So, they say, it has similarity or difference in itself, which makes it a quality. They say that equality and inequality come from its amount, just as shapes are compared because of their amounts.

But if a thing has a feature through something else, that feature may come either through the thing it belongs to or through some other feature. Unless they show that an angle's similarity does not come through some other feature, it is not enough to show only that it does not come through its amount. They have not shown this, so they have not proved that similarity belongs to an angle in itself.

The truth is that an angle is like a shape in this respect. There is an amount considered simply as an amount; there is that amount considered as having a particular form because two lines meet at a point, as in a plane angle; and there is the form itself. The first—amount as amount, or surface as surface, apart from any added form—is not an angle. The second meaning is what geometers call an angle. The third is what others call an angle. These meanings are clear enough. The remaining problem is this: why does the surface remain a surface when it has a particular angle, yet fail to divide in both directions as other surfaces do when they have qualities such as color?

The answer is in the difference between these two kinds of quality. Other qualities are present in the amount itself, or in the thing with amount, which can be divided. So they divide wherever their subject is divided. Qualities that belong to quantity, by contrast, usually arise from a particular ending or limit. A limit is not an amount; it is the absence of more amount, together with a relation to what it limits. An amount with a particular limit or unity does not have to divide into parts that are each like the whole in that respect. A circle, for example, is a surface with a special unity because of its boundary. It cannot be divided into parts that are all circles.

If we say “half a circle” or “a third of a circle,” we mean half or a third of its surface as an amount, not a part of its circular form. It is like speaking of half a sphere or half an animal: we mean half the material, not half the kind or its complete form.

An angle is similar. It can be divided into parts that each keep the angle's defining condition, except that each part is smaller than the whole. That condition is that the surface lies between two lines that meet. So we can divide the angle with one line or many lines drawn between those two sides, from the chord toward the meeting point. We can do this without end while keeping the surface's angled form. But it cannot be divided in another direction while remaining an angle, because its defining condition would no longer hold. It can be divided in that other direction only as a surface, not as an angle. A cylinder shows the same point. It is clearly an amount and a solid body. But as a cylinder, it can be divided along the direction between its two bases, not in the other directions, because only that division preserves its cylindrical form. God is the giver of guidance.

#### V. Why Shapes Have No Degrees or Opposites

This kind of quality cannot change into another, so its members are not opposites. No square is more square than another, and no number is more even than another.[^V04-52] Whenever a quality can be stronger or weaker, the same subject must be able to take on both degrees in turn. Black and white, hot and cold, health and illness, knowledge and ignorance, and generosity and stinginess work this way. The subject leaves one state and takes on another. This is how opposites with intermediate states behave: the subject may leave one extreme, pass through a middle state, and reach the other. Justice lies between two extremes, as does temperance. But when we look at individual people, we may not judge these middle states by their exact definition. We might say that one person's justice is greater than another's, even though justice, as defined, has no greater or lesser degree.

The fourth kind of quality that belongs to quantity has no stronger or weaker degree, and no opposites. A square or pentagon cannot be more or less square or pentagonal. If we say a square is larger, we mean that it has more material, not that its squareness has increased. When the amount changes, the exact form of squareness does not remain the same; our senses may simply fail to notice a difference. One square may look more exact than another, but this is only how it appears to us, not a difference in true squareness. It is like justice as people practice it today: one person may seem more just than another, though the comparison holds only as people see it, not by the exact meaning of justice.

Heat can truly be stronger than other heat, and one black color can truly be darker than another. This is not just how they seem to us. The difference between two degrees of heat is real; the difference between two squares that seem more or less exact is only in how they appear. A square that is more exact to the eye is like someone who seems more just: it is more so in appearance, not in reality.

We have already seen that straightness and circularity are not opposites. Nor are a bulging surface and a hollow one opposites, because their subjects are different surfaces. The same surface cannot become flat or hollow and still remain that very surface. So there is no opposition between the bulging outer surface of the heavens and the hollow inner surface. They are different surfaces, and one cannot take on the other's form.

Even and odd may seem to be opposites, but they are not. They do not take turns in the same number. Nor is it enough for two meanings to have separate names and fail to occur together in one subject. “Even” has a positive meaning: a number that divides into two equal parts. “Odd” means only that a number does not divide in that way. Its meaning is a negation attached to number in general, not a distinct kind of number. That does not make it the opposite of even. If oddness were given a positive meaning, it would at most name a different kind of meaning that does not share a subject with evenness. This is the gist of Avicenna's point here.[^V04-53]

### Article Three: Overall Appearance and Number Qualities

#### I. Overall Appearance (Question Five)

As we have said, the kind of sameness we count depends on what we are dividing. When we divide something into specific kinds, we count sameness of kind. When we divide it into individual things, we count each thing as its own individual. Otherwise, no division would have a finite list of parts. If we divided words into kinds, for example, we could not stop at nouns, verbs, and particles. Words can be joined in endlessly many ways, creating endlessly many further kinds.

Someone could object that overall appearance is made up of color and shape, each of which belongs to a different kind. If every pair of things that comes together counted as a kind of its own, the kinds would be endless—not just once, but again and again without end.

We can answer in either of two ways. First, as I said earlier, we do not have to say that overall appearance is one quality or one single reality just because it has one name. A name may be one word even when what it names has several parts or is not a single unified thing.

Or we can say that when shape and color come together, they produce another quality: the look by which we call someone good-looking or ugly. Shape and color cause this overall appearance; they are not parts that make up its nature. The beauty or ugliness of a shape alone, or of a color alone, differs from the beauty or ugliness of someone's overall appearance. This special quality belongs neither to shape nor color alone, nor to the two considered separately. It arises when they come together, as a distinct form called overall appearance. Its beauty or ugliness means something different from the beauty or ugliness of its color or shape on its own.

This second answer works only if the beauty of an overall appearance is not simply a combination of beautiful color and beautiful shape, and the same for ugliness. That is open to question. Since this is not an important issue, we can leave it aside.

#### II. Can Attributes Have General and Specific Parts?

Most philosophers do not think kinds of attributes can be built from a general kind and a feature that distinguishes it, as substances can. That is why they find it difficult to explain how overall appearance could be a single kind of quality.[^V04-54] Avicenna replies that we need not deny that one attribute can consist of other attributes. Ten is an attribute insofar as it is a number; as a quantity, it is made up of five and five. A square is also an attribute, made up of a surface bounded by four lines. But Avicenna means that substances can have parts that play the roles of a general kind and a defining feature, even if those parts are not literally a broader kind and a defining feature. Attributes do not have parts of this kind, even when they can be described as having parts.

I have two objections to this. First, Avicenna says in his book on metaphysics that ten is not made from five and five, or from four and six, or from any other numbers. It is made from individual units. The nature of number is therefore simple: its parts do not differ in kind. Its unity is weak because it is also a multitude. Different kinds of number differ only in the greater or smaller number of units they contain.

Second, a square is simple in reality, whether we mean the form that belongs to a surface or the figure that geometers study. The mind can analyze it into a general kind and a feature that distinguishes it, but its actual nature has no such parts. The surface, four lines, and points are not parts of the square's nature. If they were, one thing would be made up of parts from different kinds, which is impossible. Avicenna and other careful philosophers have already explained this. Those things are conditions that surround the square, not parts that make it what it is.

The sixth question is how to classify something whose existence is made up of the effects of two different principles. We discussed a similar issue when we considered the forms that make things the kinds of things they are, and set out a rule for deciding whether a doubtful case is a substance or a quality.

Avicenna's rule is this: either one of the two is the main subject and the other is a quality that belongs to it, or neither is prior to the other. In a square, for example, the surface is the subject and the form belongs to it. The whole therefore belongs to the same kind as its subject. A surface with a form is still a surface, not a quality.

But if neither part is the subject and the other an added quality, their combination is only accidental. It does not form one thing with a nature of its own. Writing and length, for example, can occur together, but their combination does not make one new thing. So the combination does not belong to one kind. If we follow this rule, these things would be composites made up of different kinds.

I say that anything belonging to a category or general kind must be one thing in reality. Its combination with other features comes from what follows from it. A square's nature is simply an amount with a particular defining feature. That feature is its defining feature, joined to its general kind in both existence and creation. Its lines, angles, and points follow from that feature; they are not parts of it. The same is true of flat and solid shapes, whether they have many sides or few. A solid with twelve pentagonal faces, for instance, is one amount given its specific form by one defining feature. Its parts are only what follows from that form.

If I may say so, the same is true of what are called composite substances. But the features that define something can be understood in two ways. We may take them from the thing itself: one meaning applies to it and other things, and another is specific to it. Both are present in the thing itself. Or we may take them from what surrounds it: its general kind from its matter, and what distinguishes it from its form. The form can contain all the meanings found in the matter that it shapes. I will examine this more fully later, if God wills.

#### III. Features and Qualities of Numbers

These questions are better handled in particular fields such as arithmetic and measurement. Here, I will mention only three points.

First, number features are not opposites. This includes being even or odd, being a divisor or being divisible, being commensurable or incommensurable, and division and multiplication. Some of these pairs lack one condition for being opposites; others lack most of them. They all share this: two unlike features do not belong to the same immediate subject. In some pairs, one side is a lack or absence. And even if we treat few and many as positive features, neither is the farthest possible opposite of the other. So number features are not opposites, just as numbers themselves are not opposites.

Second, these features cannot become stronger or weaker, or increase or decrease, in the same subject. As we have seen, a subject cannot change from one individual thing into another and remain that very same thing. One arc cannot become more curved while staying the same arc; at that point it becomes a different arc. Likewise, a number cannot become more even, or more of a square, cube, or root, while remaining the same number. Any such change would replace it with another number.

Third, being even or odd is not part of what a number is. These features are said of numbers of different kinds with the same clear meaning. If one of them were part of the nature of some numbers, it would be part of the nature of all numbers, since no number has a claim to it over another. Then the meaning of evenness would belong to every even number, and the meaning of oddness to every odd number. If that were so, we would know at once whether any number was even or odd. But we often have to work it out, especially with large numbers. So evenness and oddness are not essential features of numbers.[^V04-55] This ends what I intended to say about the category of quality. May praise of the One who gives understanding never end, and may blessings on His Messenger and family be beyond counting.

### Part Three: The Remaining Kinds of Properties

### Article One: Relation

#### I. Starting the Discussion of Relations

We need to understand “relation” in the same way we understand “existence” and “one”: each can mean three different things. “Existence,” for example, can mean existence itself; it can mean something described as existing; or it can mean an existing thing, such as a human being together with its existence. The first is like the idea of a “kind” considered in logic; the second is like human nature considered in common; the third brings the two together, as when we think of “human being” and also think of it as a kind. “White” can also mean the quality of whiteness, the thing that is white, or the thing together with that quality.

The three cases differ in where the quality belongs. Whiteness belongs to a thing in the outside world, and the subject is considered as something outside the mind. So the statement “this thing is white” says that a real thing in the world has the quality of whiteness. It depends on both the thing and the quality existing outside the mind. By contrast, when we call something universal, particular, a kind, a broader kind, essential, or accidental—or call it a proposition or an argument—the quality and its subject exist in the mind. The statement is true only as a judgment about what is in the mind.

With “existence” and “one,” the quality belongs through analysis. The subject is considered as it is in itself, apart from whether it exists outside the mind or only in the mind. A statement such as “a human being exists,” “is one,” “is a cause,” “is an effect,” “is necessary,” or “is possible” is not purely about the outside world, even when the quality exists outside, as in “a human being exists.” Nor is it purely about the mind, even when the quality is a thought, as in “a human being is understood.” It expresses a real unity between the meaning of the subject and the meaning of the quality. To make this judgment, the mind first considers human nature in itself, before adding anything else to it, whether that addition belongs to its existence or to its nature, such as existence, possibility, or being made.

The same three meanings apply to relation. We may mean the relation itself, the thing that has the relation, or the whole made up of both. The second meaning is not our subject here. The first is the category of relation; the third is the combined thing. Since a general outline is easier to understand than a detailed one, logicians define a relative as “something whose nature is understood in relation to something else.” This includes both relations and things that have relations.

But for something to be understood in relation to another, it is not enough that thinking of one happens to bring the other to mind. Otherwise, every thing would be relative to its cause or effect. Many things have their own nature and can be understood on their own, even if they are connected to something else. Rather, a relative is something whose nature cannot be established in the mind or in reality unless the other side is established too. Parenthood, for example, cannot exist without the relation of being a child.

#### II. What a True Relation Is

You may object that this definition fails. To say that something is understood “in relation to something else” brings us back to relation itself, or to a kind of relation. So the definition explains something by itself, or by something that depends on it. If “understood in relation to something else” means that understanding one thing requires understanding another, then the definition goes in a circle for the two sides of a relation: each depends on the other. If it means that two things are simply understood together, then many things that are not relatives also fit. We understand a roof together with its wall, but the nature of a roof and a wall is not a relation between the two. Someone might reply that a relative must be understood together with the thing that corresponds to it. But when we look closely at what “corresponds” means here, we find that it comes back to relation.

A similar problem arises with the definition that a relative is something whose existence is its being related. Some have defended this by saying that the word “relative” in the definition means a compound relative, which is more familiar than a simple relation.[^V04-56] Some books say that a general kind, or something like a general kind, may be more familiar than the specific kind. Experts then use the general name for the specific kind, as with general and specific possibility. The word “relative” can mean either a simple relation, such as parenthood, or a thing that has a relation, such as a father. The term is then restricted to the simple relation. But this is a mistake. There is no general and specific kind here, and no single meaning that includes both. The compound relative contains the simple relation as a whole contains a part. This is not like a general kind containing a specific kind. Nor can the general meaning include something the specific meaning lacks.

I say the first definition can be defended in a better way. The word “relative” in it means something that can be called relative, such as “measured” or “understood.” It does not mean the simple relation itself, or the combination of that relation with the thing that has it. There is no problem if a definition of a concept includes one example of the concept, because the definition explains the general idea, not the particular example.[^V04-57] So it does not make the idea depend on itself.

The second definition—“a relative is something whose existence is its being related”—is a reminder, not a strict definition. The nature of relation, whether simple or combined with its subject, is easy to understand. The reminder helps us distinguish the simple relation from the combined thing. The combined thing includes something from another category. A father is a substance with fatherhood; equality is an amount with agreement; similarity is a quality that agrees with something like itself. An amount that is equal, or a quality that is similar, is not a simple relation. It is a combination of the relation with something from another category.

As for the difference between a relation and a reciprocal relation, not every relation is reciprocal. A relation becomes reciprocal when we consider it from both sides: not only one thing's relation to another, but also the relation that follows on the other side. A roof, for example, rests on a wall, and the wall supports the roof. That is why they say a relation can be considered from one side, while a reciprocal relation involves both sides.

#### III. Properties of Both Sides of a Relation

One property of the two sides of a relation is that they depend on each other. If one exists, the other must exist too, whether in reality or in thought, and whether the relation is actual or only possible. The same is true of their absence. But this seems difficult to apply to earlier and later times. They are related, yet they do not seem to exist together. We also know that something of value will exist in the future, and knowledge and what is known are related, yet the value does not exist now while our knowledge of it does.

Avicenna answers the first problem by saying that we consider earlier and later in two ways. First, we consider them in thought: the mind brings two times to mind and sees that one comes before the other. Both are therefore present in thought. Second, we consider them as things that exist in a way the mind can recognize. When the earlier time exists, the later time is not yet there, but it can come to exist. This is what it means for it to be later. The mind understands this about the later time while the earlier one exists.[^V04-58] When the later time arrives, the mind understands that the earlier time is no longer there, as something that once existed and has passed away. This, too, is present in thought along with the later time. Any other way of understanding the later time exists only in thought, not among things outside the mind.

I do not think this fully solves the problem. The relations between parts of time may not exist in the mind until the mind considers them. But when the mind divides time into days, months, and years, it finds relations among them and judges that some come before others. These relations are not only in thought. The relation of earlier and later among parts of time is like the relation of above and below among parts of the heavens. The parts and their relations may be only possible, but if we suppose them to be actual, their relation would hold in reality, as other relations do. A real relation requires the two sides to exist together in the way that applies to them, not only in thought.

There is another problem with Avicenna's answer. He says that when the earlier time exists, the later time has a kind of nonexistence. This sounds as if nonexistence were itself something that exists, which is impossible: if nonexistence existed, a thing would be both itself and its opposite. When one part of time exists and another does not, the absent part's nonexistence is not a real thing that can have a real relation to the present part. We might mean that the mind understands the later time's nonexistence as “not existing in the earlier time,” since the existence of each part of time involves the nonexistence of the other. But then the thing described as nonexistent in this way would have to exist. And the present time is not earlier than the nonexistence of the future time; it is earlier than the future time itself. The future time is not present, so it cannot be one side of a present relation. The present time is here, but the relation is not. This leaves the problem unresolved.

As for the second problem, Avicenna says that what we truly know is the image present in the mind. The real relation is between our knowledge and the nature of what is known. When we know that something of value will exist, we know that it will be; we do not know the value as something that already exists.

I see two problems with this answer. First, knowledge is something that exists outside the mind, since it is counted among the soul's qualities. You have also said that if one side of a relation exists outside the mind, the other side must exist there too. But the nature of the thing known, considered simply as what is known, does not exist outside the mind. Second, knowledge is really the existence of the nature of the thing known in the knower. Existence and that nature are not two separate things that can have a relation between them. Avicenna answers the first problem by saying that knowledge, as a quality present in the mind, exists outside the mind; but as an image of the nature of something that exists outside, it exists in the mind. Knowledge and the nature of what is known are therefore both in the mind.

He answers the second problem by saying that the relation between knowledge and what it directly knows is like the relation between existence and the nature that exists through it: the mind distinguishes them, though they are one in reality. A thing that exists outside the mind is one thing; when the mind analyzes it into its nature and its existence, it sees a relation between them. In the same way, a thing present in the mind is one image. When the mind analyzes it into its nature and its mental existence, it sees a relation between knowledge and what is known. Knowledge is the thing's existence in the mind, apart from matter and its other attachments. The same applies to sensation and what is sensed: the image present in the sense is a mental quality that represents the qualities we sense.

Another property attributed to relations is that the two sides do not always have to exist together in every respect. Each may exist without the other. This is said to be true of knowledge and what is known, and of sensation and what is sensed—but here we mean acts of knowing and sensing, not the powers of knowledge and sensation. An act of knowledge or sensation cannot exist without the thing known or sensed. But the thing known or sensed can exist without anyone knowing or sensing it. The same is said of owner and owned: an owner can exist without what is owned, and what is owned can exist without an owner. In other cases, neither side can exist without the other, as with a particular hand's motion and the motion of the key it turns. This is Avicenna's account, and the account given by others.

I think this needs closer examination. The things they call the subjects of these relations are often not their true subjects. Avicenna uses knowledge and what is known as an example. The being of knowledge, he says, always requires that it be related to what is known. But the being of what is known does not require knowledge; it may exist without being known. He says the same of sensation and what is sensed: sensation always requires something sensed, while the sensed thing can exist without sensation. For example, the elements we can sense may exist even when no animal that can sense them exists.

But we can reply that the thing knowledge truly relates to is always known. What knowledge does not reach is not known by that knowledge, at that time or at any other time. The thing known in the mind is always known; the material thing hidden from the mind by its coverings is never known as it is in itself.

The same applies to sensation and what is sensed. We mean the image in the act of sensing, not the power to sense; and we mean the image that is sensed, not the material thing. These two images cannot be separated, just as two simple qualities that depend on each other cannot be separated. The ideas “sensing” and “sensed” belong together in thought, and so do the two things that bear these relations in the act of sensing.

This also applies to anything that is the direct subject of a relation. A thing that is only indirectly called sensed or known is also found alongside the act of sensation or knowledge. For example, a sound in moving air is heard indirectly when it strikes the ear. It exists at the same time as the sound-image that is heard directly. The outside sound is not known directly or indirectly at any time apart from that image, so no relation to it exists. The same is true of heat from a fire near the organ of touch. It can be called indirectly sensed, because what is directly sensed is a mental quality that represents the heat outside, as we have said. The heat outside our thought and outside the place where it touches the organ has no relation to us, either directly or indirectly. So the two simple sides of the relation must exist and cease together. This is also true of the two things that bear those relations, considered apart from their other features.

Someone might object that, for those who reject the idea that an image enters the eye and also reject the idea that a ray leaves it, vision is simply a relation of perception to the very thing outside the eye. I answer, first, that we were discussing sense as the image of perception. Even for those thinkers, this image is the same as the outside image, so they cannot be separated. As for the position-based relation between the sense organ and the outside thing, its two sides exist together, as with other relations of position, when we examine them closely. Second, what is directly seen is an image of size and shape present to the soul, freed from matter. This image is both seeing and what is seen; it is one thing considered in two ways.

A further property attributed to the two sides of a relation is that each can be turned around and stated of the other. Just as we say, “A father is a father of a son,” we can say, “A son is a son of a father.” But this reversal is required only when each is related to the other precisely as a related thing. If we say “a father of a child,” “of a human being,” or “of a black person,” we do not have to reverse the statement and say “a child of a father,” “a human being of a father,” or “a black person of a father.”

It can be hard to identify the true pair in a relation. The way to do so is to gather all the features of one thing and test them. If we remove some features but keep the relation, those features are not essential to it. If we remove one feature and the relation disappears, that is the feature that points to the other side. For example, if we remove from a son the facts that he is an animal, a human being, rational, a writer, and so on, but keep the fact that he is a son, the relation of fatherhood remains. If we remove his being a son but keep all the other features, that relation disappears.

We can now see that the true balance in a relation is between father and son: each can be turned around and stated of the other. When this balance is missing, reversal is not required. We can say “a rudder belongs to a ship” or “a head belongs to an animal,” but we cannot reverse these and say “a ship belongs to a rudder” or “an animal belongs to a head.” Reversal works only when we say “the head of something that has a head” and “the rudder of something that has a rudder.”

Some related terms need no linking word, when the relative itself has a name, such as “greater” and “smaller.” Others need a linking word. Sometimes it is the same on both sides: “A slave belongs to a master,” and “a master belongs to a slave.” Sometimes it differs: “A knower knows through knowledge,” while “knowledge belongs to a knower.”

#### IV. What the Category of Relation Itself Means

The definition given earlier—“something whose nature is understood in relation to something else”—can mean the concept itself: the very meaning of being understood in relation to another. It can also mean another nature to which this description applies. A father, for example, is understood in relation to a son, but he also has a nature of his own, such as being a human being or a horse. Fatherhood, by contrast, has no nature beyond being understood in relation to a son. So there are three things. First is what the definition describes directly: the category of relation, one of the ten highest categories.

Second are the kinds or specific forms that fall under the category, whether broad or narrow. Third is a different nature, belonging to substance, quantity, quality, or another category, to which the relation applies as an added feature. The first is the category of relation itself; the second is one of its kinds; the third is a thing that has a relation as one of its features. Many books explain only the difference between a relation and the thing that has it, without distinguishing the category from its kinds.

Avicenna says that the category of relation is also something related, since its nature is understood in relation to something else. If so, it seems to share the same meaning as a relative thing that does not belong to the category itself.

I have already pointed out the difference. The first is the very meaning expressed by “whose nature is understood in relation to something else.” In the same way, the category of substance means “a nature that, when it exists, does not exist in a subject.” The category of relation is the meaning of “relative” itself. This meaning cannot be separated from being a thing, but its thinghood is nothing beyond being a relation. This is unlike “white” when it means something other than whiteness that has the quality of being white. If we treated every such derived term as a separate category, the categories would be endless. So the general term “relative,” which includes both simple relations and things that have them, is not itself one category. The category includes only what has no nature beyond being related.

Someone might object that relation itself is also a thing whose nature is understood in relation to another, so it should not be a category. My answer is that relation itself is a thing only in the sense that it is a relation. But a father, a house, or anything else that has a relation is already a particular kind of thing—human being, house, or something else—before the relation belongs to it. A father considered as a person has fatherhood; fatherhood itself is a relation. A feature in this sense is the very quality it names, just as “rational,” taken as a defining feature, can mean the substantial form of a reasoning being itself.

#### V. Does Relation Exist Outside the Mind?

Many things exist without having an existence of their own. These include the meaning of an attribute and the meanings of general and specific features in a simple thing.[^V04-59] Relations and other connections are like this. A relation does not exist apart from the existence of substance, quantity, quality, or another thing. Rather, it exists when one of these things has a nature that the mind understands in relation to something else. A roof, for example, has a position, and the mind understands its relation to the wall in that position.

The sky, too, has one position-based existence. The mind understands its nature as a substance, and also understands a further meaning that is not part of its nature: being above. Something exists outside the mind when what we say of it truly applies to a thing that exists outside our thoughts.[^V04-60] For example, a person really can write or be white. Relations also exist in this sense. We can truly say that the sky is above the earth, or that Zayd is a father. This differs from things that exist only in thought, such as “animal is a broader kind” and “human being is a species.” Being a broader kind or a species is not a feature found in things outside the mind. This shows why it is wrong to say, as some have, that relation exists only in thought like the ideas of general and particular.

They offer several arguments for denying that relation exists outside the mind. The first is that this would lead to an endless chain. If a relation exists outside the mind, it must exist in a subject. But what the relation is in itself is one thing, and its being in that subject is another. Fatherhood, for example, is not the same as its being in a father. So there would have to be another relation between fatherhood and the father, and the same problem would arise again without end. Avicenna answers that we must use the general definition of a relative to solve this problem.

We say that a relative is something whose nature is understood in relation to something else. Anything outside the mind that has this nature is a relative. There are many such things, so relation exists outside the mind. If a relative has another nature as well, we should set that other nature aside and focus on the meaning understood in relation to something else. That meaning is the true relation. It is not understood in relation to something else because of a second relation; it is related by its own nature. The further fact that it exists in a subject—for example, that fatherhood exists in a father—is another way it exists. That, too, is relation by its own nature. There is no need to add another relation to explain either one.

This is Avicenna's account in his *Healing*. It clearly answers the objection in two ways. First, he shows that relations exist outside the mind. Then he stops the endless chain by showing that a relation is related by its own nature, not through another relation added to it. Nor does each different relation need another distinct relation to attach it to its subject. Fatherhood exists in a father, but this does not require a second relation, different in meaning from fatherhood, and then a third, and so on. The mind can form many similar or different ideas in this way, but the chain stops when the mind stops adding them.

The second argument is one we have already seen: if earlier and later relations exist, then past and future times must exist together. The answer is also the one given earlier. Parts of time do not exist together at one instant, as things that exist at an instant do. They exist together as parts of one continuous, changing existence. In that continuous existence, their being together is their being earlier and later, just as the unity of a number is also its many parts.

#### VI. How Relation Exists in the Outside World

There are several puzzles about how relation exists outside the mind. First, if relation exists, it must share in existence with other things and also differ from them in some special way. Unless existence is given that special feature, the relation will not exist outside the mind. So the feature must come before the relation. But being given that feature is itself the relation. If relation can exist only because another relation already exists, the same question arises about that earlier relation. The existence of one relation would then depend on endlessly many similar relations.

Second, is existence, simply as existence, a relation or not? If it is a relation, then every existing thing would be related, which is not so. If it is not a relation, then relation, if it exists, is not related simply because it exists. So existence by itself is not relation, and relation by itself is not existence. That is the point.

Third, if relation were a real thing that exists in its own right, God would be subject to change. He would have a relation of being present with every new thing. That relation did not exist before the thing appeared, and would disappear when it vanished. God would then be a subject in which new things arise, which would make Him bodily or dependent on a body. Exalted is He far above that.[^V04-61]

Here is how relation exists, in a way that answers these and similar puzzles. The existence of a relation is not separate from the existence of other things. Instead, a thing—whether a substance or an attribute—has a way of existing such that when we understand it, we also understand another thing. That is how relation exists. A cause, such as an intellect, has an existence that belongs to it as a substance. The fact that this existence affects something else is the existence of that cause as a cause, which is a kind of relation. The same existence is a substance in itself and, in relation to another, a cause. It is a substance in its own right and a relation only in a dependent sense.

I call it dependent because the idea of being a cause is not part of this existence when we consider it on its own, apart from other things. The existence of a thing as a relation is different in meaning from its existence as a substance, but the two are different ways of considering one existence. The substance that causes something and its being a cause differ in what they mean, not in having two separate existences. Taken in itself, this existence is a substance; considered in relation to another, it is a relation. When we understand it as it exists outside the mind, understanding it also leads us to understand something else. That is the existence of relation.

In short, a simple relation does not have a separate, independent existence outside the mind. It exists as a feature of things whose nature lets us compare them with something else. The sky exists in itself as a substance. When we compare it with the earth, we understand its being above as a relation.

Quantity works in the same way. In itself, a quantity has a nature that makes it able to be equal to something, greater than it, or smaller than it. When it is actually equal to something, that equality is the existence of the relation “equal.” The existence of the equal thing is not the same as the existence of quantity: if we compare the quantity with something greater or smaller, it is no longer equal. But the kind of quantity remains what it is. One existence can therefore support many relations. The number one, for example, exists as one and can also be called half of two, a third of three, a quarter of four, and so on without end. It does not change or become a collection of things by taking on these relations.

There are two ways to make a relation more specific. First, we can consider it together with the thing it belongs to, as in “a body that is white.” This is not one of the categories on its own, like an amount that is equal or a quality that is similar.

Second, we can understand the relation as a specific kind that takes its defining feature from the thing it relates to. Both the relation and its specific feature are understood together as one added feature. This is how relations are divided into kinds. Being equal is agreement in quantity, while being similar is agreement in quality. Agreement in quantity is not the same thing as a quantity that agrees with another; nor is agreement in quality the same thing as a quality that agrees with another.

The category of relation, the general kind of simple relations, and the feature that makes each kind specific are not three separate things. Their general and specific meanings form one nature. Someone might object that equality and similarity both mean agreement, but differ because one is in quantity and the other in quality. If quantity and quality themselves are the features that distinguish them, a simple relation would be made from parts belonging to different categories. One kind would then belong to two categories, or one category would be formed from another, which is impossible. Or perhaps the feature that distinguishes the relation of agreement is a second relation to quantity or quality. But then one relation would be made specific by another relation, and we would have to ask again what distinguishes the second relation from the first. The same problems would return.

I answer that agreement is a general meaning. It becomes specific through a meaning that narrows it, just as a vague meaning becomes clear—not through a different kind of meaning. When agreement becomes agreement in quantity, and so equality, it is not made specific by quantity itself; otherwise one category would be formed from another. Nor is it made specific by adding another relation to quantity; otherwise one relation would be made specific by a second. Rather, it becomes specific as agreement *in* quantity, not *by* quantity and not by another relation to quantity.

This is like the way other general kinds become specific. The feature that distinguishes an animal is its capacity to reason. This does not mean that the capacity to reason is added to perception to form a bundle of “perceiving” and “reasoning.” It means that the perceiver is one who can reason. The same applies as we move from perceiving to growing, from growing to body, and from body to substance. Confusion here can come from mistaking a general kind for the matter that makes it up.

Now that these points are clear, let us return to the difficulties. First, I accept that relation shares existence with other things, and that it must be distinct from them. But I do not accept that it must differ through a further feature added to it. Many things are distinct from others through their very existence. What they share when we call them all “existing” is an idea drawn out by the mind. Existing things, precisely as existing things, do not all belong to one natural kind whose individual members would need extra features to distinguish them.

Nor is every feature that makes a thing more specific something added to it as a separate being. Consider how the defining feature of a particular kind specifies its broader kind. The broader and narrower meanings may have one simple existence outside the mind. No further relation is needed to join them.

Second, something existing in itself may be possible or necessary, a substance or a quality. It is related only insofar as it exists in a way that makes us think of something else when we understand it. Not every existence is a relation simply because it exists. Consider an animal. In itself it has the existence of an animal; considered as able to produce another animal from its reproductive matter, it has another aspect of existence. The first belongs to the category of substance; the second to the category of relation.

Third, a relation does not have a separate, settled existence like other qualities. Its appearance or disappearance therefore need not change the thing to which it is attributed, either in its own being or in its real qualities. One side of a relation may change while the other remains as it was. A person in a room can become one of two people, then one of three, then one of four, without any change in that person or their qualities. Likewise, a change in relations involving God, whose existence is necessary, does not mean a change in God Himself or in any of His perfections. Once the preceding account is clear, this is not difficult to understand.

#### VII. When One Side of a Relation Becomes Specific, So Does the Other

If fatherhood is taken generally, it corresponds to sonhood in general. If fatherhood is taken as a specific kind, it corresponds to sonhood as a specific kind. And when fatherhood becomes an individual relation, the other side becomes individual sonhood. This rule applies when we make the relation itself more specific. It does not always apply when we make only the subject of the relation more specific.

For example, “son of this man” can apply to any number of individual sons; the phrase does not have to name one person only. But the fatherhood of Zayd in relation to Amr is determined by both men. Someone might think the relation of fatherhood is different from what I have said, since Zayd cannot have two fathers or two mothers. But this limit comes from something outside the relation. It does not mean that fatherhood becomes individual on one side while sonhood remains general on the other. In some cases, to identify one particular relation, we must specify more than the two people between whom it holds.

The same two people are enough to identify Amr's fatherhood in relation to Zayd. But they are not enough to identify the fact that Zayd is Amr's neighbor. Their homes could be next door, or there could be forty homes between them. To identify their particular neighborly relation, we must identify both homes as well as the people.[^V04-62]

Some relations are very broad kinds, such as agreement. Others are narrower kinds, such as equality; narrower still is equality of surface area, then equality of shape between two triangles, then between right-angled triangles, and still narrower when the two remaining angles are also equal. These are levels of making a relation more specific by its defining feature.

A relation can also become specific through the subject it belongs to. A feature that is not part of the relation itself may be added to its subject. If that feature were absent, the same kind of relation could still remain. For example, a man can be a just father or an unjust father. The quality of being just or unjust belongs to the father, not to fatherhood itself.

#### VIII. Different Ways to Classify Relations

Some relations have different things on their two sides, as with father and son, double and half, or a square number and its square root. Others have the same kind of thing on both sides, as with equal things, brothers, or neighbors. In a pair of unlike things, the difference may have a fixed limit, as with half and double. Or it may have no fixed limit. Some unlimited relations are based on a fixed one, such as many times more or fewer, or the whole and one of its parts. Others have no fixed limit and are not based on a limited relation, such as greater and less.

Relations can also be divided by whether either side needs another real feature, beyond simply existing, in order to be related. Some do not: right and left, earlier and later.

In others, each side has a real feature that makes it related. A lover has a way of perceiving the beloved, and the beloved has a feature by which the lover perceives it. In still others, only one side has an added feature. A knower has knowledge and is related through it; the thing known does not gain anything new in itself by being known.

This is what we find in Avicenna and other writers, though I have already questioned some of it. Avicenna says in *The Healing* that relations fall into a few main kinds: equality, increase, action and being acted on, and likeness. Increase may be in amount, which is clear, or in power, as when something overcomes, dominates, or holds back another. Action and being acted on include father and son, or a cutter and what is cut. Likeness includes knowledge and what is known, and sensation and what is sensed: knowledge reflects the form of what is known, and sensation reflects the form of what is sensed. It is not easy to find a clear rule that covers all these cases.

Relations also occur in every category of things. In substance, we find father and son, knower and known. In quantity, we find large and small for continuous amounts, many and few for separate amounts. In quality, we find hotter and colder; in relation, nearer and farther; in place, higher and lower; in time, older and newer; in position, more upright or more bent; in having, better clothed or less clothed; in acting, more or less cutting; and in being acted on, more or less heated or cut.

#### IX. Can Relations Be Opposites or Have Degrees?

A relation, considered by itself, does not have an independent existence. As we have seen, it depends on the things that bear it. So it follows those things in all matters of existence. Opposition, before and after, potential and actual, and similar features belong directly to the things themselves; they belong to their relations only because those relations depend on them. For example, hot and cold are truly opposed. The relations “hotter” and “colder” are opposed only because the things that bear them are hot and cold. A relation cannot be opposed to another if we set aside the things it belongs to.

Avicenna's discussion of quantity seems to say that relations themselves cannot be opposed. He gives two reasons. First, opposition is not the same as being related. Opposite qualities are not always related to each other, and many related things are not opposites, such as knowledge and what is known, or a neighbor and the person next door. Since opposition itself is a kind of relation, the things that oppose one another must also have something in them that is not a relation. That something is the subjects that bear the opposition. So opposition belongs only to things that are not themselves relations. Second, relations do not exist independently, so they cannot be the direct subjects of opposition. To be a subject at all, something must exist in its own right.

But in his discussion of relation, Avicenna says that relations share the features of the category to which they belong. Since greater and less belong to quantity, which has no opposites, he says that they have no opposites. But excellence and its opposite are qualities; so the relations of being more or less excellent can be opposites. Likewise, since hot and cold are opposites, hotter and colder can be opposed. There is no conflict between these statements. He denies opposition to relations considered on their own, but allows it when a relation follows from something in its subject. In the chapter on quantity, he says relations are not opposed in their own right; he does not deny that they can be opposed through subjects outside quantity.

The same applies to whether relations can be stronger or weaker, or increase or decrease. A relation can share these features when the category of its subject allows them. These are general rules about relations. It is also useful to discuss some of their kinds in particular.

#### X. Universal and Particular, Essential and Accidental

“Universal” is a relational feature that can belong to a nature: it means that the nature is shared by many things. Any nature other than existence itself can have this feature. It moves from possibility to actuality when individual examples of it appear. This relational feature is different from a thing's simply being able to apply to many or to be shared among them. As we have seen, “universal” may mean the feature itself, the thing that has the feature, or both together. Here I mean the feature itself.

“Broader kind” is also a relational feature that applies to some natures. It may mean the thing that has the feature, such as animal; the feature itself; or the feature together with the thing it describes. The first is called a natural broader kind, the second a logical broader kind, and the third a mental broader kind: a feature joined to the thing it describes.[^V04-63] The first is a simple relation, the second is the category of relation itself, and the third is a combination. The same distinctions apply to species, defining feature, property, and accident. These relations all exist in thought.

If you keep this in mind, the universal understood as a relation is a general kind that includes five types: broader kind, species, defining feature, property, and accident. By these we mean the ideas themselves, not the things they describe or the ideas joined to those things. The thing that has the feature of being a broader kind is, in turn, a natural broader kind in relation to them. Each of the five is a type of universal in this sense, even if it is a broader kind, a defining feature, or something else in relation to the things it describes.

When people say that a species falls under a broader kind, or that a broader kind and a defining feature are parts of a species, they do not mean that the idea of “species” falls under the idea of “broader kind,” or that the concepts of broader kind and defining feature are parts of the concept of species. They mean that the nature described as a species falls under the thing described as a broader kind, or belongs in its nature when that nature is made up of a broader kind and a defining feature.

The same applies to a defining feature and its place in a species. The five features—broader kind, species, defining feature, property, and accident—are truly separate kinds under one general idea: “universal.” The general idea is an added relation to the things that have it, but being universal is part of what it means to be a broader kind. These are subtle distinctions. We must pay attention to them, because confusing them leads to many mistakes.

“Universal” itself can mean several things. It can mean the nature that the mind understands before it adds the feature of being universal. It can mean that nature together with the feature of universality. Or it can mean the relation itself: that this nature is shared by many. It can also mean a nature that, if joined with different matter and surrounding features, would be another individual. These are not the same as the logical meaning of universal.

People agree that the first, second, and fourth of these meanings exist in the outside world. They disagree about whether the third—the separate forms described by Plato—exists there. I have already argued that a natural universal does not exist on its own; what exists on its own is the particular thing. I have also set out the case for Plato's separate forms. The relation between a whole and its parts is another kind of relation, different from the relation between a universal and its individuals.

Here are the differences between a whole and a universal. First, a whole exists in the outside world, while a universal as universal exists only in the mind. Second, we count a whole by its parts, but we do not count a universal by its individuals.

Third, a universal may help make up an individual, while a whole is made up of its parts. Fourth, the nature of a whole does not become one of its parts, but the nature of a universal can be identical with an individual. “Human being,” for example, is this human being. Fifth, no single part is the whole, but each individual on its own can be called by the universal that applies to it. Sixth, a whole has a limited number of parts, while the individuals that fall under a universal may be endless.

Completeness, incompleteness, self-sufficiency, and being beyond completeness are also related ideas. Something is complete when it has everything it should have. “Complete” can be said of many kinds of things. We may call a number complete when it has everything that belongs to a certain numerical pattern. Most people do not call a number below three complete, since three has a beginning, a middle, and an end. No number is complete simply as a number, because a larger number has something that a smaller one does not. A number is complete only within a particular pattern, such as a group of ten or a group of five. It can be incomplete because it lacks a middle between its beginning and end, or because it has only a beginning and middle, or a middle and end. Nor can a number have two beginnings with no middle between them, except when there are only two numbers; the same applies to two ends.

There can be many middle terms, since together they count as one middle. There is no fixed limit to how many there can be. So having a beginning, a middle, and an end is the fullest pattern, and three is the smallest number that has all three. This is what Avicenna says in *The Healing*.

We also call a bodily measure complete, as when someone's height is right for a person like them. Measures are defined by setting a fitting limit. We speak of qualities and powers as complete too: a complete strength, sense, or knowledge. Philosophers also use “complete” to mean that a thing has all its perfections in actuality. Sometimes they add that both its existence and its perfections must come from itself, not from another.

If a thing that has all its own perfections also gives existence to other things, it is beyond complete: it has all the existence and perfection it should have, and more, because it gives existence to others as well. Only God has this quality. Intellects are complete in the first sense, but not in the second, since possible beings depend on another for their existence. Something that falls short of completeness comes in two kinds. A self-sufficient thing has been given what it needs to gain its own perfections. The souls of the heavens are like this: they continually gain their perfections without needing an outside teacher such as we do. They are perfected and taught, and brought from possibility into actuality, by an intellect that gives freely of itself and is not separate from them in its way of existing. The souls of the prophets are likewise self-sufficient, since God gave them what they need to reach their perfections and closeness to Him. The other kind is incomplete: it needs something outside itself to bring it to perfection, as with things that come to be and pass away.

Completeness differs from “whole” and “all” in how we use the terms. A complete thing does not have to contain many parts, either actually or potentially. God, for example, is complete. A whole or all, by contrast, must include many things. When something does contain many parts, “whole” and “complete” refer to the same thing in two ways: we call it a whole because it includes those parts, and complete because nothing that should be outside it remains outside.

You may ask what the philosophers mean when they say that God is all things. I reply that the meaning is subtle. Because God is utterly simple and one, no reality is outside His essence. Yet no thing exists in Him as a separate part, and there is no multiplicity in Him, either actual or possible.

### Article Two: The Remaining Categories

#### I. What “Where” Means

“Where” has been defined as a thing's being in its proper place. It should not mean merely the relation between a thing and its place, or it would belong to the category of relation. Rather, it is a state of the thing to which that relation to its place belongs.

Some say that blackness has its own nature, and that its being in a subject is a relation added to it. They say “where” works the same way: it has its own nature, and being in a place is a relation added to it.[^V04-64] But this is not right. A thing's being in a place is not like blackness being in a subject. Blackness exists only by being in a subject; that is how blackness itself exists. Its existence differs from its nature in thought, not in reality. But a thing's being in a place must be something added to its existence in itself. Otherwise, when it left its place, it would cease to exist and would have to come into existence again somewhere else. The thing that had disappeared would be brought back to life. So being in a place is not the same as existing outside the mind. If it were, being in time would also be existence, and one thing would have many existences.

One could say that a physical thing's being somewhere in general, or existing at some time in general, is simply its own way of existing. But being in a particular place or at a particular time is something added to its existence in itself. We call that added feature “where” or “when.” Neither one is the relation itself; each is what gives rise to a relation to a place or time.

Someone has also argued that being in a place cannot be the same as existing outside the mind. Existence applies to all things that exist. If existence meant being in a place, then everything that exists would have to be in a place. But not everything is. So being in a place must differ from existence in itself.

I answer that the same argument could be made about every particular thing that exists. It would imply that being physical, being separate from matter, or being able to sense are features added to each thing's existence. That is not so. The mistake is to treat general existence as one shared kind that applies to every individual in the same way. As I have said many times, the idea of existence in general is an abstract idea; it is not one common nature found in every existing thing.

#### II. Different Kinds of “Where”

There are two ways to divide “where.” First, some where is exact and real; some is broad and not exact. The first is a thing's being in the particular place that belongs to it, where no other thing can occupy the same place with it, as water in a cup. The second is what we mean when we say that someone is in a house. A house can hold many things, so it is not all filled by one person. Broader still is being in a dwelling, then in a city, a country such as Iraq, a region, the inhabited world, the world of elements, or the physical universe contained by the outermost sphere.
