This is the broadest reach. But simply being in the universe is not a “where” in the strict sense.

The second division is into general kind, specific kind, and individual case. The general kind is being in a place. Specific kinds include being in air or water, above or below. An individual case is this person being in this particular place at this particular time.

Some say that each exact, individual place must be caused by a quality in the thing located there. The author of *The Discussions* objects that this cannot be right. If the quality could exist when the thing was not in that particular place, it would not cause that place; the effect would be absent while its cause was present. But if the quality could not exist unless the thing were in that place, then the quality would depend on the place, and the place would depend on the quality. That would be circular reasoning.

I see two problems with this objection. First, the quality in question may be one part of the cause, a condition, or a factor that prepares the thing. It need not be the sole cause that must always coincide with the effect. Second, even if the quality occurs only when the object is in its place, the two may simply coincide in time; that does not show that one causes or precedes the other. The difference is like the difference between saying, “this holds while the quality is present,” and saying, “this holds on the condition that the quality is present.” The second is narrower, and the broader statement does not imply it. Every writer must move their fingers while writing, and that movement is required at that time because of the causes at work then. Writing does not cause the fingers to move. If anything, the movement of the fingers brings writing about in the world.

#### III. Further Features of “Where”

“Where” can have opposites. Being at the outer edge of a place is opposite to being at its center. These are both real states, they differ as much as possible, and they cannot belong to the same thing at the same time. Yet one can follow the other. So they are opposites.

“Where” can also be stronger or weaker, but not simply as “where.” One thing is not more in its place than another just because both are in their places. Being somewhere does not come in degrees. Bodies can, however, be more or less in a particular kind of place, such as above or below. If two bodies are both above and one is higher—closer to the outer boundary—it is more above than the other.

So strength and weakness do not belong to “where” itself. They belong to some of its kinds, such as above and below. But this is not because the added relations of being above or below grow stronger or weaker, as the author of *The Discussions* claims. As we have already seen, relation itself does not take degrees.

#### IV. What “When” Means and Its Kinds

“When” is one of the categories. It means that a thing exists at a particular time or at some limit of time. Many things occur at the edges of time without occupying a stretch of time, yet we can ask when they happen. Arrivals, contacts, and other events of this kind occur at something that has some connection to time.[^V04-65] “When” can be general or particular, just as “where” can: a thing may be in time in general, in a certain time, or at a particular instant.

A real “when” is a time that exactly matches the thing's duration, with nothing left over. A second, less exact kind is what we mean when we say that a motion lasting an hour took place at the start of the day, in a certain month, year, century, or era, or simply in time. This is like the broader meanings of “where” described earlier. One difference between the two is that many things can share the same real time.

It is often said that many things cannot share one real place. That is not quite right. A real place is unshared only when we also assume a single, actual time. In the same way, when there is one actual place, a real time cannot be shared. Just as each thing in a place has its own “where,” each event has its own “when”; neither is shared with another. That is why place and time are said to be alike.

People then say that motions and moving things have a “when” in themselves. Their substances do not have one in themselves; they are in time only because of their motions. I say instead that the existence of a substantial nature is renewed and flowing. This is a gradual way of existing that matches time. Some quantities, qualities, positions, and places also exist gradually, so they too have gradual states of being. Motion is a gradual process that occurs in a state of being; it is not that gradual state of being itself. Motion is therefore a relative feature. As we have seen, a relative feature can be stronger or weaker only because of the thing it belongs to. “When” belongs first to these gradual states of being, and only then to their natures. Motion itself is not something gradual in addition to its process: motion is that process. So it has a “when” only because of something else, not in itself.

Anyone who defines time only as the measure of motion in terms of earlier and later must say that every motion in the world below the heavens has its own time. The definition needs another condition: it must mean the measure of the celestial sphere's motion, or of an unbroken motion, or of the clearest, fastest, or strongest motion.

#### V. Position

Position is a body's arrangement, with its parts related to one another in different directions, as when it is standing or sitting. Position is not the relations among the parts themselves. Those relations belong to the category of relation, as does being next to something. Rather, position is the body's state in which its parts have these relations, as we have explained.

This position is also different from the position of a point, which means that the point can be shown by the senses. It is different, too, from the position discussed as part of quantity. The position we are discussing takes account of how a body's parts relate to what contains them, what they contain, and the directions outside them. The position discussed under quantity does not. Some define it as a thing's being such that we can point to where it is in relation to what it is joined to by a fixed connection.[^V04-66] But this mixes two things together. If this “where” were a real place, there would be no difference between position in this sense and the category of position itself. The parts of a body do not each have an actual place, with their places joined together so that we can say where one part is in relation to another.

If someone says the difference is that one applies to measured quantity and the other to a natural body, I answer that a natural body gets position through its measured size. Without size, it could not be divided into parts by measure, as we explained when discussing quantity. So position belongs to a body through its size, whatever kind of body it is.

In my view, we must distinguish position as a feature of a body from position in quantity and its boundaries. A thing's merely being a continuous quantity or size is not enough for the senses to point it out as here or there, above or below, or in some direction. This ability belongs to continuous quantity when joined to matter that can undergo change, motion, and difference. Size on its own can be pointed to in imagination, but not by the senses. Earlier writers sometimes used “sense” loosely to mean anything short of the intellect. A point, line, surface, or three-dimensional body is not, by itself, located in a direction or available to the senses. It can be sensed and located only when it exists in matter that makes it possible to point to it, place it in a direction, divide it, and so on.

When we imagine size apart from matter, it still has a boundary and parts joined together, but not in the sense that one can ask where each part is in relation to another. We can imagine its shape even though that shape has no place. Its surfaces and lines also have positions in another sense, and the points on a line have positions that cannot be shown by the senses. In short, the intellect points things out in one way, the senses in another, and the imagination in a third. The intellect has position for ideas and general things; the imagination has position for mental images; and the senses have position for material things. This last kind may belong to a point, to a size divided in some directions but not all, or to a body divided in every direction. The position that is a category is this last kind. Position as a part of a category, too, occurs only among features of matter.

A point cannot be shown to the senses unless it exists in this world. And the parts of a line or surface cannot be said to have one place in relation to another unless they exist in matter that can receive this position. Position can be natural or not natural, actual or potential. The earth's position within the celestial sphere is natural and actual, since their places are distinct in reality. The position of someone living in a house is actual but not natural: their places differ, but not by nature. A potential position is one we only imagine, such as the closeness of the circle of a millstone to its center and its relation to the circle around the center. These circles are not actual, so their position exists only in imagination or potentially.

Position can have opposites and can come in degrees. A person upright, head toward the sky and feet toward the earth, has the opposite position to that person turned upside down. These are two real states that follow one another in the same subject, cannot occur in it together, and differ as much as possible. The same is true of lying on one's back and lying face down. Position also comes in degrees: someone can stand more upright or bend more deeply.

You might ask: in our discussion of qualities tied to quantity, we said that they cannot have opposites or come in degrees. We said that a line cannot become more or less round, and a surface cannot become more or less curved. How can we now say that straightness and bending do come in degrees, when these features first belong to measured sizes?

I answer that the relation between this straightness and bending and the features discussed earlier is like the relation between position as a category and the geometric positions we just described. A circle considered apart from matter cannot become more round while remaining the same subject, because there is no matter there that could change. But when roundness, bending, or another such feature occurs in particular matter, it falls under the category of position. Then it can have opposites, come in degrees, and so on. Natural bending can grow stronger; geometric bending cannot. The former belongs to the category of position, the latter does not.

Keep this distinction in mind; many people miss it.

#### VI. Possession

Possession is another category. It is a state that arises when one body is wholly or partly surrounded by another, and the surrounding body moves when the surrounded body moves. Examples include wearing armor or clothes, a turban, a ring, or shoes. Possession can be natural, as an animal is with its hide, or acquired, as when someone wears armor or clothes.

“Having” can also mean what something has. In this sense it may be natural, as the powers belong to the soul, or based on an outside convention, as a horse belongs to Zayd. Strictly speaking, this kind of ownership is different from the category of possession. It belongs only to the category of relation.

#### VII. The Categories of Acting and Being Acted On

The first is a substance acting on something else and producing a changing effect in it for as long as the action continues. Heating, for example, is present while something is warming; cooling is present while something is cooling. The second is a substance being affected by something else, with a changing effect for as long as the change continues, as when something is warming or turning black.

Once the agent has finished acting or the patient has finished being affected—in other words, once their ongoing change has ended—we no longer call one a mover and the other something being moved. Warming ends in a stable state of heat; blackening ends in a stable state of blackness. We call these categories “acting” and “being acted on,” rather than “action” and “effect,” because those latter words can also mean producing something without motion or receiving something without change. For example, God is said to produce the world and the world to receive existence from Him, though there is no motion in either the giver or the receiver.[^V04-67] In that case, one existence follows another and only a relation arises between them. The agent and receiver are related in that sense alone. This differs from the two categories of ongoing change, which take place in time.

What about a state that occurs when an agent's motion ends, or when the thing being moved stops moving—for example, something being cut at the end of a motion, or a garment burning after it has come to rest? Do these states belong to the two categories? We can count them as belonging to these categories when we specify that they occur at the ends of the motions. The same applies to anything that occurs at an instantaneous limit or endpoint of a motion. Without that qualification, they do not belong to these categories.

These categories can have opposites and come in degrees. Whitening is opposite to blackening, and heating to cooling, just as white is opposite to black and hot to cold. They can also grow stronger. One process of blackening may be closer to its final state than another; one may reach that state faster than another. This increase or decrease is different from the degrees of a quality such as blackness. It is not measured against blackness itself, but against blackening—the process of becoming black. The process of becoming black is not the same as being black. That is what has been said.

Know that the existence of either category in the outside world is not the gradual process itself, since that is motion. Nor is it simply the existence of the category that is being changed, such as a quality like blackness, a quantity like the size of a growing body, or a position like sitting or standing. Their existence is the existence of one of these categories while it is acting or being acted on. Blackness or heat, considered just as blackness or heat, belongs to the category of quality. But either one, considered as coming about gradually and producing another gradual change—or coming about through another gradual change—belongs to the category of acting or being acted on. The gradual process itself—the shift from what something can be to what it actually is, whether in what acts or in what is acted on—is motion and nothing else. This shows how these categories exist outside the mind and why they are accidents.[^V04-68]

#### VIII. Answering an Objection to the Existence of These Categories

The author of *The Discussions* argues as follows. One thing's acting on another cannot be a positive feature added to the agent and its effect. In the same way, one thing's being affected by another—its capacity to receive an effect—cannot be a positive feature added to the receiver and what it receives.

If acting were something real in its own right, it would be one of the things that needs an agent in order to exist. That agent's acting on the act would then be something added to it, and the same question would arise again. The argument would continue without end, which is impossible. Even if an endless chain were possible, the problem would remain. Suppose there were an endless sequence of agents and effects, with an act between each agent and its effect. Either two items in the sequence meet directly, with nothing between them, or they do not.

If two meet directly, then there is an agent and an affected thing between which no third item intervenes. In that case, the agent's act is not something added to the agent and its effect. So the first agent's act on the second, the second's act on the third, the third's on the fourth, and so on, are not all extra features added to their agents and effects. If no two items meet directly, then there is no agent that directly acts on anything else. That denies the existence of agents altogether. The same argument applies to receptivity: if it were a positive feature in its own right, the same regress and difficulty would follow.

He says this is a decisive proof that acting and being acted on cannot be positive features. He adds that, as will be shown in the discussion of causes, if acting were a positive feature, the Necessary Existent—God, exalted is He—would not exist. That is his argument, in brief.

I say: this long argument comes from misunderstanding several principles of philosophy, including the nature of existence, its being distinct from essence, and the fact that every essence has its own mode of existence. Essences differ in meaning and concept, and they also differ in existence, though the existence of one may in some way be united with the existence of another. For an essence or meaning to exist outside the mind is for its definition to apply truly to something outside the mind. The Sheikh uses this point to show that relation exists outside the mind: there are many things in the world that, when understood, require us to understand something else along with them.

Once this is clear, we can say that, just as many things truly fit the definition of substance, quantity, quality, and the other categories—and the definitions of their kinds, such as a celestial sphere, an animal, blackness, or a surface—so there are many things in the world to which one of these two definitions applies. Some fit the definition of acting, others that of being acted on. Both are therefore real features of the world, not imaginary things with no external existence, not logical concepts such as universal and particular, broader kind, species, and difference, and not negations or negative concepts.

For something to exist outside the mind means that its meaning truly applies to something outside the mind, as happens with the predicates in statements about actual things. Whether these two categories have an existence added to that of the agent and the thing affected—and so count as accidents—or do not—and so count as substances—is a separate question. One can also show that they are accidents by noting that each agent and each thing affected has an essence and a definition apart from its being an agent or patient. For example, a kind of blackness can exist without growing more intense; in that case it is black without undergoing a gradual change in its blackness. So becoming blacker is something added to blackness. Likewise, heat can exist without gradually increasing. Heating something, which happens over time, is therefore a further feature beyond heat simply existing.

As for the claim that, if acting were something real, it would itself need an agent, we reply that some features do need a cause, but not a new cause separate from the cause of the thing they belong to. A maker does not come between a thing and what belongs to its essence, nor between a thing's existence and what necessarily follows from that existence. Fire is not first made into a substance and then separately made hot. Rather, making it exist also makes it hot as a consequence, even though the existence of heat is not the same as the existence of fire: fire is a substance, while heat is an accident. The same applies here.

An act that unfolds over time may be something real in the world, yet follow necessarily from the existence of certain instances of a category. A heat that warms something, for example, may be an added concept beyond the nature of heat without being a separate thing beyond this particular instance of heat. Even when the act is distinct from the agent in its individual existence, it need not have another maker or another act of making. It may follow from that existent nature, just as what follows from an essence needs no new making beyond the making of the essence itself. So the ongoing act's being something real, added to the agent, does not mean that it needs another act or another agent.

It is also not true that the agent's act upon that act must be something further, added to both. The changing act, which belongs to the category of acting, may be something added to the agent; but the creative act is not an extra thing in the outside world, added to the agent. It is the agent's own relation of acting and bringing about. We have already learned that relations do not exist independently in the outside world.

### Part Four: Substance, its basic kinds, and their properties

These topics concern features of being as such, so they belong in general philosophy, which studies the features of existence without considering the particular details of individual things. This art has an introduction and several questions. The introduction explains the nature of substance and accident and their general features, in chapters.

#### I. Examining the Nature of Substance and Accident

Know that some things are said to exist in themselves, while others are said to exist only by extension. An example of the first is “a human being is an animal”: the existence attributed to “human being” truly belongs to “animal” as well, since animal is truly predicated of human being. An example of the second is “Zayd is blind” or “Zayd is white.” The existence attributed to Zayd does not truly belong to blindness or whiteness. It is attributed to them indirectly, through some connection between the predicate and its subject. The connection may be negative or positive: the source of the predicate is either drawn from the subject or present in it. In this second kind of statement, it is the subject that exists, but its existence is attributed indirectly to the predicate. This kind of existence has no clear limit, so we will set it aside and focus on what exists in itself.

We have learned that every existent is either such that it can be said, “Its reality is its existence”—meaning that it has no essence apart from pure existence, beyond which nothing is more complete—or it is not. Some of the features of the first kind have already been discussed at the level of meaning; we will later examine its existence, its perfections, and its properties as existence. Here we are discussing things whose existence is not their essence in the sense explained earlier: they do not exist through themselves, but through something else.

The most basic division among these things is substance and accident. A thing either exists in a subject or does not. By “existing in a subject,” we mean that its existence is in something else—not as a part of it, but as something that accompanies it and cannot be separated from it.

Thinkers have found it difficult to explain what it means for something to be “in” a subject. The word “in” is used in many ways, and those uses do not all share one meaning. We speak of something being in time, in a place, in the mind, in the outside world, in an end or purpose, in a whole, in its parts, in a general kind, in a particular thing, in a field, in comfort, or in a word. These uses do not all have one common meaning. Nor can “in” simply mean “related to,” since then it would also apply to “with,” “from,” “on,” and other relational words, as well as to fatherhood, sonship, and similar relations.

If we narrow it to a relation that is not understood on its own, this may exclude things that have their own names. But that single feature is not specific enough to cover the various uses of “in” while excluding everything else. “When” and “where,” for example, both use “in,” yet there is no one special feature common to both that makes the word apply to them. Each has its own feature that brings it under the word.

The same is true when still more cases are added. In many of them, “in” is used metaphorically, by resemblance. A whole is said to be in its parts because it is identical with all of them together; a part is said to be in a whole. If the whole were in a part in the same way that the part is in the whole, the whole would contain itself, which is obviously impossible. You might say that “in” means containment or surrounding, and so can be applied to all these cases. But then we are back where we started, because containment is itself understood in different ways. Time contains motion differently from the way a place contains a body. And a thing's being in a motion is not the same as a motion's being in a thing.

The point is that “being in a subject” has no clear, shared meaning that covers all the cases in which “in” is used. We are not trying to study the meanings of the word itself; that would not belong to this subject, even if it might help a reader who wants to follow the argument.

If “in” is used in different senses, the qualifications people add to distinguish “being in a subject” are not precise safeguards that narrow one shared meaning. A word with several meanings does not need one further conceptual feature to distinguish those meanings: it shifts from one meaning to another through context, whether verbal or conceptual. To clear up doubt about a shared word, one can define the intended meaning, describe it, or rule out the meanings that are not intended. In that case, the features in the usual definition of inherence—something's existing in something else, not as its part, spread throughout it, and unable to be separated from it—serve as clues to which use of “in” is meant. They work like a description, with a degree of looseness.

If, however, “in” did have one shared meaning that applied both to what is in a subject and to other things, the definition's qualifications would distinguish the subject's case from the others. Being spread throughout and joined to the whole would distinguish it from something particular in a general kind, and from something in time or place. Being unable to separate or move away would distinguish it from existing in a place, in comfort, or in a field. Many definitions of inherence have been offered, but each has some fault, either by including too much, leaving out too much, or both. For example, some call it “a feature that describes its subject,” or “a relation in which one thing is a description and the other is described by it.” If “description” means direct predication, this definition excludes blackness, whiteness, and similar cases; if it means predication through a derivative form, it includes places, stars, and bodies in general.

Some have tried to avoid this by saying that “describes” does not mean predication through a derivative form. Others define inherence as one thing's flowing through another so that pointing to either one would point to the other as well. When that definition was challenged by the states of things separate from matter, they added “actually or potentially.” But it still fails to cover points, fatherhood, and other things that do not flow through their subjects. So they resorted to denying that such things exist outside the mind. They did not notice that this objection is not limited to external endpoints and boundaries; it also applies to imagined or assumed things. In our commentary on *Athir al-Din's Guidance*, we discussed some of the objections and problems with these definitions.

The definition that God inspired us to give is this: a thing inheres in a subject when its existence in itself is the existence of something else in it, as a feature that describes it. This avoids the objection raised by the substances that proceed from God and the higher principles, since, as we have established, a caused thing's existence in itself is its existence for its cause. This definition avoids both including too much and excluding too much. It applies to all accidents and inhering forms, but not to other kinds of relational presence that are not inherence: a part's being in a whole, a particular's being under a universal, a thing's being in time or place, in comfort or in a field, or a difference's being in its broader kind. A difference's existence is the same as its broader kind's existence, not an existence that belongs to the broader kind as a feature. The same is true of existence belonging to an essence: existence is the essence's own existence, not something that exists in it. In short, this definition has no flaw, as careful examination shows.

Once inherence is understood this way, we see that what inheres must depend on its subject in one of two ways. It may depend on the subject both for its individual existence and for the reality of its kind, so that it cannot exist independently of a subject in any way. Or it may depend on the subject only for its individual existence, while its kind of reality can exist on its own, without a subject. The first is called an accident by everyone. The second is called a form by careful thinkers in Aristotle’s school.

The subject cannot depend for its existence on the particular existence of what inheres in it, whether that thing is an accident or a form. Otherwise each would depend on the other in the same respect, creating an impossible circle. The subject either needs what inheres in it neither for its kind of reality nor for its individual existence; this is called a subject by everyone. Or it needs the general existence of what inheres in it, whatever its individual form may be; the Aristotle’s followers call this matter. Accident and form share the general idea of something inhering; subject and matter share the general idea of a subject. A subject of anything—whether a form, an accident, an accident in a form, or, if possible, an accident in another accident—must ultimately lead to a subject that is not itself in another subject and that supports all the rest. Every accident depends on substance for its existence, but substance does not depend on accident. Substance is therefore prior to accident by nature.

The Stoics hold that anything inhering in something is an accident, and that no substance inheres in anything. Yet they allow some accidents to constitute substance and be prior to it, since they allow substance to be composed of a substantial subject and an inhering accident. On their view, then, substance is not always prior to accident by nature. We will examine this position later, God willing.

They also distinguish form from accident, and subject from matter, by a difference in how we consider them, not by a difference in their reality. Any natural or made kind—such as a bed—that is composed in some way of an inhering element and a subject has, in their terminology, matter (the wood) and form (the particular arrangement). These count as parts of one general kind. They do not require the formal part to complete its subject in such a way that it becomes a substance; otherwise the thing that makes a substance complete would itself have to be more substantial than the substance. Nor must the material part depend on the form for its existence as a kind. For them, what is called “form” is the same accident, considered as a part of a compound; and what is called “matter” is the same subject, considered as a part of the compound formed with what inheres in it. On this account, all accidents that specify or classify a thing can count as forms, and their subjects as their matter.

Now that we have seen how these two positions and their terms differ, and recalled the logical rule that denying a general feature—or something equally general that follows from it—is narrower than denying a specific feature—or something equally specific that follows from it, let us consider the results. Substance, or what is equally specific to it, is defined as what does not exist in a subject. Since “subject” is narrower than “substrate” for Aristotle’s followers, its negation is broader than the negation of “substrate.” So substance includes both what is in a substrate and what is not, under the single description “not existing in a subject.”

Likewise, accident—what exists in a subject—includes both the inhering thing and its subject for anyone who allows one accident to exist in another. It is broader than either one on its own, since what inheres may be an accident or a form, and its subject may be a substance or, on this view, an accident.

On the account given by Aristotle’s followers, substance has five primary kinds. It either exists in a subject or does not. What exists in a subject is material form. What does not exist in a subject either supports something that depends on it, in which case it is matter, or does not. If it does not, it is either composed of matter and form, in which case it is body, or it is not. The latter either has some kind of relation to a body through being acted on by it, in which case it is soul, or has no such relation, in which case it is intellect.

A better division is this: if a substance can receive three dimensions, it is body. If it cannot, then either it has a part that makes it actual—whether as part of its kind or of its species—in which case it is form, either spatial or natural; or it has a part that exists only potentially, in which case it is matter. If it has no part, then if it directly governs a body, it is soul; otherwise it is intellect. This distinction will become clearer as our discussion proceeds. The human rational substance, for example, is matter for the intellectual form through which it becomes another, more complete substance in actuality, belonging to a kind that exists in a different way from the natural kinds that have bodily existence.[^V04-69] Understanding this is a gift of God's grace.

#### II. Defining Accident

This topic does not follow directly from the previous division of everything that exists into what is in a subject and what is not. Still, following the early philosophers, we will give here the usual definition of accident. An accident is something that exists in another thing, is not what makes that thing what it is, is not a part of it, and cannot exist apart from what it is in. These are four qualifications.

The phrase “in another thing” rules out one accident existing in two or more things. But the subject of a number, a whole, a structure, or the like need not be one simple thing in reality. It is enough that it be one in some respect that makes it a subject. Ten, for example, is the subject of the number ten as a whole; its subject is the collection of units, not each unit individually. Otherwise each unit would be a ten, and there would be many tens.

You might ask how this unity belongs to the subject. We answer that it need not be an external feature added to the subject. It may help constitute the subject, as existence relates to essence—especially on our view, explained earlier, that each thing's unity is the very mode of existence by which it is what it is. Existence is not an accident in what it makes exist. It is the form through which a thing's reality is made actual. This also answers the difficult objection that has troubled many scholars, as you have already seen.

As for relations, especially relations whose two sides are alike, such as being next to, close to, together with, or being brothers, we will show that each side—whether the sides differ, as in cause and effect or before and after, or are alike, as in similarity, shared kind, and brotherhood—has something particular to it that the other side does not have.

Some may think that “in another thing” is meant to exclude a whole's being in its parts. This is mistaken. It is only a figurative way of speaking to say that a whole exists in its parts. The whole exists in itself, not in its parts. If its existence were related to its parts, then either it would exist in each part separately, making each part a whole and producing many wholes, contrary to what we assumed; or it would exist in all the parts together, which is also impossible, since it is those parts taken together, not something that exists inside them. That would make a thing exist in itself, which is impossible because a thing is not different from itself. A whole has a complete form in its parts, and that form is nothing other than the existence of all its parts together. It is not another thing inside them, as some have supposed, nor does it exist in each part individually.

This makes clear what we have established: the natural form of a physical compound is the existence of that compound itself. In the outside world, matter and form make one thing. The form of ten, for example, is all the units that make up its matter. Consider these points carefully and without hesitation.

The phrase “is not what makes it what it is” rules out something that inheres in matter and makes it actual as a kind of thing. Such a thing is not an accident; it is a substantial form.

Know that features of things can be divided into several kinds. The thing described may already have a settled, actual nature, or it may not. If it does, the feature that comes to it may be external to it, either necessary or separable; or it may not come from outside but be part of what makes it what it is. If the subject's nature is not yet settled, the feature may establish it, whether or not that feature is part of the meaning of its essence. Or the feature may only belong to it after its nature is fully established, following from what makes it what it is, either necessarily or separably. Examples of these kinds are: whiteness in a body or laughter in a human being; the soul in an animal;[^V04-70] the natural form in body considered simply as extended nature; a form in prime matter; and whiteness or occupying a place in prime matter.

The form of a species or broader kind, considered as a form or broader kind, is only said to exist in it figuratively, as we explained earlier. When these are considered as forms and broader kinds, their existence is one and the same. To say that one exists “in” or “for” the other implies a difference between them, so this case was already excluded by the first qualification. If, however, broader kind and difference are taken as distinct in meaning, the difference belongs in the definition of the species and completes its meaning, while the broader kind enters into its being and makes it actual, as we explained in the discussion of essence. That is excluded from the definition of accident by the second qualification.

Some define an accident as something that depends on a thing that exists in itself. On its face, this definition is wrong for anyone who allows one accident to exist in another, since it would make the subject accident a substance. We have not established that such accidents exist.

The examples given to prove that one accident can exist in another—speed in motion, straightness in a line, shape on a surface, a point on a line, or a line on a surface—deserve scrutiny. These features seem related to their subjects more like differences to broader kinds in simple essences than like accidents to their subjects. It is striking that Avicenna, who allows one accident to exist in another and says this is not objectionable, uses these examples as proof, even though he explicitly says straightness and roundness are differences of line and surface. He also lays down a test for distinguishing an accident in something from a feature that makes it what it is: a feature is an accident if the subject can exist as the kind of thing it is without it; if it cannot, the two are related as form and matter, or as difference and broader kind.

By this test, motion cannot exist without some degree of speed or slowness. A fast motion is not complete as motion in its essence and kind, only to acquire speed after its nature has been completed. It is complete only with some degree of speed or slowness. These are therefore differences that complete the motion, not accidents that depend on it. Avicenna also argues that these accidents are related to unity and multiplicity, which, as we will show, are both accidents. But you already know that his and others' account of unity as an accident is weak. Each thing's existence is its unity and individuality. The existence of something is not an accident in it that depends on existence to be constituted; the unity of an accident is an accident in the same way as its existence, and the unity of a substance is a substance in the same way as its existence. Multiplicity is nothing but units.

The status of unity and multiplicity in substance and accident follows the status of the units that make them up. Some have explained “what exists in itself” to mean “what does not depend on what inheres in it.” They have overlooked that figurative meanings of words do not belong in strict definitions.

The phrase “not as a part of it” rules out a part's existence in a whole; the existence of a broader kind in a species, considered as a particular kind, and the existence of a species as something general in a broader kind, also considered as general; and the existence of matter and form in a compound. Each of these exists in something of which it is a part. An accident's existence in its subject is different.

The phrase “cannot exist apart from what it is in” means that, by its nature, the thing cannot exist except in a subject, and that, as an individual, it cannot exist except in that particular subject. This distinguishes an accident's existence in its subject from a body's existence in time or place, and from a thing's being in an end or purpose—for example, the soul's being in happiness, or matter's being in form. By their nature, such things can be separated from what they are said to be “in.” If one cannot be separated from another, that is due to something outside its essence and its particular mode of existence, such as the planets' remaining in their spheres or something enclosed remaining within its container. That does not make it an accident.

A body's never being apart from some place or some time, or a human being's never being apart from some end or purpose, does not make the place, time, end, or purpose a subject. As we said, “cannot exist apart from what it is in” means that a thing's nature requires a subject in general and its individual existence requires a particular subject. Universal things do not exist outside the mind as universals; what does not exist cannot have something else existing in it outside the mind.[^V04-71] We are discussing an accident's existence in a subject in the outside world, not in the mind.

It is also open to question whether every body requires some general or particular place, since the outermost sphere cannot itself be said to have a place. Replacing “place” with “region” does not help, because “existing in a subject” does not apply to something in a region if region means position or orientation. It is also questionable that every body must be in some time throughout all times and instants, as an accident must be in its subject.[^V04-72] A body at an instant is not in time.

You might say: if a body can exist at an instant, then while it is there it must either be moving or at rest, since it cannot be neither. But both movement and rest require time. So the body must be in time at the very moment it is outside time, which is impossible.

We can answer in two ways. First, choose the first option: a moving body, such as a sphere, is described at every assumed instant during its motion as moving—that is, as having motion. This statement is broader than the claim that its motion occurs at that instant. “This body is moving at this instant,” or “is in a state of motion at this instant,” can be understood in two ways. In the first, the words “at this instant” describe our calling the body moving.

In the second, the words “at this instant” describe the motion itself, rather than our description of the body.

In the first sense, we need not say that motion or rest exists at an instant. Even if rest means the absence of motion in something that can move, the absence of motion at an instant does not imply that there is rest at that instant. Denying motion at an instant does not mean rest is present there, because rest and motion are opposites and cannot both apply to one thing.[^V04-73] To deny a qualified statement, one may deny the thing itself or only the qualification. So denying motion at an instant is broader than denying motion altogether; the body may be moving in the time that borders that instant, and the denial still holds.

Second, the two options in the objection do not cover every case. A body may be neither moving at an instant nor at rest at an instant. Motion and rest cannot both be absent from reality altogether, but they can both be absent from one level or aspect of reality. Zayd, for example, is neither moving nor still in the sky, even though in reality he is either moving or still somewhere. Reality is broader than the aspect under discussion.

You might object that, on the philosophers' account, celestial bodies have an originating, unchanging existence and cannot leave their particular places. So they are accidents, since they cannot be separated from those places.

I answer that an accident cannot be separated from its subject because its individual existence depends on that subject. A celestial body is different: each one is individuated by itself and by features settled in its own nature, not by being in a particular place. In the natural world, each celestial kind has only one individual. Its occupying a region follows from its individuality and mode of existence.[^V04-74]

You might say this answer does not apply to the matter of celestial bodies. Their matter exists in their forms, while the forms have a settled existence; matter is not part of the forms, and cannot exist apart from them.

Some scholars have answered this objection by saying that we should not say matter exists “in” forms. As we said, “in” means that one thing describes its subject; matter does not describe form. The relation is the other way around. That answer is wholly inadequate. If “we should not say” is only a verbal objection, it does not settle what is true. If it is meant as a claim about reality, the reason given does not prove it. As we have seen, merely being a feature of something is not enough to define what it means to inhere in it, whether “feature” means something directly predicated of it or something predicated by a derivative form.[^V04-75]

If “describing” means following something in existence, then matter is the one that follows form in this sense, since matter depends on form for its existence, as will be explained. The direction of the relation has been reversed. The right answer is that matter has an indefinite, potential existence; form makes that existence actual. Form is a way in which matter itself exists. An accident, by contrast, has an existence that depends on the existence of its subject; it does not exist by being the subject's own existence. In brief, an accident is something that exists in an independently existing thing. Matter is something that exists through an independently existing thing. Another way to put it: an accident's existence in itself is its existence for its subject, while matter's existence in itself is the existence of its form.[^V04-76] The difference is clear.

Know that accident and matter share a low and weak kind of existence, but each has an advantage over the other in one respect and a shortcoming in another. An accident's advantage is that its existence is distinct from its subject's existence; its shortcoming is that it lies outside the subject's essential makeup and has no independent standing at that level. Matter's advantage is that it is part of what makes its subject what it is; its shortcoming is that its own reality is indefinite and its existence is not distinct.

#### III. Defining Substance: Existing Not in a Subject

We have already explained the meaning of substance, “what exists not in a subject,” in a way that avoids including God as a counterexample or excluding the intelligible forms in the mind, for those who think they inhere in the mind. On our view, there is no difficulty: imagined substantial forms are not imprinted in the imagination, nor are intellectual forms imprinted in the intellect. Rather, the knower becomes one with what is known, and the imagining or sensing soul becomes one with its imagined or sensed forms.

There is another way to answer the difficulty raised by substantial forms in the mind. The concepts of things that appear in the mind do not have to be individual instances of themselves. What is in the mind when we think of substance is the concept “what exists not in a subject.” Likewise, what is in the mind when we think of animal is the concept “a substance that can receive three dimensions, grows, and senses.” It does not follow that the concept of substance is itself an instance of substance, or that the meaning of animal is itself an animal—something that can be described as a body, growing, sensing, and moving. What follows is only that the concept contains the features included in its meaning. We can truly say these features of the concept when we are explaining what it means. But that does not make the concept a thing of that kind outside the mind.

Once this is clear, there is no contradiction in saying that something is the concept of substance and an instance of accident, just as a mental concept may be a concept of a particular and an instance of something universal.

We have also mentioned that an unrestricted term derived from a predicate—such as “existent,” “one,” or “writer”—may be understood in two ways. First, it may refer to a thing that is existent, one, or something else. Its meaning then breaks down into a subject, a feature, and the relation between them. For example, “one” may mean a human being described as one. It includes the meaning of humanity, another meaning distinct from it—oneness—and the relation between the two: one is the subject, the other its feature.

Second, it may mean something simple that cannot be analyzed into a subject, a feature, or a relation except by a supposition we introduce, with no reason in the thing itself to require that analysis. This is the meaning of “one” taken simply as one. I am not saying that reason can immediately tell us that either meaning exists outside the mind; each claim requires an argument. The point is only that an unrestricted word can be used for either meaning. The same applies to “existent”: it can mean either the simple or the composed sense. Here it means the composed sense, since we are defining one of the most general kinds. Existence in the simple sense is not a broader kind.

As we have learned, whatever can be universal or general is not the pure reality of existence. Even the concepts “existence” and “thing,” simple though they are, fall short of that reality. Pure existence is too exalted to have a mental image that matches its true nature. What is in the mind is one of its aspects, not its essence: it represents existence but is not existence itself. So the concept “existence” or “existent” can be universal and general like other things in the mind.

Thus, when we say, “Substance exists not in a subject,” we mean that its universal essence, when it exists outside the mind, exists without a subject. A thing's own existence is what brings about its particular effects and is the source of the distinct features that set it apart from other things. The existence attributed to substance is the kind that is not in a subject. When its essence appears in the mind, it exists there in another mode. This does not change its essence or its substantial nature, since one essence may have several modes of existence in different worlds and levels of reality.

Indeed, one and the same meaning can exist in this world sometimes in an independent, immaterial way and sometimes in a material way. Knowledge and power, for example, are found in both angels and animals. The same meaning can also exist in a way that is preserved from corruption, or in a created form that can perish. Bodily existence is shared by celestial and earthly bodies, though the celestial body has it more fully and strongly. God says, “We made the sky a protected roof,” and, “We built above you seven mighty heavens.” Of the earth and what is made from it, He says, “Everyone on it will perish,” and, “You will die, and they too will die.” The universal end and death of all things, however, is a different kind of nonexistence from corruption. It is what is meant by the verse: “The Trumpet will be blown, and all who are in the heavens and the earth will fall unconscious.”

Thus, even if substantial meanings exist in the mind as the philosophers say, we can still say that their external existence does not depend on a subject. In this sense, they are substances. But the Creator, exalted is He, is far above being described in this way, since He sustains Himself, rather than existing through some particular mode of being. This is so if substantiality is taken as a feature of essence. If it means substantial existence, then what distinguishes it from God's existence is possibility rather than necessity, and limitation rather than boundlessness. A limited existence does not go beyond its boundary; in that respect it differs from quantity, quality, and the other categories. God, exalted is He, has no boundary or end. He encompasses all things and is their end.

He is the First and the Last, the Manifest and the Hidden, and He encompasses all things. What distinguishes substantial existence from the existence of accidents—including the existence of substantial concepts in the mind, as those who hold that they inhere there would say—is that substance does not depend on a subject, while accidents do. Hold fast to what we have explained. You will not find it stated with this clarity in the books commonly read.

#### IV. Is “Substance” Predicated of Its Subordinates as a Broader kind?

Most philosophers hold that substance is predicated of the things beneath it as a constituent broader kind, answering the question “What is it?” in a shared sense. A minority hold that it is predicated of them like an external feature that necessarily follows them. They have sometimes supported this view with weak arguments.

First, they say that existence is included in the meaning of substance, yet existence is outside the essences of things. “Not being in a subject” is also outside those essences, since no real essence can be made up of absences and negations. A concept made up of things external to an essence is itself external to it. So the concept of substance lies outside every real essence.

Second, if substance were a broader kind, then the Necessary Existent would have to be composed of a broader kind and a difference, since it shares substantiality with other things.

Third, if substance were a broader kind, then intellects and souls would not be simple in reality, since they share substantiality with bodies. The first thing to proceed from the One Reality would then be many, overturning their principle that multiplicity cannot proceed from the absolutely One.

Fourth, substance applies to originated and created things, to immaterial and material things, and to simple and composite things with varying degrees of priority and worthiness. The substance of the higher world is more worthy of being called substance than things below it; form is more worthy than matter.

Fifth, the universal concepts of bodies are equal in meaning and essence to their particular instances. If substantiality were a constituent of them, it would be equally present in each, which is clearly false.

The first argument is answered by the distinction, explained several times already, between “existent as such, apart from a subject” and “the sort of thing that can exist without a subject.” The first is a feature shared by all essences; the second can be a constituent of some of them.

The second argument has the same flaw as the first. The third can be answered in two ways. First, the first effect is simple in its external reality, so it can proceed from the absolutely One. Second, when we analyze it and consider it as composed in thought, reason sees the difference as prior to the broader kind. There is no contradiction in saying that a cause's influence on the difference is prior to its influence on the broader kind in the order of thought.

The fourth argument is answered by what careful philosophers have established: priority and posteriority may belong either to a thing's own concept or to another concept. The first kind occurs when substance is prior to accident in the meaning of existence: the existence of substance is a cause and comes first, while the existence of an accident that depends on it is caused and comes later. But the meaning of substance does not cause the meaning of accident. Rather, the existence of one causes the existence of the other. Substance, through its existence, causes accident through its existence.

The second kind occurs when one kind of substance is prior to another, or one instance of a kind is prior to another. A father is prior to his son, but not in the meaning of humanity. The son can be called human only once his humanity exists; the father's humanity is not a cause that first produces humanity in the son.

For we have established that making does not act on essences themselves. How, then, could the essence of one thing cause the essence of another and come before it? In truth, humanity applies equally to father and son, just as all essences apply equally to the things that have them, as we have explained. Their existence in time—or outside time—may differ: the father's existence comes before the son's in time. The same applies to the essential priority of intellect over body, and to the relation of matter and form to the compound they make. Matter, as an external part of body, is not what makes the compound substance. Body is substance in itself, without a cause. Neither matter nor form is required for the predicate “substance” to apply to body; nor does the substantiality of either one cause the substantiality of any other substance, whether a body or something else.

I am not speaking here of their being existent substances. Matter and form may have a stronger claim to be called “existent” than body, but that differs from their having a stronger claim to be called “existent not in a subject,” as explained earlier. The relation is like that between five and ten: five is prior to ten in existence, but it is not prior in being a discrete quantity. That kind of priority lies outside the meaning of discrete quantity.

This is what philosophers have said in this kind of discussion. We have already examined degrees of priority and gradation; keep that in mind. In the chapter after this, we will give further evidence for first substances, second substances, and what follows from them. As for the fifth argument, a universal concept of a substance can be called a substance when we say what that concept means. But the concept is not itself a substance in the ordinary sense of a thing that exists independently. We will explain this difference more fully.

#### 1. Discussion and Clarification

In their writings, philosophers sometimes give three arguments to show that universal substances are substances. They call them proofs.

The first says that the meaning of substance they have made into a broader kind is this: a thing is such that, whenever it exists outside the mind, it does not depend on a subject. The universal forms in the mind have this feature, so they too are substances.

The second says that universals are predicated of particulars with the very same meaning, and the particulars are undoubtedly substances. No accident is predicated of something in this way. So no universal substance is an accident; and whatever is not an accident is a substance. Therefore, universal substances are substances.

The third says that if an individual is a substance because of its individual identity, then nothing else could be a substance. But if it is a substance because of its essence, then its essence must be a substance, and so the universal essence must also be a substance.[^V04-77]

To us, all three arguments are weak and look more like fallacies than proofs.

The first argument fails because we do not accept that the essence of substance as understood in the mind can leave its subject—the mind—and exist independently of what supports it. The universal concept of substance exists in the mind, which is a real subject that does not depend on what inheres in it. So the concept is an accident: it is knowledge by which we know its object. Its object, however, is a substance,[^V04-78] the unrestricted essence, whether or not it is understood or sensed. Several confusions are at work here.

First, the argument mixes two ways of considering an essence, discussed in the section on essence. Sometimes we take an essence as universal and understood; at other times we take it without qualification, even without the qualification of being universal. This second sense is what is called the natural universal. Philosophers have disagreed about whether it exists through the very existence of its individuals, or exists only in the sense that its individuals exist. The argument goes wrong because the word “universal” is used for both senses.

Second, it confuses being the very essence of substance with having a substantial essence. In the universal concepts of substances in the mind, the first is true, not the second. The concept of the First Intellect is not itself the First Intellect; the concept “subsisting in itself” does not itself subsist in itself. This is the same mistake as using predication in the wrong sense and confusing primary predication with ordinary predication.[^V04-79]

Third, it confuses knowledge with what is known. They are not the same in every case or in every respect. Knowing something impossible is not impossible, and knowing something nonexistent is not nonexistent.

Fourth, it confuses something's existing mentally with its existing in the mind. Not everything that exists in the mind has mental existence. The power and courage found in a powerful or courageous person are features of that person. The soul is the mind, and a feature of the mind exists in it; that does not make the feature mental in the sense opposed to real existence. The power of the powerful person and the courage of the courageous person exist in the mind as realities. Likewise, the universal concept of substance is something real in the mind, an accident of the soul; at the same time, it is a “mental substance”—a description taken from substance and present in the mind—without the universal nature of substance applying to it as it does to one of its instances.[^V04-80]

So the sound view held by most philosophers is that universal substances—the concepts of substances in the mind—are not substances by ordinary predication. They would be substances only if we meant self-subsisting intelligible forms, as Plato and the Platonists do.

The second argument is also weak. We do not accept that universals, just as universals, are predicated of particulars. In that respect, their existence differs from the existence of particulars: universals exist intellectually, while particulars may exist materially. So universals cannot be predicated of particulars as being the very same thing, since predication requires the two sides to be united in existence.

Someone may object: “Isn't the universal predicated of the meaning ‘human,’ and isn't human predicated of Zayd and ʿAmr? So the universal human is predicated of its individuals. If Zayd is a substance in himself, then universal human must also be a substance. Therefore the universal of a substance is a substance.”

We answer that this argument is fallacious. “Human” in the two claims—“Zayd is human” and “human is understood”—is not one and the same thing in meaning and respect. The middle term has shifted. If this kind of argument were sound, a particular would be universal and a species would be a broader kind. Human is predicated of Zayd as what he is; the universal is predicated of the concept human as what it is. That would make Zayd universal, which is impossible. Likewise, the concept of a broader kind is predicated of animal, which is predicated of human; that would make human a broader kind, which is also impossible. Logic books count such arguments among fallacies. They arise from mixing judgments about the mind with judgments about the outside world and using the middle term in one sense in the minor premise and another in the major. It has not been repeated with one meaning and one point of view.

In brief, the claim that universals are predicated of substantial particulars is false if “universal” means the concept of universality—the logical universal and its subject considered as its subject, or the two taken together under that qualification. If “universal” means the essence that can serve as the subject of universality—the natural universal, essence considered in itself without another qualification—then that is not what we are discussing. The question here is whether the intelligible form of substance, precisely as an intelligible form, is a substance or an accident.

The third argument is also weak, because “essence,” the middle term, does not keep the same meaning on both sides. We agree that an individual such as Zayd is a substance because of his human essence. But we do not agree that the essence of him as understood in the mind is a substance. The conclusion that follows is only that the unrestricted essence, whether sensed or understood, is an essence of substance—that is, the essence of something whose existence is not in a subject. This means that some instances of it, namely the sensible things outside the mind, are substances. Since this claim is about an indefinite number of things, it amounts only to saying that the predicate belongs to some of them.

Now we have established the point. Do not confuse these meanings. Let us return to our question: is substance a broader kind of its external instances, or merely a feature that necessarily belongs to them? The author of *The Eastern Discussions* gives several arguments to deny that substance is a constituent of the kinds that fall under it. We will set out each argument as he presents it, then explain what is right about it.

The first argument is this: if substance were a broader kind of the kinds that fall under it, those kinds would have to differ through differences. Those differences would either be substances in their essence or not. If not, they would be accidents. But that is impossible, because an accident depends on substance, and what depends on something cannot constitute that same thing. So the differences must be substances.

If “substance” is predicated of the differences as a broader kind, each difference would need another difference to distinguish it, and the regress would continue. If substance is predicated of them as a feature that necessarily follows them, then the point is proved: it is not a broader kind. This argument has several flaws.

First, we can choose the view that the difference of substance is not itself a substance, without concluding that it is an accident. The division of possible things into substance and accident concerns things with real existence and a distinct essence. The simple meaning of a difference, like that of the most general kind, does not have a complete essence of its own.

Second, we can say that the difference of substance is a substance without making substance part of its own definition, or making it an external accident that is not itself a substance. The difference is a substance in reality, though not at the level of its essence considered in itself. That does not mean it is an accident at that level. Some aspects of a thing can be neither one of two opposites. Zayd, considered only as human in his essence, is neither existent[^V04-81] nor individual. That does not mean he is nonexistent or universal in that respect.

In short, when philosophers say that a difference is a feature of its broader kind, a broader kind is a feature of its difference, or existence is a feature of essences, they do not mean that the feature has an existence distinct from the existence of what it belongs to. Such relations arise only when we analyze two meanings that exist through one existence.

Given this, the difference of substance is a substance in reality, but the concept of substance is not part of its definition. It therefore needs no further difference just because it shares substantiality with the kind it completes and with other kinds. It can differ from the kind it completes through the relation of part to whole, and from other kinds through complete difference in essence and existence.

Third, our established view, as we have hinted, is that simple differences are the particular existences of essences. Existence is distinct in itself; through its degrees of strength and weakness, completeness and incompleteness, essences take shape and differ from one another, as you have learned. We will explain this further when we discuss substantial forms, God willing.

Fourth, the argument would rule out every broader kind. If it were sound, nothing could be a broader kind of what falls under it—not even animal. The difference that makes animal into a particular kind would either be animal by definition, in which case it would need another difference to distinguish it from other kinds of animal; or it would not be animal, in which case the essence of animal would be made up of something non-animal, and “not animal” would apply to animal because the difference is predicated of the species it completes.

The answer in all these cases is that a broader kind need not be a broader kind of everything that falls under it, whether a completed species or a completed difference. All broader kinds are features of the most basic simple differences. But this does not mean that a broader kind is an existent feature of a difference, as some limited interpretations suggest; that misunderstanding leads to such fallacies. Rather, its relation to a difference is like the relation of a species' essence to its individual instance. One should be surprised that this argument overlooks how substance, as a broader kind, is a feature of substantial differences, a point Avicenna makes explicitly in several places in *The Healing*, *The Salvation*, and other works.

Fifth, the argument that an accident depends on substance, and that what depends on a thing cannot constitute it, contains a confusion. Suppose a substance were made up of a substance and an accident that completes it. It would not follow that what completes the thing is the same thing that the completed thing depends on. The substance constituted by that accident—the compound—is different from the substance that constitutes it—the simple element. Our view does not accept this possibility, but the point here is to show that the argument against it is unsound.

The second argument the author believes and relies on is that the human soul is an immaterial substance that exists in itself. Philosophers agree, he says, that the soul cannot acquire knowledge of itself. If substance were essential to the soul, then it would always know its substantiality, and that knowledge would be immediate.

The right answer, I say, draws on principles we have already explained in our discussion of essence: the soul and other simple realities belong to the category of substance in one respect[^V04-82] and do not belong to it in another. The phrase “existent not in a subject” has one meaning when applied to substantial essences and another when applied to existences that do not depend on a subject. In the first meaning, it can be a broader kind for a group of meanings other than existence itself. In the second, it is only a feature drawn from existences and predicated of them and of those essences. We must also remember that what we know through direct awareness is the known thing's existence and identity, not its concept or universal essence, and that the soul and other simple forms exist in different degrees and ways.

So the human soul—and every animal or non-animal species-form—is simple and has its own particular existence. Its particular nature can be known only when its identity is present to someone capable of knowing it, as the objector himself accepts. It may be present and directly witnessed even while the knower is unaware of every universal meaning, let alone relational and negative concepts.

There is another answer, closer to the familiar philosophical method. The usual concept of substance names one shared generic reality among substances, which the intellect describes as “existent not in a subject.” The concept “existent” is a general feature of them all, while “not in a subject” is a qualified negation. A combination of a general feature and a negation cannot be the broader kind of external realities. The broader kind is the simple nature shared by substances. We can describe it only through external features, so the whole description is a definition by description rather than a strict definition. Because it names that shared nature and serves as a way to consider it, however, it is used in place of a definition.

If this is clear, we can say that, even if the human soul belongs to the category of substance as a species belongs to its broader kind, what constitutes the soul is not the description just given but the simple reality it names. So when the soul knows itself, it does not have to bring the concept of substance—that is, the description—before itself. It can still be uncertain whether the description applies to it and need not know that it is “not in a subject.” Yet anyone who turns inward finds the self present, even while every feature may be denied of it. Once that person learns that philosophers call this state “substantiality,” and that something is a substance when it has this feature in itself, apart from other things, then they will no longer doubt that the self is a substance.

Anyone who hesitates over whether the self is a substance or an accident may not understand what substance means, or may not have sorted out the concepts they already possess, or may be absorbed in things that distract the heart and veil the truth. Such veils and lapses affect most people: they are occupied with what keeps them from remembering themselves and what makes them what they are, what causes them to forget themselves and comes between a person and their heart, as God points out in many verses of the Clear Book.

We must also know that, at the beginning of its development, the human soul is not yet actually independent of the body. It has the capacity to subsist on its own, be independent of a subject, and separate from matter once it is perfected through understanding intelligible things and clearing away its obscurities. This state is not reached by everyone. The claim that every person can observe the self as if it were separate from bodies, positions, places, motions, and times can be challenged. Most people seem unable to make this observation or to focus on the self apart from its additions, because they are submerged in the dark sea of matter and the night of nature.

The author's third argument is that each substantial essence involves three things: its independence from a subject; the essence as the cause of this independence, once it exists; and the essence that has this causal feature.

If substance means the first, it is a negative meaning and cannot be a broader kind. If it means the second, it still is not a broader kind, because being a cause is a property of the essence that applies only after the essence is fully established. Until a thing's essence exists, it cannot actually be a cause of anything. Causality is not even a positive feature, let alone a generic meaning. If substance means the third—the essence that has this causal feature—we say that the thing bearing the feature in each substance might be that substance's particular essence: in a body, its being a body; in an intellect, its being an intellect. Different essences may share one necessary feature. If that is possible, then no shared element has been established, so how can substantiality be a broader kind? The argument needs further examination.

First, the same argument could be made against any supposed broader kind. No one doubts that animal is a real broader kind of animal species. Yet the meaning given for animal is only “a growing body that senses and moves by choice.” Animal itself is also said to be a broader kind, and its meaning is composed of corporeality and a kind of motion, such as feeding, growing, and reproducing. Body, too, is said to be a broader kind of bodies, with “substance capable of three dimensions” in its definition. None of these meanings can be a broader kind or an essential feature of bodily and animal species, since each is relational or negative. Still, anyone with sound judgment sees the point: we mean the real foundations of these features. The same applies to all differences. They are simple realities expressed through their consequences and effects.

Every difference is described through a relational or dispositional feature. “Sensing” and “rational,” for example, are differences that constitute animal and human. The first is explained as perception in general or particular perception; the second as grasping universals. Some take perception to be negative, others relational. Neither is a fully real essence outside the mind. How, then, could either constitute the reality of an existent thing? The answer in every case is that these expressions point to their real foundations and actual subjects, which cannot be expressed except through these consequences. I do not mean the foundations or subjects considered as foundations or subjects, which would make the constituents merely relational features, unable to belong to the category of substance. I mean the realities themselves, pointed to by these abstract descriptions, which are drawn from their very essence and therefore serve as names for them and stand in place of definitions.

Second, the alternatives the author gives are not exhaustive. The meaning that serves as a broader kind may be that a thing, when it exists, exists outside a subject, as we have explained. This meaning belongs to it whether it exists outside the mind or in the mind, and whether its existence is actual or only possible.

Third, the claim that different things can share one necessary feature is not sound in every respect. It has been shown that things cannot share one feature precisely as different things. Nor can different things share a necessary feature that belongs to each by its own essence, considered apart from everything else, unless there is some common constituent. Just as one particular effect cannot depend on two independent causes, and an effect of one kind cannot depend on two different natures unless they share an essential feature, so different things cannot each have an essential feature that follows from its own essence alone unless there is something essential common to them.

That is the case here.
