All substantial essences share one feature that necessarily belongs to them, even apart from other things: when they exist outside the mind, they do not depend on a subject.[^V04-83] This feature is either a common constituent or follows from some common constituent. In either case, substance is a broader kind, since sound reason does not allow one feature to belong to many things that differ in themselves without some common ground in their own natures.

Fourth, the most that follows from the author's argument is that it is possible for the concept of substance not to be a broader kind of the things beneath it, even if it is shared by them. That is all he shows when he says that there may be no common constituent. But how does that support his main argument that substance is an external feature rather than a constituent broader kind?

Fifth, the author's discussion here is contradictory. He accepts that the meaning of substance, in the third sense he gave, is shared by different essences in one meaning. Yet he says that this feature may belong to each essence because of its own particular nature, not because of something essential they share. From this he concludes that there may be no common element, then asks how substantiality can be a broader kind if it is at least a shared feature.

The fourth argument is this: an essence called a substance is either simple or compound. A simple essence cannot fall under a broader kind, or it would need something to distinguish it from another species and become compound, contrary to the assumption that it is simple. So a simple substance falls under substance without falling under a broader kind; substance is therefore not a broader kind.

As for a compound essence, it consists of simple parts. Each part either depends on a subject or does not. If it does, the constituents of substance are accidents, and what is constituted from accidents cannot be a substance. So the compound is not a substance, contrary to the assumption. If the parts do not depend on a subject, they are substances; but because they are simple, they have no broader kind. Substance, then, is not a broader kind of anything beneath it, which is the point to be proved.

This argument has several problems. First, it would rule out every simple broader kind, and indeed every broader kind, whether simple or compound. The same argument could be applied to animal: it is not a broader kind of animals, since the essence called animal must be either simple or compound. If simple, animal is not a broader kind of it, though it is predicated of it. If compound, its parts are either animals or not animals. Completing the argument in the same way would show that nothing in existence has a broader kind, which is plainly false. So the initial claim is false too. The argument is a fallacy.

Second, here is the answer. If a simple part combines with something else to form a species that falls under a broader kind, and the part itself does not fall under that broader kind, there are two possibilities. Either the meaning of the broader kind does not apply to it, or the meaning does apply but is not essential to it, so it does not fall under the broader kind as a species falls under its broader kind. The first possibility also has two cases: the broader kind does not apply because the part is identical with the meaning of the broader kind—nothing is an individual of itself—or because it is not identical with that meaning. So there are three ways for a thing not to fall under a broader kind. Only one of these three can prevent a compound made from that thing and something else from being a species of the broader kind.[^V04-84]

For example, human is an essence under the broader kind animal and is made from two parts, animal and rational. Neither part falls under animal as something under an essential broader kind. Animal taken absolutely as a broader kind does not fall under itself, because there is no difference between them. Animal taken apart from the qualifications that specify its differences either does not exist outside the mind, so it cannot constitute an existing species, or is matter for the compound made from it and the difference. It is therefore not a broader kind predicated of the compound species, as we explained when distinguishing two ways of abstracting.

As for “rational” taken without qualification, animal is only an external feature of it. If “rational” is taken in abstraction, animal is not predicated of it—either because, on one account, the abstracted form does not exist, or because it is a form rather than a difference, with animal as matter rather than a predicate.

You might say that animal taken in abstraction falls under animal taken absolutely as a broader kind, just as a species falls under its broader kind: it is the same broader kind, with the added condition of being abstracted, and so is a species of it. I answer that animal taken this way may be a species, but only in an abstract, intellectual sense. It does not exist outside the mind in that respect. If it did, it would be composed of broader kind and difference, though it was assumed to be simple. That is impossible. Besides, if this kind of inclusion were enough for a thing to fall under a broader kind in the outside world, it would settle the very case in dispute: a simple substance, or one of the two parts of a substantial species, could be a substance taken without any added condition or qualification. It would then be possible for the simple to be an existing substance.

#### V. Why Some Substances Are More Basic Than Others

Philosophers commonly divide substances into primary substances, such as individual things; secondary substances, such as species; and third-level substances, such as broader kinds. They say that individuals are more truly substances than species, and species more truly substances than broader kinds. They do not mean that the very meaning that makes substance a broader kind comes in degrees. They have already established that an essential feature cannot be stronger or weaker in this way. Rather, they mean that individuals are more substantial in one of three respects: the existence included in the definition of substance, independence from a subject when something actually exists, or rank and excellence. In these respects, individuals are more truly substances than species: they are more independent of a subject, exist before universal species, and are the first things called substances.

In terms of dependence, the universal depends on the individual: without individuals, a universal would have no existence. The reverse is not true. If an individual needed a universal in order to exist, it would also need another individual alongside it for that universal to apply to both. But this is not so, as we have already shown. In terms of existing without a subject, individuals already have this kind of existence, while universals do not. Universality is a relation: a universal needs something to which it can apply. In terms of excellence, nature aims, through its generative activity, to bring a species into existence as an individual.

When philosophers say that sensible substances are more truly substances than intelligible ones, by “intelligible” they mean only the mental forms of sensible things and their universal essences. They do not mean separate intelligences, or individual beings free from matter. These are more truly substances and exist more fully than all the rest. They are the earliest and strongest substances because they actively cause other individuals and species to exist.

The relation between species and broader kinds is like that between individuals and species. A species is closer to being fully realized, more complete in existence, and a fuller answer to the question “What is this?” than a broader kind. Logical defining features—that is, terms such as “rational”—are substances because they are predicated of substances by saying what they are. Simple defining features are also substances because they make up substantial species; what constitutes a substance is itself a substance. This is what the philosophers say.

There is an obvious looseness in this account, as we can see by recalling what we said earlier about simple defining features and substantiality. Also, if anything predicated of a substance were itself a substance, then “white” would be a substance, although it is an accident.

The Sheikh of the Illuminationists raises several objections to the philosophers who follow the First Teacher, Aristotle. First, did they not say that substance is a broader kind, and that a broader kind cannot apply to its members in degrees? If they say the priority is not in substantiality itself but in some other respect, then they should not say that individual substances are more substantial than species, or species more substantial than broader kinds. They should say instead that some things exist more fully than others, or are more independent of a subject.

Second, it is not right to say that individuals have more existence in the outside world than species and broader kinds. Species and broader kinds are universals, and a universal, just as universal, does not exist outside the mind. So there is no external existence in which they could be more or less present. The only exception would be if “universal” meant the nature considered in itself, whether it exists in the outside world or in the mind. But then an individual would not be more substantial than its species either: the individual has added features, but Zayd is a substance because he is human, not because he is dark or fair.

Third, anyone who allows degrees of substantiality should also say that a simple substance is more substantial than a compound one, such as a body, which is made of two substances: matter and form. The whole body gets its identity from its parts, so without their substantiality the whole would not be a substance. Just as a human being gets bodily existence through being an animal, a body gets its substantiality through its two parts. And just as a human being would not be a body if animality were not bodily, the whole body would not be a substance if its parts were not substances.

Moreover, if humanity belongs to a person through animality, and substantiality belongs to an animal through bodily form and soul, then a body would be more substantial than the whole made up of its parts.

Fourth, for anyone who says that existence has no reality outside the mind, existence cannot be a cause or an effect. In that view, substantial causes and effects cannot be prior to one another in existence, since existence is only a concept and has no place in the outside world. Their priority could only be in essence and reality.

Fifth, a body is made of matter and form, and we have shown that each is made to exist separately. Together they make a body. So the body is not substantial in its own right: it is only the two substances and the relation of their coming together. That relation is a concept, an accident. It cannot create a third substantiality beyond the substantiality of its two parts. The whole adds only their joining, which is an accident. So substantiality does not belong independently to the whole, and one thing cannot be both substance and accident.

That is a summary of his objections to the philosophers. With God’s help, I will answer them all.

The first objection is answered by distinguishing between the meaning of substance and the degree to which things are prior or superior in existence. The philosophers did not mean that substantiality itself is what differs in degree. They meant that some individuals are more fully substantial than others, though this is not because they are more substance-like in the very sense of being substances.

Avicenna explains the same distinction when discussing quantity in *The Healing*. After saying that quantity does not increase or decrease in its own nature, he adds: “I do not mean that one quantity cannot be greater or smaller than another. I mean that one quantity is not more fully a quantity than another quantity of the same nature. A line is not more fully a line—that is, something with one dimension—than another line, though it may be longer in comparison with it.” Greater length does not make one line more of a line. He develops this point through further examples. So when philosophers say that some substances have a greater claim to substantiality than others, they do not mean that the very definition of substance comes in degrees.

This is like saying that Zayd the father is earlier in being human than Amr the son. That does not contradict the claim that humanity itself does not come in degrees. It would be contradictory to say that Zayd is earlier than Amr specifically in being human. So some substances are prior and more basic than others because their substantial existence comes first, not because substantiality itself is prior in its own nature. People often miss this distinction and confuse the point at issue with something else. This is the difference between the two sides of the debate.[^V04-85]

As for the second objection: although a universal does not exist outside the mind as a universal, it does exist in the mind as an individual act of understanding, with features belonging to the soul. This is a different kind of existence from the existence of the individuals whose essence is understood. For example, the nature of humanity exists outside the mind through the existence of individual human beings, and there it has its own effects. When this nature exists in the mind and is understood as universal, however, it exists as a mental quality—knowledge.

In the mind it belongs to the category of quality, not substance, even though its meaning is still humanity. It is not an instance of humanity in the way that an actual human being is.

The substance outside the mind is plainly more truly a substance than the substance in the mind. Substance, in its usual predicative sense, does not apply to a universal in the mind; the mind only holds the concept of substance. This offers another way to understand the philosophers’ claim that individual substances are more substantial than species and broader kinds.

To answer the third objection, recall that “substance,” defined as “what exists without being in a subject,” can be considered in two ways: in terms of essence, and in terms of existence that is independent of a subject. These philosophers hold that existence is real in itself, as we have already explained.

When priority and posteriority are said of essences, the basis for that order lies outside the essences themselves. For example, existence is the basis of causal priority and the priority of an effect; time is the basis of temporal priority; place and other factors are the basis of rank; and honor or lowliness may also determine priority. When priority and posteriority are said of existence, however, existence itself may be what makes one thing prior to another.

With this distinction in place, the existence of a compound substance, considered as a compound, is certainly different from the existence of its simple parts. Its substantiality is numerically different from theirs and depends on them. But the philosophers in Aristotle’s school and their followers do not say that the substantiality of a compound body, in the sense of its being a substance, is different from the substantiality of its parts, each considered as a substance. They say only that the existence of the compound differs from the existence of its parts in number. This is clear in a natural species made up of parts.

There is no third substance, however, when there is no natural unity, as when a stone is placed next to a person. There are only the two substances. In short, substantiality does not pass from cause to effect. It is one meaning, with no difference or degrees in the concept itself. Existence is different. In its own reality, it is earlier or later, the cause of something or what is caused. Its many degrees do not differ merely as instances of a single general kind differ. Other meanings and essences become distinct through existence, but the different ways of existing differ through existence itself. Those firmly rooted in knowledge recognize this distinction. The general idea of “existence” that we form in the mind is not existence itself, but only an idea drawn from it.

So the substantiality of a cause or part is prior to that of an effect or compound in this respect: it exists earlier and more fully, not because its substantiality as substantiality is greater. Existence is the basis of priority and excellence. A body is prior in existence to an animal, but not in substantiality; an animal exists before humanity, but not because it is more substantial. Humanity has bodily existence through animality, just as animality has substantial existence through bodily form—not in the sense that it is more bodily or more substantial. Likewise, without the parts existing as substances, the whole would not be an existing substance. But this does not mean that the whole depends on their substantiality as such. The substantiality of the whole and of its parts is the same in meaning, and neither receives substantiality from the other. So the conclusion drawn from the third objection does not follow.

To answer the fourth objection: the philosophers who deny causal relations, effects, and priority among substantial essences—and essences in general—are Aristotle’s followers. They hold that existence has reality outside the mind. Those who say that existence has no reality or form outside the mind must, by contrast, hold that causal relations and priority belong to essences. If cause and effect are both substances, such as intellect and soul, or matter and form, they must, on that view, be related as one substantial essence causing another.

So the objection does not apply consistently to either view. It applies only to someone who combines parts of two incompatible views: agreeing with Aristotle’s followers that essential features do not differ by degrees of priority, while agreeing with the Stoics that existence is only a mental concept and has no effect in reality. That combination leads to a contradiction.

This difficulty arose earlier in this journey too, when certain authorities combined positions that the leading philosophers had kept apart, contradicting their shared conclusions and the proofs on which reason relies.

To answer the fifth objection: the substantiality of a body is not different in meaning from the substantiality of the whole made from its parts, but it is numerically different from the substantiality of each part. This follows the view, which the author shares with most philosophers, that matter and form are brought into existence separately. If they were brought into existence together as one, no one could say that the body’s substantiality is numerically different from the substantiality of each part—just as a simple species is not numerically distinct from its broader kind and defining feature.

Still, the body is certainly an existing substance. Its substantial existence, on the view we have chosen, is not identical with the existence of either part, and the substantiality of the whole is not numerically the same as the substantiality of either part. The whole has a third substantiality, different in number from the substantiality of matter and form, though not different from them in the meaning of substantiality or in the fact that the whole is a substance. The substantiality of the whole is that of the whole, not something separate from the whole; nor is it different from the substantiality of each part in its meaning.

#### VI. The Properties of Substance

One property of substance is that it has no contrary. This is true if by contraries we mean things that take turns in the same subject and are as far apart as possible. If by “subject” we mean any underlying thing, including both the subject of accidents and the matter of substances, then substances do have contraries: the form of fire is contrary to the form of water. Some have reported that Avicenna considered this dispute merely verbal. As we will see, it is not.

Some kinds of quantity share this property with substance. Numbers have no contraries: three and four do not take turns in one subject, and for every number there is another number greater than it. So no two numbers are as far apart as possible—unless one holds that contraries do not need to be maximally opposed.

You already know that logicians and metaphysicians use two different definitions of contrariety. They also say that another property follows from substance’s lack of a contrary: substance cannot grow stronger or weaker. They argue that increase or decrease in intensity occurs only in things that have contraries. It means moving toward greater or lesser perfection, and the stages of such a movement are not all present in one place at once. The subject must remain the same through the whole movement. If the subject is the weaker state, it disappears as the thing grows stronger; if it is the stronger state, it disappears as the thing grows weaker. In either case, no subject remains, so there can be no motion in substance.

We will see how weak this argument is when we discuss the world’s coming into being through changes in natures and substantial forms.

There is another objection. It is not right, someone might say, to count the inability to grow stronger or weaker as a property of substance, because quantity also cannot do so. Careful thinkers do not distinguish intensifying or diminishing from increasing or decreasing in meaning. A movement toward greater perfection means the same thing whether it occurs in quality, where we call it intensification, or in quantity, where we call it increase. The same is true of decreasing. Also, a particular quantity disappears when it increases or decreases, if it is continuous. If it is discrete, what is added is not the same as what is lost. So quantity does not move in its quantity. By the same reasoning, quality does not move in its quality either. Intensification does not mean that one quality remains and another like it is added; a strong quality, like a weak one, is a simple accident, not a sum of weaker qualities. It is matter that changes through different magnitudes, just as a subject changes through different qualities.

But the philosophers do not mean that substance does not move. They mean that motion does not occur in substance. So this is not parallel to saying that quantity and quality do not move. The argument would apply only if it showed that motion does not occur in quantity or quality, and it does not show that. People often confuse these two claims when reading the philosophers. Even the author of *The Wisdom of Illumination* changed the wording when listing the properties of substance in *The Discussions*. He wrote: “One of the special properties of substance is that it does not grow stronger or weaker. This is because it cannot have contraries, and intensification and diminution occur only between contraries. But this is not unique to substance, since quantity too cannot grow stronger or weaker, as we will explain.”

His later discussion shows only that quantity does not itself move; it does not show that motion does not occur in quantity. Those are different claims. In fact, what moves in every motion is, in itself, material substance. The property of the category of substance is not that nothing moves, but that substance does not come about gradually through a process of motion. That is what the philosophers mean when they say that substance does not intensify or move: motion does not occur in it.

### 1. A Closer Look

Those who have spiritual insight, supported by strong proof and clear knowledge, take a different view: just as change can occur in quality, it can occur in substance. In the material world, the individual unity of what undergoes substantial motion is preserved by two things: an intellectual, luminous, active unity belonging to the intellect that causes it, and an open-ended, receptive unity belonging to matter, which remains through its changes and transformations.

Motion in substance is therefore possible, either toward greater completeness or toward decline. The first follows nature and divine care; the second follows force imposed from outside or a limitation in what can receive its full potential. The first is seen in the development of human substance, from its beginnings as an embryo—or even as an unremembered thing—until it becomes an intellect in actuality and goes beyond that. A clear mind can see that the difference between an undeveloped child and a fully mature adult is not just a matter of added accidents, such as changes in temperament, size, or position. It is almost self-evident that this cannot be the whole difference.

The second kind is seen when the elements change into one another. When intense heat gradually weakens the nature of water, the water approaches the nature of air and becomes air. This does not mean that water remains water while somehow turning into air. Nor does water disappear at one instant and air appear at the next, right beside it; that would require two successive instants, which is impossible. If air appeared somewhere else, matter would have to exist for a moment without any form, which is also impossible. Rather, the water’s substantial nature gradually diminishes until it becomes air.

The matter that underlies this substantial motion is prime matter, though not by itself, since it cannot exist without some form. It is matter together with some form, not one particular form, that remains as the subject of change. The motion occurs through the different substantial forms. This is like change in quantity: prime matter underlies measurable size, but cannot exist without some size. Matter together with some size remains the subject as one particular magnitude gives way to another. Its unity is preserved through the changing magnitudes, just as it is through changing forms.

You know that measurable size cannot be separated from matter, either in reality or even in thought. So the subject of substantial motion remains the same, with all that is needed for its existence, even as different substantial forms come and go. There is no problem here, despite what Avicenna and his followers thought.

Also, if there were no shared limit between water and air—no point at which the hottest kind of water meets the coolest kind of air—then matter would have to be without any form for an instant. As we have said, that is impossible both by common agreement and by proof. The only way to avoid this problem is to allow motion in substantial forms: intensification and weakening.

### 2. A Note

One thing that points to and supports this view is the development of semen in the womb. It gradually becomes complete enough to perform the functions of plant life, then develops until it performs the functions of animal life, and then those of a human being. If these perfections appeared all at once, with no gradual intensification or development, the change would have to happen through an abrupt destruction of one form and an instantaneous arrival of another. This would mean that agents with entirely different natures took turns acting on the same matter. That does not happen in natural processes. One agent can hand over the material of its work to another only in deliberate crafts, where the work is done by intention and planning. In nature, the material of each process is bound up with the agent that acts on it.

The nature present in the semen clearly has an inherent drive to develop and reach perfection. That drive does not destroy itself or undo its own form. So what would cause the first form to perish and the second to appear? Nature seeks only to preserve its form and move toward its end and perfection, with help from higher powers.

Just as the natural form perfects bodily matter, plant life is the perfection and end toward which the natural form develops. Animal life, in turn, is the perfection toward which plant life develops, and so on, up to the acquired intellect and beyond. At every stage, the unity of what undergoes change is preserved by a shared form: one kind of form succeeds another in an unbroken sequence. Its continuity is held together by one active substance that gives each new perfection as the stages of development advance. Continuity itself is also a kind of individual unity, even though it unfolds gradually and never settles into one fixed state.

This view does depart from the familiar account. But you know that a thoughtful person does not abandon what is familiar or accepted by most people unless there is no way around it.

Anyone who looks at the stages of human life, the wonders of its development, and its many worlds—how it rises at times toward the heaven of intellect and sinks at others toward the earth of nature—will see that it grows stronger and weaker in its very substance. I do not mind disagreeing with the majority or going beyond the familiar view. The traveler takes joy and pride in doing so. Most people stay where they were born, in the first home of their existence; only a few leave and set out on a journey.

What should remove your unease and disbelief at the idea that this means essences and essential features change is this: if you understand what we have already established—that existence is what makes things real, and essences follow the different ways things exist—you will see that some forms of existence are stronger and some weaker, just as some are prior to others in themselves. Intensification, whether in a quality or in a substantial form, is a movement in a thing’s existence, not in its essence.

The philosophers in Aristotle’s school say that stronger and weaker stages are different kinds, but that during intensification they do not exist as a series of fully distinct states. Otherwise, motion would be made up of infinitely many instantaneous states, which is impossible. You can then see that essences remain what they are at every level of existence; they do not turn into something else. Each meaning and essence is itself, and each particular mode of existence is itself. But existence has many forms and stages within its own reality. These do not come about through a separate act of creation or the influence of an external agent. This is very subtle and not the place to explain it. Let us return to the point we left.

Another property of substance is that it can be directly pointed out. A pointing may be sensory or intellectual: it directs attention to a thing that is not shared with anything else. If we try to point to accidents, sensory pointing reaches them only through things that depend on matter and bodies. So pointing to an accident depends on pointing to the subject in which it exists. Intellectual pointing reaches particular accidents only through knowledge of their causes, which gives us only general knowledge. That is not direct pointing, since direct pointing is to something that cannot be shared, as we have said.

From this we can see that universal substances cannot be directly pointed out either, just like universal accidents. So it is a mistake to say that this property belongs to every substance. Nor, according to the common view that a bodily individual substance cannot be understood in itself, can it be pointed out intellectually. It can be understood only through knowledge of its causes.

Some say an accident can be pointed out intellectually because we understand its nature without tying it to a particular subject. On this view, substance and accident do not differ in their ability to be pointed out: universals of both can be understood intellectually, while individuals of both can be pointed to by the senses. But anyone who understands pointing as a kind of presence—rather than simply knowing something through a general idea—will not confuse the two. An accident’s essence depends on its subject for its existence; substance does not need a subject.

The apparent dependence of material forms on a subject is not what it seems. Their need for matter is not what makes them exist; it is tied to the conditions that make each one an individual. As we will explain later, the essence of any substance, considered in its mental existence, can clearly be pointed to intellectually because it is mentally distinct and determinate.[^V04-86] It cannot be pointed to as an externally existing universal, because there it is indeterminate. An accident’s essence, by contrast, always follows something else in its existence, so it can be pointed to only indirectly.

Can a purely separate substance be singled out by intellectual pointing, both as an individual and in itself? The philosophers in Aristotle’s school deny this, except in the case of a separate being’s knowledge of itself and its essential attributes. The Stoics affirm it. The Stoics are right, as we will explain later in the discussion of God’s knowledge.

Another property of substance is that it exists in itself, as opposed to existing in relation to something else. This applies only to substances that are not in a subject. According to the philosophers in Aristotle’s school, material forms and universal substances exist for something else, because they are not self-subsisting. We say “according to the Aristotle’s followers” because earlier philosophers held that natural universals exist on their own in a realm of separate beings.

One might object that material forms, considered in their nature, do not need matter. They need it only in order to be individuals. Matter does not depend on any one particular form, since it remains when that form passes away. So what is primary and essential to substance does not exist for another; whatever exists for another is an accident. We have also shown that universals and mental forms belong not to the category of substance but to the category of quality, even though their definitions and meanings are substantial and substance is predicated of them—not in the ordinary sense of predication.

We answer the first point as follows. A substantial form may not need matter in its essence, but its essence is united with its individual determination in one mode of existence. So its individual existence is for something else. We answer the second point in the same way. The essence of humanity falls under substance in itself, but it can also be said to exist by depending on a mental subject that holds it. In that respect, when it exists in the mind, it exists for something else. So this property does not belong to every individual instance of substance, or to everything of which substantiality can be predicated.

Another property of substance is that one individual substance can receive opposing qualities, such as a body’s being black and white, or a soul’s having hope and fear.

You might object that one belief can be true or false. Belief is a kind of knowledge in the soul, and acquired knowledge is an accident. So quality shares this property with substance.

I answer that the point concerns opposite qualities changing in themselves, not a change in their relation to something outside them. Truth and falsehood are relational. If a true belief becomes false, what has changed is the outside thing to which it relates. The mental image itself remains as it was; what changes is its relation to reality.

This property, too, does not belong to every substance. Separate substances do not change, and the intellectual world is protected from change and renewal, like a tablet on which nothing is erased or replaced. Only what lies below that world can change, such as the celestial world of souls. There, things can be erased or affirmed, as the Qur'an says: “God erases what He wills and confirms what He wills; and with Him is the Mother of the Book.”

Do not think that universal substances—the so-called second and third substances, or species and broader kinds—change because they are predicated sometimes of white things and sometimes of black things. They apply to different individuals; they do not themselves change. Species and broader kinds do not change in what they are as species and broader kinds, just as separate beings do not change.

Nor should you think that this property belongs to accidents because “color in general” can be thought of as black or white and can change from one to the other. When white loses its defining feature, it loses its very nature as a color, since the color and its defining feature are brought about together. When one color disappears, it disappears altogether and another color comes to be. A color does not lose its defining feature while retaining some general color nature. In the mind, the general idea of color can be applied equally to all colors.

People say that a broader kind is ready to receive defining features, but this is true of the broader kind of compound kinds, not of broader kind as broader kind. It is true of it insofar as it is taken as matter. We have already explained the difference between these two ways of considering it. If someone says that the broader kind of simple kinds can receive defining features, that only means the mind can strip away a general nature, such as color, and apply it to either of two defining features, such as what draws the eye together or spreads it apart. This is only something the mind can do.

The property we are discussing is that one individual thing in reality can receive two opposing qualities. If the universal itself, as universal, could receive both, every color would be black and white. And if the universal nature of color could become black, it would be something made black, not black itself; the same would be true if it became white. There would then be no actual black or white in existence. Such errors often come from confusing numerical unity with unity of meaning.

#### VII. Why One Thing Cannot Be Both Substance and Accident

The truth of this claim is obvious as soon as we consider what “substance” and “accident” mean. One is the negation of the other: an accident exists in a subject, while a substance exists without being in a subject. A thing and its negation cannot both apply to the same thing. By “substance” we mean something whose existence does not depend on a subject; by “accident” we mean something whose existence depends on some subject. So one thing cannot fall under both meanings.

Some ancient thinkers allowed this, though Avicenna thought it unlikely. They offered several arguments. First, the defining features of substances are substances, yet philosophers often call defining features “qualities,” and qualities are accidents. So these defining features would be both substances and accidents.

Avicenna answers that “quality” is used in two different senses here: it is one thing when said of defining features, and another when said of the category of accidents. We can also explain why the defining features of substances are not accidents. As we said earlier, a defining feature does not exist separately from its broader kind except in the mind, when we analyze them. If it were an accident of the broader kind, its existence would have to be separate from the existence of its subject, as an accident’s existence is separate from that of its subject in reality. But the defining feature and broader kind exist as one, since the defining feature is predicated of the broader kind by saying “it is this.” In the mind, where we consider each apart from the other, the defining feature relates to the broader kind as form to matter, not as accident to subject. The defining feature is an intellectual form, and the broader kind is intellectual matter that needs the form in order to become definite and exist. The same is true of the broader kinds and defining features of compounds, though these also differ somewhat in their existence outside the mind: they are matter and form there, not accident and subject.

The second argument is that form exists in an underlying bearer without being a part of it, so form must be an accident. Yet form is a part of the substance made from it and matter, and a part of a substance is a substance. So the same thing would be both substance and accident.

The answer is that form does not exist in its bearer in the same way that an accident exists in a subject. Nor does it exist in a compound in that same way. Since it has no other kind of existence, it is not an accident. An accident depends on a subject for its existence; form does not depend on anything in that way. So form is substance in itself.

The third argument is that heat is part of what is hot, and what is hot is a substance. Heat is therefore part of a substance, and a part of a substance is a substance. Yet heat is an accident in relation to the body that receives it. So heat would be both substance and accident.

The answer depends on what is meant by heat. If it means the unseen fiery nature, then its existence in a fiery body—its matter—is like the existence of form in matter, as we have already explained. If it means the sensible quality, heat is not part of what is hot, whether in fire or anywhere else, except by assumption and in thought.

The fourth argument[^V04-87] is this: an accident in a compound is a part of it, so it is not an accident in that compound. Anything that is not an accident in a thing must be a substance in it. Yet, in relation to what receives it, that same thing is an accident. So one thing would be both substance and accident.

The answer is that this argument confuses “substance” with “substantial,” and “accident” with “incidental.” Substance and accident are absolute realities; substantial and incidental describe relations. Something can be an accident in itself and incidental to one thing, while essential to another. Or it can be a substance in itself and essential to one thing, while incidental to another.

For example, a human being is a substance in itself and essential to humanity, but incidental to “laughing.” Whiteness is an accident in itself and incidental to the body that receives it, but essential to the compound of body and whiteness. Animal is a substance in itself and essential to the human being, but incidental to “walking.”

So a thing’s being a part of a compound does not make it a substance in itself. It is a substance only if its essence does not depend on a subject. If its essence depends on a subject, it is an accident, whether or not it is part of something. When philosophers say that a part of a substantial compound is itself a substance,[^V04-88] they mean a natural compound with one nature. A part of a natural compound exists in it as matter or form, not as an accident in a subject. Avicenna discusses these points at length in the *Categories* of *The Healing*. What we have said is enough for a seeker whose aim is simply to reach what is needed to understand the subject, clear away obstacles, and resist those who mislead—not to master every argument, school, and opinion.

### 1. A Difficulty and Its Solution

It is surprising that the author of *The Eastern Discussions*, after repeating these ideas in his books and commentaries on the philosophers, later says that they could defend their view this way: anything that exists in something else can be considered as being in a subject or as being in a compound. The second way does not make it an accident, since it is a part, and an accident cannot be a part. The first way—being in a subject—must mean either that the subject is constituted by what exists in it or that it is not.

The first possibility is false for two reasons. First, what exists in the subject depends on the subject. If the subject also depended on it, each would depend on the other, creating a circle. Second, the matter of the elements is shared by their different forms. If the existence of any one elemental form helped constitute the matter, then when that form disappeared, the matter would disappear too. The matter would no longer be shared, which is impossible. This would make what exists in the subject both substance and accident. That is the difficulty raised by those who allow one thing to be both.

The answer is to recall what we have already said. First, this argument confuses substance with substantiality—that is, being essential—and accident with being incidental, or outside the essence. It argues that anything in a subject is a substance because it is a part of the whole. But a part of the whole is not incidental to that whole; that does not mean it is not an accident in itself. Being a part does not make something a substance in itself, only essential to something else. So a thing can be an accident in itself and substantial to something else without contradiction.

Second, the argument again confuses a thing’s state in itself with its relation to something else. It reasons from the denial of one relation to the affirmation of its opposite. But it is wrong to infer that something is a substance because it is not incidental. The opposite of “incidental” is “essential,” not “substance.” A thing can be essential to something without being a substance in itself. Color, for example, is essential to blackness but is not a substance.

Third, the circle it describes is not always impossible. The form that constitutes matter and the matter constituted by it depend on one another in different respects, as we will explain in the discussion of how matter and form are connected. Not every relation called a circle—even if the same word is used—has the same kind of dependence on both sides. Only a circle in which both sides depend on each other in the same respect is impossible. That is what the proof rules out, not every relation that can be called circular.

Fourth, as we will explain, matter depends for its existence as a subject on some form or other, but it does not depend on any accident in this way. This is the basis for distinguishing form from accident. An accident is a descriptive feature that cannot exist unless its subject supports it. A substantial form needs a subject only because of the conditions that make it an individual and the requirements of its existence in the outside world.

Let this be the end of what we set out to say about substance and accident. Here ends the first volume of the Second Journey; the second volume follows, God willing.

## Notes

[^V04-1]: Delaying the discussion of substances until after accidents does not mean that knowledge of substances belongs in divine science. It may mean only that this knowledge belongs close to, and in connection with, divine science.

[^V04-2]: “Knowledge of substances” means knowing substances as what accidents depend on. This belongs more naturally in the study of divine realities than in a first philosophy that studies general concepts. The relation of accidents to substances is like the relation of concepts to essences; knowledge of accidents therefore comes before knowledge of substances, just as general philosophy comes before divine science.

[^V04-3]: The commentator says this was Ibn Sahlān al-Sāwijī, though the teacher he asked could not confirm the name.

[^V04-4]: An objection: even if the argument works, it would require the thing related to also be one of the seven relational categories. But that is not so. Place, for example, is not itself a category in the way the argument needs, nor is time. The same applies to the other cases. One might reply that a category follows, in some sense, the broader kind of what it relates to; still, there seems to be no single essential feature shared by all these relations.

[^V04-5]: This does not show that unity and a point are outside quantity in every sense. At most, a point is outside continuous quantity and unity outside discrete quantity. That is enough to undermine the claim that either is a starting point for all quantity. No one says that a point, as discrete quantity, is the beginning of the continuous, or that unity, as continuous quantity, is the beginning of the discrete. This also shows why the Sheikh's answer to the objection is not sound.

[^V04-6]: In short, heaviness and lightness are divisible only incidentally, while quantity itself is divisible by nature. The argument fails because it shifts its key claim: if it says heaviness is divisible only incidentally, it has not shown that heaviness is quantity by nature. If it says heaviness is divisible by nature, that claim is false.

[^V04-7]: “Arrangement” here means that the parts are distinct in existence but coexist. Time has no position because its parts do not exist together. Number has no position because its parts are not continuous.

[^V04-8]: The odd is simply what is not even. That is why this description can also apply to the Necessary Being. The two oppose each other as affirmation and negation.

[^V04-9]: The parts of an element can be continuous with other parts of the same kind. A celestial sphere, by contrast, can be continuous with another body only through their shared broader kind.

[^V04-10]: Even a little reflection shows the problem with the replies to the second and third ways of matching. It is astonishing that the author, for all his standing among scholars, should have said this. Think carefully, and the point will become clear. — Fath Ali Khoyi.

[^V04-11]: This refers to the proof by matching. It says that two infinite distances must differ by greater and smaller amounts if one contains the other. But if a greater and a smaller infinite distance can be compared this way, then both must be finite. The same reasoning applies to the example used against the proof.

[^V04-12]: The same argument that says an infinite body acts in no time by acting on a large part also says this if we consider a smaller part, and indeed every part, when the thing acted on is finite. The action in no time on the large part would then be faster than the action in no time on the smaller part. So the contradiction is no clearer in the second case.

[^V04-13]: The thought is that if the parts of the cause act on the parts of the thing affected in no time, then the whole cause would act on the whole thing in no time too—even though we assumed that this happens over time.

[^V04-14]: If the surface were a part of the body, it would have to be either a defining feature or a form, not a material part or a general kind. A material part or general kind is substance taken either apart from other features or without excluding them. A defining feature or form is what completes a thing and makes it actual. But a surface, considered as the body's end, does not complete the body. It is the body's limit, cut, or boundary, though it exists in the body and not in something else. So it is an accident of a finite natural body, but not of a geometrical body, whose three dimensions are already complete.

[^V04-15]: There are two objections. First, an accident cannot exist in another accident. Second, a geometrical surface is not something outside the three dimensions of geometrical body: we obtain it by considering two dimensions without the third. Once we set the third aside, what separate subject is left for the surface to exist in? This is how our teacher explained it, though the first objection needs further thought. — Fath Ali Khoyi.

[^V04-16]: A point is the end of a line that has only one dimension, so the point itself has no dimension. In the same way, a line is the end of a surface with two dimensions and therefore has one; a surface is the end of a body with three dimensions and therefore has two. — Avicenna.

[^V04-17]: The moving body seeks place through its movement. Place must therefore exist while it moves; otherwise, it would be seeking something that does not exist.

[^V04-18]: Place as a dimensioned substance has position. Immaterial substances such as intellect and soul do not. The argument proves only that place is not an immaterial substance; it does not rule out every kind of substance.

[^V04-19]: The comparison is not exact. “Known” is not formed from “knowledge” in the same way, because “knowledge” names an act, while “known” names what receives that act.

[^V04-20]: If this dimension were material, it would be a form; but, as we have seen, a form cannot be place. Also, if the dimension were material, the dimension and matter together would form a body, which would itself need a place. Its place could not be a part of the first body's dimension, since it would move with it. It would therefore have to be part of another body, which would also need a place that was not the dimension belonging to the first body; otherwise, material substances would overlap. That place would be another dimension belonging to a third body, and so on without end. This would mean an infinite regress of dimensions, which earlier arguments have ruled out. Think carefully. — Ismail.

[^V04-21]: This completes both examples and qualifies both claims. It is what makes the first example a genuine objection. In the second example, either this point or the fact that both surfaces are concave completes the objection. — Fath.

[^V04-22]: If “infinite” means “not finite” in a sense that applies only to things that could be finite, we can say that these dimensions are neither finite nor infinite. A subject that cannot have either feature can be free of both, just as the First Intellect is neither blind nor sighted. And even if infinity in this sense is ruled out, finitude has not thereby been proved. If “infinite” simply means “not finite,” we can say that the dimensions are not finite in this broad sense. Denying finitude does not prove that they are infinite in the other sense, so the earlier proofs would not refute them.

    Now suppose they are finite and have shape. The claim that the shape belongs to them by their own nature does not mean that the part must have the same shape as the whole. That would follow only if they had parts and wholes, but parts and wholes result from division, which requires matter—whether the division is real or imagined. A thing without matter cannot be divided. We may instead say that an agent gives them shape without matter. The claim that this would make them ready for division, joining, and stretching is not established; it would follow only if shape could arise in those three ways and no others. A thing might receive its shape from its original nature, without any division, joining, or stretching. If it naturally excludes other shapes, there is no arbitrary choice of one shape over another. — ل ره

[^V04-23]: This applies to the second and third movements only. If it applied to all of them, the movement through a vacuum would take no time, and the problem would remain. The argument first assumes that movement has a definite time of its own, since it assigns a duration to movement through a vacuum. It then denies that movement has a duration of its own, making its time depend on the resistance in the path. The contradiction comes from combining these two claims. — The Teacher.

[^V04-24]: Expansion means that a vacuum forms between the body's parts; contraction means that the vacuum disappears. The reply denies that these are the only possible explanations: a body may expand by growing larger after being smaller, or contract by shrinking. — Ismail.

[^V04-25]: The second claim can also be disproved by this argument, as the author's closing line indicates: a directional push would conflict with the claim that all parts of a vacuum are alike. — Ismail.

[^V04-26]: This is true when the cause of a whole is directly and fully the cause of it as a whole. Otherwise, a cause need not cause every part of what it produces. A carpenter, for example, makes a chair but does not separately make all its parts. — Ismail.

[^V04-27]: In the usual technical sense, “acting” means affecting something through a gradual process, and “being acted on” means undergoing such a process. The verb forms suggest an action continually renewed. This is why the author says in *The Testimonies* that neither of these categories contains motion. Each is a feature beyond the thing that acts or is acted on, so calling quality a “stable state” excludes them. As for the author’s second objection, there is no problem if a later condition in a definition also excludes what an earlier condition excluded, as the philosophers themselves acknowledge. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-28]: The definition could simply have said “a state which, when understood, does not require us to understand something else.” The familiar version adds the word “stable.” The author’s point is not that an earlier condition in a definition must do work that no later condition can do. Rather, this later condition already rules out the things that “stable” was supposed to exclude, so the added word does no further work. His next question, “What if it was added to exclude time?”, confirms this reading. — The teacher of Rasht.

[^V04-29]: The intended point is that qualities of the soul do not relate to bodies as quantities. If they relate to bodies at all, they do so because bodies have souls, not because of their quantity. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-30]: We can understand the phrase “qualities specific to quantity” to mean qualities that relate to bodies in a way that lets us consider them apart from souls. This kind of relation does not prevent such qualities from existing outside bodies too. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-31]: The kind that is not a readiness for completion, but is itself a completion, consists of qualities of the soul, which divide into temporary states and established traits. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-32]: Imam al-Razi says that if air were always perceptible, people would not doubt that it exists or imagine that the space between heaven and earth is completely empty. — Jalwa.

[^V04-33]: The claim in the first chapter was that color does not exist; this claim is that light does not exist. Keep the distinction in mind. — Jalwa.

[^V04-34]: He means that a lack of light makes the reflected brightness weak, which keeps the color from appearing. Think about it. — Ismail.

[^V04-35]: These words seem to make darkness and light opposites by simple denial and affirmation, rather than by absence and possession, as some other accounts say. Think carefully. — Ismail.

[^V04-36]: The wave is called the nearest cause of sound because it always accompanies sound: where one exists, so does the other, and where one is absent, so is the other. Al-Razi says this connection gives us only a likely explanation, while the question needs certainty. The reply is that examining many individual cases, together with strong insight, can show with certainty that sound is caused by a particular kind of wave in the air. — Ismail.

[^V04-37]: I say that this explanation seems to imply that hearing a sound does not require the air carrying it to reach the eardrum. If a particular wave has already produced sound in the air, and the soul is connected—even indirectly—to the air touching the body, the soul should be able to hear that sound. We have assumed that this connection is enough for the soul to perceive, through the appropriate sense, whatever happens in the air and can be sensed by that faculty. Sound would seem to meet that condition. Unless, that is, we say the soul cannot hear sound until the air carrying it reaches the eardrum. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-38]: The difference between a “station on the path” and a “spiritual station” is like the difference between a state and a settled habit. The Persian verse says: “From the Beloved’s door to the Kaaba of the heart, lovers have a thousand and one stations.” — Ismail.

[^V04-39]: God is not a possible being. Just as He is necessary in Himself, so He is necessary in every respect, and everything proceeds from Him. The word “possible” can also be used in a broader sense: in God, it means necessity in Himself; in a contingent being, it means possibility in itself. Consider this carefully. — Ismail.

[^V04-40]: Will has one reality, but degrees and levels that differ. Each degree and level gives the word “will” a somewhat different meaning. In that sense, all the definitions given for will are right in their own way. This will be explained in the discussion of divine things. — Ismail.

[^V04-41]: Yet awareness of the pleasant thing is still present. If pleasure were simply awareness of what suits us, then pleasure should not cease when the condition has settled. But if pleasure consists in leaving an unnatural condition, that movement ends when the new condition becomes settled; on that account, the pleasure ends with it. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-42]: He treats awareness of what naturally suits a faculty as equivalent to its pleasure. On this basis he reverses the general statement “Every pleasure is awareness of the perfection proper to the faculty that perceives it,” and says, “Every awareness of what is natural to a faculty is pleasure for that faculty.” Do not overlook this step. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-43]: If the mental image is wholly different from separation, it cannot oppose continuity, which is a perfection, and cannot be pain. If it is identical with separation, then it is the object known outside the perceiver, not knowledge, and still cannot be pain. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-44]: At times, a disturbed bodily mixture directly causes pain, and the break in continuity that follows causes pain only indirectly. At other times, the relationship is reversed. A settled mixture causes no pain, directly or indirectly, because it is not felt. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-45]: A thing’s evil may be described as something it has, or as something it lacks if evil is itself an absence. So being evil in itself does not mean that it has no existence. — Isma‘il.

[^V04-46]: The author holds that the sensed form present to the soul is one with the perceiving substance. If that first sensed form ceases, how can it still be present to the soul? One possible answer is that it has ceased in time but remains present in the timeless order. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-47]: I say: a careful thinker will see that greater resistance means less change in the sense organ, and therefore weaker perception and sensation. Pleasure and pain should also be weaker, contrary to the physician’s account. — Isma‘il

[^V04-48]: When this spirit forms in the liver, it is called the natural spirit; when it forms in the heart, the vital spirit; and when it forms in the brain, the spirit of the soul. The natural spirit carries the body’s natural powers, the vital spirit its animal powers, and the psychic spirit its powers of the soul. — Isma‘il

[^V04-49]: Existence is one reality with levels that differ in perfection. What is more complete reflects what is less complete in a fuller way, while what is less complete reflects what is more complete in a more limited way. This is why people say that a cause tells us fully what its effect is, while an effect tells us only part of what its cause is. Think about this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-50]: If the confusion is explained this way—that shape is a form of a thing because it relates to something outside it, namely its boundary, while position is also a form of a thing because it relates to what is outside it—then shape would count as position. The first and second points below would not answer the confusion; only the third would. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-51]: That is, a genuine relation, not merely a relation in the broader, commonly used sense. — Isma‘il

[^V04-52]: Two opposites must take turns in one and the same individual subject, and they cannot be present in it together. For a quality to be stronger or weaker, its subject must be able to pass from one degree to another and remain the same. The subject of this kind of quality cannot remain the same through such a change, so the quality has no greater or lesser degree. Reflect on this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-53]: Two opposites must be positive features that take turns in the same subject and cannot exist together in the same respect. Even and odd do not fit, because oddness is negative. And if oddness were positive, it still would not take turns with evenness in the same individual number. This gives another argument based on the first condition. Consider it carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-54]: Attributes have only two of the four causes of existence: an acting cause and a purpose. They do not have the causes that make up their essence: matter and form, or the general kind and the feature that distinguishes it. The difference between matter and a general kind, and between form and a defining feature, depends on how we consider them. What has no matter has no general kind; what has no form has no defining feature. Attributes have no matter or form in outside reality, so they are simple there, though the mind can analyze them into a general meaning and a specific one. Some say that the mind can do this without there being matter and form in the outside world. On that view, attributes can be composed in thought while remaining simple in reality. The majority's point is that attributes lack outside matter and form, since they clearly have general and specific features. In the standard account, there are nine highest categories of attributes. — Isma‘il

[^V04-55]: An essential feature is clear only when the thing is known fully and in detail. It is not certain that both conditions are met in the case being discussed. Think it over. — Isma‘il

[^V04-56]: The defense says that the definition describes something like a specific kind by means of something like a general kind, and that “relative” is used in both ordinary and technical speech for the defined idea and for its definition. The objection is that although a compound relative includes the simple relation, it is not broader in the way a general kind is broader than a specific one. It includes the simple relation as a whole includes a part. So the circularity remains. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-57]: This works if what defines the concept applies to it only incidentally, or if what is being defined—the particular example—is itself known in some respect. Think it over. — Isma‘il

[^V04-58]: Earlier there was a denial that the later time exists. When the later time comes into being, that earlier denial no longer holds. The mind thus understands a former absence as something that is no longer present. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-59]: He specifies a simple thing because, in a compound, the general kind and the defining feature have distinct existences outside the mind, as matter and form. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-60]: Someone might say that a statement about the outside world requires the subject and its feature to be joined outside the mind, but not that the feature itself exist there. For example, “Zayd is blind.” I answer that the feature must exist there in some way, though its existence may be weak and dependent, not independent. This was explained earlier in the discussion of general matters. — Isma‘il

[^V04-61]: God's presence with things is His sustaining presence, not a presence in time. It is eternal and continuous. The things He sustains are not lacking or passing away in relation to Him, even if they are so in themselves. Understand this carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-62]: Zayd's being Amr's neighbor is not like Amr's being Zayd's father. The two people are enough to identify the fatherhood, but they do not identify their particular neighborly relation: their houses may be next door, or they may be separated by forty houses. To identify that relation, we must specify both houses. — Isma‘il

[^V04-63]: “First,” “second,” and “third” refer to the three meanings just listed: the feature itself, the thing it describes, and the two together. “Category” means one of the ten basic kinds of things, since the thing being described belongs to one of them. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-64]: The relation to a place is not an added feature of the nature of “where”; it belongs to a body because of its “where.” So a body's relation to a place is something added to its existence in itself. This differs from blackness's relation to a body, which is simply how blackness exists. But the relation of “where” to a body is like that of blackness to a body. Understand this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-65]: The relation itself to time, to one of its limits, or to something connected with time is not “when.” Rather, “when” is a state that gives rise to this relation for a thing that exists in time. — Isma‘il

[^V04-66]: Considering connection excludes number; considering something fixed and stable excludes time. The quantities that have position are line, surface, and geometrical body. — Isma‘il

[^V04-67]: That is, at the level of the concept. In reality, God's outpouring of existence is necessary. It is called His mercy to all worlds, His will, His all-embracing mercy, the Real by which things are created, the Breath of the All-Merciful, and other names. — Isma‘il

[^V04-68]: The agent acts on the recipient through its act, but it acts on its act through its own essence, not through another act. The hadith says: “Things were created through will, and will was created through itself.” Understand the point. — Isma‘il

[^V04-69]: In that case, the substance is composed of matter, which is the soul, and an intellectual form. It is not a body; its part is neither a material form nor matter. This shows the flaw in the first division. On the second division there is no flaw: while the substance is connected to the body it is a soul, and afterward it is an intellect. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-70]: If “soul” means animal soul, there is no difference between the second and third cases; if it means rational soul, there is no difference between the second and fourth. One might argue that animal soul is intended, and that the second case considers the existence of an animal and the soul as part of its existence, while the third considers the meaning of body and the soul as part of its meaning. Perhaps the author says “part of what makes it what it is” in the second case and “part of its essence's meaning” in the third to point to this difference. Understand the point. — Isma‘il

[^V04-71]: By “thing” in the phrase “exists in a thing,” the author means something that exists outside the mind. So the definition of accident does not apply to a body being in some place or time in general, or to a human being being in some general end or purpose. These two kinds of presence are excluded by the words “in a thing.” Consider this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-72]: This is questionable in light of what the author says at the end of the Metaphysics. In his account of the world's origination, he establishes that change and renewal in bodies, and their belonging to the category of “when,” are formal and substantial features that constitute them, or constitute what their existence and individuality require, or belong to the level of their existence and individuality. They are not accidents from which a body could in reality be separated or free. A body's relation to being in time would then be like matter's relation to form. Do not overlook this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-73]: So denying motion at an instant is not rest, since rest cannot be predicated of motion: opposites cannot apply to one another. Consider this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-74]: The celestial body's inability to leave its place is because leaving it would require straight-line motion, which is impossible for celestial natures, as established in its proper place. — Isma‘il

[^V04-75]: You know that a feature is something that follows its subject in a way that describes it and is affected by it. This applies to form in relation to matter, not to matter in relation to form. — Isma‘il

[^V04-76]: On this account, it is difficult to say that form inheres in matter, since an inhering thing's existence in itself is identical with its existence for its subject, not with the existence of its subject. Consider this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-77]: The point of this argument is that universal substances are the essence of a particular substance, and the essence of a particular substance is a substance; therefore universal substances are substances. The major premise is explained by analyzing the particular substance into its individual identity and essence. If its substantiality comes from its individual identity, then no other individual would be a substance, which is impossible. So it must come from its essence. The answer is that, if “essence” in the claim “universal substances are the essence of a particular substance” means the unrestricted essence, or natural universal, the minor premise is false: universal substances are concepts understood from individuals, and no understood essence is the unrestricted essence of individuals. If “essence” means the essence as understood, the minor premise is true, but the major premise is false: the essence as understood is not an analytic part of individuals, so their substantiality does not come from it. It comes from the unrestricted essence, which is an analytic part of individuals. If the meaning changes between the two premises, the middle term has not been repeated, and the conclusion does not follow. Consider this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-78]: By primary predication. So when one understands a kind of substance, such as human, what is known is substance—that is, substance is included in its definition. — Jalwah.

[^V04-79]: Essence considered in itself is neither substance nor accident; it is not an instance of either. If it exists in a way that is not in a subject, it is substance; if it exists in a subject, it is accident. I would add that philosophers say all categories are of the order of essences, and that substance and accident are of that order; the author says so too. How, then, can there be an essence that is neither substance nor accident? Also, the essence of substance in the outside world is substance, since the definition of substance truly applies to it. If it enters the mind in its true reality and is then said to be neither substance nor accident in itself, it would have changed from substance into neither substance nor accident. Jalwah.

[^V04-80]: Avicenna says in the Metaphysics of *The Healing*: “The essence of substance is substance in the sense that it exists in reality without being in a subject; this essence is understood from something whose existence in reality is not in a subject. Its existence in the intellect in this way, however, is not part of the definition of substance: the definition does not say that it exists in the intellect without being in a subject. Rather, its definition says that whether or not it exists in the intellect, its existence in reality is not in a subject.” If someone says, “The intellect is also part of reality,” the answer is that “reality” here means a place in which substance, when present, produces its actions and effects. If someone says, “Then you have made the essence of substance sometimes an accident and sometimes a substance, after denying that this is possible,” we reply that we have also denied that the same essence can exist in reality sometimes as an accident needing a subject and sometimes as a substance not needing one. We have not denied that its intelligible concept becomes an accident—that is, exists in the soul, not as a part. The change is not in the essence itself, which would imply that it had changed into its opposite. The change is in the existence, which is distinct from essence. Jalwah.

[^V04-81]: A thing is either existent by its essence, in which case it is necessary; nonexistent by its essence, in which case it is impossible; or neither existent nor nonexistent by its essence, in which case it is possible. Since Zayd is possible, he is neither existent nor nonexistent considered only as human essence. This does not make him nonexistent in that respect, because a possible thing can lack both opposites at a given level, as explained in the discussion of essence. Consider this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-82]: The soul has two aspects: its existence in itself and its existence for another. In the first respect, it falls under substance as a species under its broader kind. In the second, substance is a general feature of it, because in that respect the soul is a form, identical in reality with its difference though distinct in how we consider it. A broader kind is a general feature in relation to a difference; since a form is identical in reality with its difference, broader kind is also a general feature in relation to it. We make this point specifically about the soul because, unlike other forms imprinted in matter, its existence in itself differs from its existence for another: because it is immaterial, it does not cease to exist when the body perishes. Notice this. — Isma‘il, teacher of our divine teacher.

[^V04-83]: The shared meaning of substance, predicated of substances in one sense, is explained by Imam Fakhr al-Din as the third meaning: an essence that, once it exists, has the property of independence from a subject. He then makes this meaning particular to each substance and uses that to deny that the third meaning is shared, whether essentially or accidentally. Is this not a clear contradiction? Unless he means that the concept is shared as a description, while the thing described is particular in each case. But then he would still have to explain how one meaning can be drawn from things that differ precisely as different things—a point already shown to be impossible. Consider this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-84]: That is, the broader kind does not apply to the simple part because the part is identical with the meaning of the broader kind. Do not overlook this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-85]: The right answer to the second objection is to choose its second option: by “species” and “broader kind,” we mean the natural species and natural broader kind. These exist outside the mind through the very existence of their individuals. An individual is more fully present in time than a natural species or broader kind, since it exists in itself while the species and broader kind exist by being predicated of it and united with it. Likewise, a species is more fully realized in the eternal order than a broader kind: the broader kind is wholly indeterminate, while the species is determinate in that order, though indeterminate in time; the individual is not indeterminate. This is what it means to say that individual, species, and broader kind differ in their existence. The author’s reply would apply only if the objection concerned degrees of substantiality. But the objection is explicitly about degrees of existence and of being existent. Consider this carefully. — Isma‘il

[^V04-86]: Universal substances are determinate in the mind but indeterminate in the outside world. Each universal has its own distinct existence in the mind, though it applies to all its external individuals. So it can be pointed to intellectually in view of its mental existence and determination, but not in view of its external indeterminacy. The objector has confused external indeterminacy with mental determination. Consider this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-87]: The fourth argument differs from the third only in being broader and more specific about why a part of a substantial compound is a substance. The third says that it is part of a substance, and a part of a substance is a substance. The fourth says that a part of a substance is not an accident in it, and whatever is not an accident is a substance. Consider this. — Isma‘il

[^V04-88]: A natural compound must have a need between its parts, without a circular dependence. A natural compound cannot be made from an accident and its subject: although the accident needs the subject, the subject does not need the accident. Their combination is therefore only conceptual, not natural. This shows that the confusion came from mixing up a conceptual whole with a natural compound. Do not overlook this. — Isma‘il
